## 6.2  Qualities of a good test {#s6-2}

*Syllabus: Evaluation Systems: Elements and Types of evaluation*

::: {.box .footprint}
[EXAM FOOTPRINT]{.boxlabel}

Asked occasionally; usually a match of test qualities (validity, reliability, objectivity) with definitions or ways of checking them.

:::

### Core {-}

#### The four qualities {-}

A good test has four main qualities: **validity** (वैधता), **reliability** (विश्वसनीयता), **objectivity** (वस्तुनिष्ठता) and **usability** (व्यावहारिकता), also called practicability. Standardised tests also have **norms** (मानक): tables of how a reference group performed, so that a score can be compared with that group. Learn one sentence for each quality.

| Quality | The question it asks | A quick test of the idea |
|---|---|---|
| Validity | Does the test measure what it claims to measure? | A reasoning test that mainly rewards fast reading is not valid. |
| Reliability | Does it give consistent results? | The same students get similar scores if tested again under similar conditions. |
| Objectivity | Is the score free of the examiner's personal judgement? | Two examiners give the same mark to the same answer. |
| Usability | Can it be given, scored and interpreted without undue time, cost or difficulty? | A valid test that costs too much to give is of little use. |

**Validity is the most important quality.** A test that measures the wrong thing is useless for its purpose, however consistent, objective or cheap it may be.

#### Validity and its types {-}

Validity always means: does the test measure what it is meant to measure? Anything that lets something else affect the scores lowers validity. Suppose a mathematics test is written in long, difficult English. Students weak in English lose marks for a reason that has nothing to do with mathematics. The test is now partly a reading test. Its validity falls, even though its scoring key is perfectly clear (objectivity is unaffected).

There are several kinds of evidence of validity. Learn each with its question.

- **Content validity** (विषयवस्तु वैधता): do the items represent the course content and objectives in proper proportion? It is ensured by preparing a **blueprint** (a table that spreads marks over topics and objectives) before the items are written, and judged by checking the items against the syllabus or blueprint.
- **Criterion-related validity**: do the scores agree with some other measure (a criterion) of the same ability? It has two kinds, which differ only in timing.
- **Concurrent validity** (समवर्ती वैधता): the criterion is available now. A new short reading test is given in the same week as a well-established reading test, and the two sets of scores correlate highly.
- **Predictive validity** (भविष्यसूचक वैधता): the criterion lies in the future. Scores on an entrance or aptitude test are later found to forecast the students' grades in the course. Predictive validity is essential for entrance and aptitude tests.
- **Construct validity** (संरचना वैधता): do the scores reflect the theoretical quality (the construct) that the test claims to measure, such as intelligence, creativity or anxiety? A construct cannot be seen directly, so this evidence is built up through correlations with related measures and through factor analysis.
- **Face validity** (आभासी वैधता): does the test look, on the surface, as if it measures what it should, to candidates and lay observers? This is an impression, not validity in the technical sense. It matters because it affects how seriously candidates take the test.

Remember the timing pair with two words: "now" is concurrent, "later" is predictive.

#### Reliability and how it is estimated {-}

Reliability is consistency. A reliable test gives similar results when the same students are tested again under similar conditions, or when the test is scored by another examiner, or when another version of the test is used.

Reliability is usually expressed as a **reliability coefficient** (विश्वसनीयता गुणांक). It is found by correlating two sets of scores, and it runs from 0 (no consistency) to 1 (perfect consistency). The nearer to 1, the more reliable the test. It is not a percentage of marks, a percentile or a grade point.

The methods differ in what two sets of scores are correlated:

| Method | What is done | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| Test-retest (परीक्षण-पुनर्परीक्षण विधि) | The same test is given twice to the same group, with a gap between the two sittings | Coefficient of stability: steadiness of scores over time |
| Parallel forms or equivalent forms (समानांतर प्रारूप विधि) | Two equivalent versions of the test are given to the same group | Coefficient of equivalence; if given after an interval, stability and equivalence together |
| Split-half (अर्ध-विच्छेद विधि) | The test is given once, divided into two halves (usually odd and even items), and the halves are correlated; the Spearman-Brown formula then gives the reliability of the whole test | Internal consistency |
| Kuder-Richardson (rational equivalence) | The test is given once; formulas KR-20 and KR-21 use the item data of a test scored 1 or 0 | Internal consistency |
| Inter-rater or scorer reliability | Two or more examiners independently mark the same scripts | Agreement between scorers |

A sorting rule helps. Two administrations: test-retest (same test twice) and parallel forms (two versions). One administration: split-half and Kuder-Richardson. Scorers: inter-rater.

**Longer tests are more reliable.** A longer test samples the ability more fully, so the chance errors on single items tend to cancel out. Other things being equal, adding good items raises reliability. Very short tests are usually unreliable. The Spearman-Brown formula expresses this link between length and reliability (see Going Further).

#### How reliability and validity are related {-}

Learn this pair of sentences: **a test can be reliable without being valid, but it cannot be valid without being reliable.**

Picture a weighing scale that always reads two kilograms too much. It is consistent (reliable), but it gives the wrong weight (not valid). In the same way, a test may give very consistent scores while it measures memory for trivial details instead of the reasoning it claims to measure. It is reliable but not valid.

The reverse, "valid but not reliable", cannot occur. If a test gives inconsistent scores, they are largely error, and a test made largely of error cannot be measuring its target accurately. So reliability is **necessary but not sufficient** for validity.

#### Objectivity and usability {-}

**Objectivity** means that the score does not depend on who marks the paper. Different examiners, or the same examiner at different times, give the same score to the same answer. Multiple-choice items are highly objective, because the key decides the mark. Essay answers are less objective, because marking needs judgement. Objectivity supports reliability, since scorer bias is one source of inconsistent scores.

**Usability** or practicability covers the practical side: time needed, cost, ease of giving and scoring, clear instructions, and ease of interpreting the results.

::: {.box .example}
[CLASSROOM EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

The Department of History at a university in Haryana is choosing an entrance test for its MA programme. The committee asks four questions. Do the scores of past entrants forecast their first-semester grades (predictive validity)? Does the paper sample all the BA topics in proportion, as the blueprint says (content validity)? Would a candidate get a similar score if she sat a parallel version a week later (reliability)? Can 600 candidates be tested in two hours and the answers machine-scored (usability and objectivity)? A paper with long essays only would fail the last two questions; a paper of 100 well-made multiple-choice items with a careful blueprint would pass all four.

:::

::: {.box .confused}
[OFTEN CONFUSED]{.boxlabel}

- Reliability vs validity: reliability is consistency; validity is measuring the right thing. Reliable but not valid is possible; valid but not reliable is not.
- Objectivity vs reliability: objectivity is about the scorer; reliability is about the consistency of scores more generally. Objectivity is one support of reliability.
- Content vs face validity: content validity is judged systematically against a blueprint; face validity is only the surface impression.
- Concurrent vs predictive validity: both compare the test with a criterion; the criterion is available now for concurrent and later for predictive.
- Construct vs content validity: content validity asks whether the course is sampled; construct validity asks whether the theoretical trait is reflected.
- Test-retest vs parallel forms: one test given twice gives stability; two equivalent versions give equivalence.
- Split-half vs Kuder-Richardson: both use one administration and give internal consistency; split-half correlates two halves and needs the Spearman-Brown correction, while KR uses item-level right/wrong data.
- Standard error of measurement vs standard deviation: SEM is the error band around one person's score; SD is the spread of scores across the group.

:::

### Going Further {-}

*This layer is for Sets 11 to 20 of the booklet. Read it after you have finished Set 10.*

#### Method 1: the Spearman-Brown formula (split-half reliability) {-}

When a test is split into halves, the correlation between the halves is the reliability of a half-length test. The full test is twice as long, so it is more reliable. The formula, published in 1910 by Charles Spearman and William Brown in separate papers in the *British Journal of Psychology*, corrects for this.

Steps:

1\. Find r, the correlation between the two halves.

2\. Full-test reliability = 2r / (1 + r).

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 1. The odd-item and even-item halves of a test correlate at 0.70. Find the reliability of the whole test.

::: working
Full-test reliability = 2 x 0.70 / (1 + 0.70)
:::

::: working
= 1.40 / 1.70
:::

::: working
= 0.82 (to two decimal places)
:::

The general form is for a test made n times as long: reliability = n r / (1 + (n - 1) r).

Example 2. A test has reliability 0.50. It is made three times as long with similar items. Predict the new reliability.

::: working
New reliability = 3 x 0.50 / (1 + 2 x 0.50)
:::

::: working
= 1.50 / 2.00
:::

::: working
= 0.75
:::

:::

#### Method 2: the Kuder-Richardson formulas {-}

G. F. Kuder and M. W. Richardson published these formulas in *Psychometrika* in 1937. They use a single administration of a test whose items are scored 1 (right) or 0 (wrong). The symbols are: k = number of items; p = proportion answering an item correctly; q = 1 - p; s² = variance of the total scores (the square of their standard deviation); M = mean of the total scores.

::: working
KR-20 = [k / (k - 1)] x [1 - (sum of pq over all items) / s²]
:::

::: working
KR-21 = [k / (k - 1)] x [1 - M (k - M) / (k s²)]
:::

KR-21 is a shortcut. It needs only the mean and the variance, and it assumes that all items are of equal difficulty. When items differ in difficulty, KR-21 comes out lower than KR-20. KR-20 is a special case of Cronbach's alpha, the coefficient used when items are not scored simply right or wrong.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 3 (KR-21). A 40-item test has a mean of 24 and a standard deviation of 6, so s² = 36.

::: working
k / (k - 1) = 40 / 39 = 1.026
:::

::: working
M (k - M) / (k s²) = 24 x 16 / (40 x 36) = 384 / 1440 = 0.267
:::

::: working
KR-21 = 1.026 x (1 - 0.267) = 1.026 x 0.733 = 0.75 (to two decimal places)
:::

Example 4 (KR-20). Six students take a 5-item test. Their totals are 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 and 0. The proportions correct on the five items are 5/6, 4/6, 3/6, 2/6 and 1/6.

::: working
Mean of totals = 2.5; variance s² (dividing by 6) = 2.917
:::

::: working
sum of pq = 0.139 + 0.222 + 0.250 + 0.222 + 0.139 = 0.972
:::

::: working
KR-20 = (5/4) x (1 - 0.972 / 2.917) = 1.25 x 0.667 = 0.83
:::

::: working
KR-21 for the same data = (5/4) x (1 - 2.5 x 2.5 / (5 x 2.917)) = 0.71, lower than KR-20 because the items differ in difficulty
:::

:::

#### Method 3: the standard error of measurement {-}

Every obtained score contains error (6.1). The **standard error of measurement** (मापन की मानक त्रुटि), SEM, tells how far a person's obtained scores would spread around her true score if she could be tested again and again. It turns the reliability coefficient into a band of marks.

Steps:

1\. SEM = SD x square root of (1 - r), where SD is the standard deviation of the test scores and r is the reliability coefficient.

2\. Obtained score plus or minus 1 SEM gives a band within which the true score probably lies (about 68 times in 100).

3\. Obtained score plus or minus 2 SEM (strictly 1.96 SEM) gives a band with about 95 per cent confidence.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 5. A test has SD = 10 and reliability 0.91. Ravi obtains 62.

::: working
SEM = 10 x square root of (1 - 0.91) = 10 x square root of 0.09 = 10 x 0.3 = 3
:::

::: working
68 per cent band: 62 - 3 to 62 + 3, that is, 59 to 65
:::

::: working
95 per cent band: 62 plus or minus 1.96 x 3 = 62 plus or minus 5.88, that is, about 56 to 68
:::

The higher the reliability, the smaller the SEM. If r were 1, SEM would be 0: no error at all.

:::

Strictly speaking, the band is built around the obtained score, so it tells us where the true score probably lies, not that it certainly lies there.

#### Method 4: the ceiling on validity {-}

A test cannot correlate with any criterion more highly than it correlates with itself. The square root of the reliability coefficient is called the **index of reliability**, and it is the highest validity coefficient the test can reach.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 6. A test has reliability 0.81. Its validity can be at most the square root of 0.81 = 0.90. With reliability 0.49, the ceiling is 0.70; with 0.36, it is 0.60.

This is a ceiling only. Actual validity coefficients are usually much lower.

:::

::: {.box .slips}
[USUAL SLIPS]{.boxlabel}

- Reporting the half-test correlation as the reliability of the whole test. The full test is always more reliable than its halves (0.60 becomes 0.75).
- Writing 2r / (1 + 2r) or 2r / 2 instead of 2r / (1 + r).
- Forgetting the square root in the SEM: with r = 0.91 the error factor is the square root of 0.09, which is 0.3, not 0.09.
- Taking the reliability itself (0.64) as the ceiling on validity; the ceiling is its square root (0.80).
- Confusing SD (spread across a group) with SEM (error around one person's score).

:::

#### When split-half goes wrong: speed tests {-}

A **power test** has items of increasing difficulty and enough time for everyone to attempt all of them. A **speed test** has easy items and too little time, so the score depends on how many items the student reaches. On a pure speed test a student who reaches 37 items gets about 19 odd items and 18 even items right. The two halves are almost identical for every student, so the odd-even correlation is close to 1 whatever the real reliability. (A computer simulation of 200 such students, run for this guide, gave an odd-even correlation above 0.99.) Split-half therefore gives a spuriously high reliability on speed tests. Test-retest or parallel forms should be used for speeded tests. Split-half is suitable for power tests.

#### Why the group matters: restriction of range {-}

A reliability coefficient is not fixed for a test. It depends on how widely the scores of the group are spread. In classical test theory, reliability is the share of true-score variance in the total variance of scores:

::: working
reliability = 1 - (error variance / total variance)
:::

Suppose the error variance is 9 in every group. In a mixed-ability group the total variance is 90, so reliability = 1 - 9/90 = 0.90. In a group of only top students, the scores bunch together and the total variance is 22.5, so reliability = 1 - 9/22.5 = 0.60. The test has not changed; the group has. This is **restriction of range**. A homogeneous group gives a lower coefficient.

#### Validity threats named by Messick {-}

In a well-known account of validity, Messick (1989) named two main threats to validity:

- **Construct under-representation**: the test is too narrow and leaves out important parts of what it should measure. A research methodology course aims at designing studies, but its examination asks only for definitions.
- **Construct-irrelevant variance**: scores are affected by something outside the construct. A mathematics test in difficult English measures reading ability too; an essay test may reward handwriting.

In short: something essential missing is under-representation; something extra measured is irrelevant variance.

#### The trade-off in performance assessment {-}

Suppose a teacher-education programme replaces a written test on methods of teaching with an observed classroom lesson judged on a rubric. Observing real teaching gives direct evidence of the skill, so validity rises. But one lesson is a small sample, observers differ, and observation takes much time, so reliability and practicability fall. Rubrics, trained observers and several observations reduce the loss. The general rule: performance tasks are stronger on validity and weaker on reliability; objective tests are the reverse.

::: {.box .check}
[CHECK YOURSELF]{.boxlabel}

\(A) 0.80
:::

::: option
\(B) 0.40
:::

::: option
\(C) 0.89
:::

::: option
\(D) 0.64
:::

**3.** Given below are two statements, one labelled Assertion (A) and the other Reason (R):

## 6.3  Types of evaluation {#s6-3}

*Syllabus: Evaluation Systems: Elements and Types of evaluation*

::: {.box .footprint}
[EXAM FOOTPRINT]{.boxlabel}

Asked in most cycles; usually a scenario asking which type of evaluation a teacher is using, or a match of types with purposes.

:::

### Core {-}

Evaluation is classified in two main ways. The first asks **when and why** it is done: before, during or after teaching, and for what purpose. This gives placement, formative, diagnostic and summative evaluation. The second asks **against what** a score is compared: other students, a fixed standard, or the student's own earlier work. This gives norm-referenced, criterion-referenced and ipsative evaluation. Keep the two classifications apart in your mind.

#### By timing and purpose: four types {-}

**Placement evaluation** (स्थापन मूल्यांकन) is done **before** instruction. It finds the learner's entry behaviour: what she already knows and can do, and whether she has the prerequisites for the course. Its results are used to decide where she should begin, for example a beginner, intermediate or advanced group in a language course. A placement pre-test answers two questions: does the learner have the prerequisites, and has the learner already achieved the outcomes of the unit? Learners who have already mastered the unit should skip ahead or be given enrichment or advanced work. Teaching them the same unit again wastes their time.

**Formative evaluation** (रचनात्मक मूल्यांकन) is done **during** instruction, while teaching and learning are still going on. Its purpose is feedback: to tell the learner how she is progressing and the teacher how well the teaching is working, so that both can improve before the course ends. It is often informal: oral questions, class quizzes, short class tests, observation, and comments on drafts returned for revision. It is not meant for final grades.

**Diagnostic evaluation** (निदानात्मक मूल्यांकन) looks for the **causes** of learning difficulties that persist in spite of ordinary teaching and feedback. Formative evaluation shows that a student is weak in, say, subtraction; diagnostic evaluation finds out why, using specially designed tests and close observation. Diagnosis is not an end in itself. It is followed by **remedial teaching** (उपचारात्मक शिक्षण) aimed at exactly the cause found.

**Summative evaluation** (योगात्मक मूल्यांकन) is done at the **end** of a unit, term or course. It sums up how far the objectives have been achieved. Its results are used for grades, promotion and certification. It is usually formal and high-stakes: a final examination, a year-end examination, a degree viva voce.

| Type | When | Purpose | Typical tools or uses |
|---|---|---|---|
| Placement | Before instruction | Find entry behaviour; decide starting point and grouping | Pre-test, entry test |
| Formative | During instruction | Monitor progress; feedback to learner and teacher | Quiz, oral questions, comments on drafts, observation |
| Diagnostic | During instruction, when difficulties persist | Find causes of persistent difficulties; plan remedy | Specially designed diagnostic test, interview |
| Summative | End of unit, term or course | Certify achievement; grade; promote | Term-end or year-end examination, final viva |

Norman E. Gronlund, as Indian B.Ed. texts quote him, put the formative-diagnostic difference in a picture: formative evaluation gives "first aid" for simple learning problems, while diagnostic evaluation searches for the underlying causes of problems that do not respond to first aid.

#### Use decides the label {-}

The same instrument can be formative or summative. What decides the label is how the results are used, not what the test looks like.

- A mid-semester unit test given only to find weak areas for re-teaching is **formative**, even though it looks like an examination.
- A term-end examination used to decide promotion is **summative**.
- A mid-term test returned with detailed comments, whose marks also count for part of the final grade, is **both**: the comments are a formative use and the marks a summative use.

So when a question asks "which distinguishes formative from summative evaluation?", the answer is the use of results to modify instruction still in progress. External setting, whole-course coverage and final grades are typical of summative evaluation but do not define formative evaluation.

#### Do not confuse diagnostic with prognostic {-}

A **diagnostic test** locates the specific difficulties of a learner and their causes now. A **prognostic test** (पूर्वानुमानात्मक परीक्षण) forecasts how well a person is likely to do in a subject or course in the future; "prognosis" means forecast. It is used in guidance and selection. An **aptitude test** measures potential for a particular field, and an **intelligence test** measures general mental ability.

#### By frame of reference: norm, criterion, self {-}

A raw score needs something to be compared with. There are three possible yardsticks.

A **norm-referenced test** (मानक-संदर्भित परीक्षण), NRT, interprets a score by comparing it with the scores of other students in a group. Results are given as ranks, percentiles or standard scores. "Rina scored higher than 85 per cent of her class" is a norm-referenced statement. Competitive examinations for a fixed number of seats work this way: the success of one candidate depends on how others perform.

A **criterion-referenced test** (निकष-संदर्भित परीक्षण), CRT, compares performance with a fixed standard or a defined set of tasks, whatever others do. "Rina solved 17 of the 20 problems", "Rina typed 40 words a minute" and "Rina crossed the pass standard of 50 per cent" are criterion-referenced statements. A driving-licence test is the common example: everyone who meets the standard passes, however many candidates there are.

**Ipsative assessment** (स्व-संदर्भित आकलन), also called self-referenced assessment, compares a learner with her own earlier performance. A teacher who compares each student's present essay with the essay she wrote two months earlier, and grades the improvement, is using ipsative assessment.

| Point | Norm-referenced | Criterion-referenced | Ipsative |
|---|---|---|---|
| Compared with | Other learners | A fixed standard or task domain | The learner's own earlier work |
| Typical report | Rank, percentile rank | Mastery or non-mastery; percentage of objectives mastered | Progress or gain |
| Example | Admission of the top 100 in an entrance test | Driving test; licence for all who reach 70 per cent | Improvement since the last assignment |
| What floats | The cut-off mark floats with rank | The number who pass floats | Nothing is compared with others |

Two consequences are asked often. First, certifying that a learner has mastered minimum competencies needs a criterion-referenced test. A norm-referenced test is built to spread scores out for ranking, and a high rank does not show that the competencies have been mastered. Second, a percentile rank is norm-referenced, while the percentage of objectives mastered is criterion-referenced. The two words sound alike but belong to opposite systems.

Many selection examinations combine both. "Qualified candidates must score at least 40 per cent, and must be among the top 6 per cent of those who do" uses a criterion (the fixed 40 per cent, which any number may reach) and then a norm (the top 6 per cent, which depends on rank).

#### Assessment for, of and as learning {-}

These three phrases are now common. Lorna Earl used all three in her book *Assessment as Learning* (2003).

- **Assessment for learning** (अधिगम के लिए आकलन): the teacher gathers evidence during learning and uses it to adjust teaching. This is formative assessment.
- **Assessment of learning** (अधिगम का आकलन): records and reports what has been achieved at the end. This is summative assessment.
- **Assessment as learning** (अधिगम के रूप में आकलन): the students themselves monitor their work against criteria, judge their own drafts, and set goals. It is self-assessment and self-regulation.

The deciding question between "for" and "as" is: who uses the evidence? If the teacher uses it to adjust teaching, it is for learning. If the students use it to regulate their own learning, it is as learning.

#### Internal and external evaluation {-}

**Internal evaluation** (आंतरिक मूल्यांकन) is carried out by the teachers of the institution itself, often continuously through the term. **External evaluation** (बाह्य मूल्यांकन) is carried out by examiners or an agency from outside, usually at the end. External evaluation is usually summative; internal evaluation can serve formative and summative purposes.

::: {.box .example}
[CLASSROOM EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

In the first week of the BA English (Honours) programme at a college in Hisar, Dr Kaur gives a placement test of grammar. Five students already score above 90 per cent; she gives them an advanced writing project in place of the grammar unit. During the unit she runs a five-minute quiz every Friday (formative). One student, Pooja, keeps writing "he goed" and "she taked" even after feedback. Dr Kaur sets her a short special task with twenty past-tense verbs and finds that Pooja adds "-ed" to every verb, regular or not (diagnostic). She then teaches irregular verbs in groups (remedial). The end-semester examination gives the final grade (summative). The university's rule that a student must get at least 40 per cent to pass is criterion-referenced; the gold medal for the top scorer is norm-referenced.

:::

::: {.box .confused}
[OFTEN CONFUSED]{.boxlabel}

- Formative vs summative: formative improves learning while it is going on; summative certifies achievement at the end. The use of results decides the label, not the instrument.
- Formative vs diagnostic: formative shows that a weakness exists and gives quick feedback; diagnostic finds the cause of a persistent difficulty and leads to remedial teaching.
- Diagnostic vs prognostic: diagnostic finds causes of present errors; prognostic forecasts future performance.
- Placement vs diagnostic: placement looks at entry behaviour before instruction to decide the starting point; diagnosis searches for causes of difficulties that persist.
- Norm-referenced vs criterion-referenced: comparison with other learners vs comparison with a fixed standard. In NRT the cut-off floats; in CRT the number passing floats.
- Percentile rank vs percentage of objectives mastered: norm-referenced vs criterion-referenced.
- Criterion-referenced vs ipsative: a fixed external standard vs the learner's own earlier work.
- Assessment for learning vs assessment as learning: the teacher uses the evidence vs the students use it themselves.

:::

### Going Further {-}

*This layer is for Sets 11 to 20 of the booklet. Read it after you have finished Set 10.*

#### Who introduced the terms {-}

**Michael Scriven** introduced the terms formative and summative evaluation in 1967, in his essay "The methodology of evaluation". He was writing about the evaluation of **curricula and programmes**: formative evaluation improves a curriculum while it is being developed; summative evaluation judges the finished curriculum. **Bloom, Hastings and Madaus**, in their *Handbook on Formative and Summative Evaluation of Student Learning* (1971), applied the terms to **student learning**, especially within mastery learning, where short formative tests are given after each learning unit. So the order is: Scriven, curriculum; Bloom and colleagues, student learning. An option that reverses the order is wrong.

**Robert Glaser** introduced the distinction between norm-referenced and criterion-referenced measurement in a 1963 article in the *American Psychologist* on instructional technology and the measurement of learning outcomes. He argued that new forms of instruction needed tests that showed whether a learner had reached a specified level of performance, not merely her rank. **Ralph W. Tyler** is known for the objectives-based approach to evaluation (6.1). Do not give Tyler or Glaser credit for formative and summative, or Scriven credit for criterion-referenced measurement.

#### How NRT and CRT differ in construction {-}

Because a norm-referenced test exists to rank people, it needs a wide spread of scores. Its items are chosen to be of middle difficulty and to discriminate well between able and weak students. An item that every examinee answers correctly (or that nobody answers correctly) adds nothing to the differences between people and is dropped from an NRT.

A criterion-referenced test exists to show mastery. Its items are chosen to represent the tasks in the defined domain. If every student answers an item correctly, that may simply show that teaching succeeded, and the item is kept. Results are reported as mastery or non-mastery. After effective teaching, CRT scores are not normally distributed; they bunch at the high end (skewed towards high scores). A normal spread is what an NRT aims at, not a CRT.

How is a CRT cut-off fixed? Often by expert judgement of what a minimally competent candidate can do. In the **Angoff method** (Angoff, 1971), each judge estimates, for each item, the probability that a just-competent candidate will answer it correctly; the estimates are summed and averaged across judges to give the cut-off. A rank-based quota works the opposite way: the proportion who succeed is fixed in advance, and the cut-off mark is whatever the last selected candidate scored.

Three further points:

- A criterion-referenced test can be summative. A final mastery test is both.
- Norm-referencing is a way of interpreting scores, not a property of standardised tests alone. A classroom teacher who ranks her class is interpreting her own test in a norm-referenced way.
- The same scores can be given both interpretations. A score of 70 per cent can be read against a pass mark (criterion) or converted to a percentile rank (norm).

#### Purpose and user decide the label, again {-}

A term-end examination decides the students' grades, so for them it is summative. If the department later analyses the item-wise results and uses them to redesign the teaching of the course for the next batch, the same results serve a formative purpose for the course. The examination is summative for these students but formative for the course.

#### The steps of diagnosis, and reading error patterns {-}

Educational diagnosis follows a fixed order:

1\. Identify the students who are having difficulty.

2\. Locate the specific areas of difficulty.

3\. Find the causes of the difficulty.

4\. Plan remedial instruction.

The cause cannot be looked for until the area has been located, and remedy cannot be planned until the cause is known. Remedy without diagnosis only repeats the teaching that has already failed.

A diagnostic test contains **many items on each narrow sub-skill**, so that a pattern of errors can be identified reliably for each sub-skill. Diagnostic tests are generally not used for awarding grades; that is a separate feature, not the reason for having many items.

Read the pattern of errors. Careless errors are irregular: different mistakes in different places. A **systematic** error is the same wrong procedure applied every time, and it shows a misconception. A student who writes 64 - 27 = 43 and 52 - 18 = 46 is subtracting the smaller digit from the larger in each column (7 - 4 = 3, 6 - 2 = 4; 8 - 2 = 6, 5 - 1 = 4), whichever number it belongs to. That is a misconception about regrouping ("borrowing"), not carelessness and not low ability. The remedy is to re-teach place value and regrouping, for example with concrete material such as bundles of ten sticks. Pooja's "goed" and "taked" in the example above are a systematic error of the same kind.

#### Direct and indirect assessment in outcome-based education {-}

In **outcome-based education**, a course states its course outcomes, and the institution checks how far students attain them. Two kinds of tools are used.

- **Direct assessment** (प्रत्यक्ष आकलन) examines actual student performance: tests, assignments, projects, laboratory work, examinations.
- **Indirect assessment** (अप्रत्यक्ष आकलन) gathers opinions about learning: course-exit surveys of students, alumni surveys, employer surveys. A course-exit survey asking students how well they think they achieved the outcomes is indirect, because it records belief, not performance.

Attainment is usually computed mainly from direct tools, with indirect tools as supporting evidence.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Attainment of a course outcome. Target: at least 60 per cent of students should score 50 per cent or more on the questions linked to the outcome. In a class of 90, 63 students do so.

::: working
Attainment = 63 / 90 x 100 = 70 per cent
:::

::: working
70 per cent is above the target of 60 per cent, so the outcome is attained.
:::

Keep three numbers apart: the score level (50 per cent), the target (60 per cent of students) and the result (70 per cent of students). The number of students who reached the level (63) is not itself a percentage.

:::

::: {.box .check}
[CHECK YOURSELF]{.boxlabel}

**1.** Match List I (type of evaluation) with List II (purpose):

| List I | List II |
|---|---|
| A. Placement | I. Locating causes of a persistent difficulty |
| B. Formative | II. Certifying achievement at the end |
| C. Diagnostic | III. Deciding the starting point before instruction |
| D. Summative | IV. Feedback while learning is in progress |

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

::: option
\(A) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
:::

::: option
\(B) A-IV, B-III, C-I, D-II
:::

::: option
\(C) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
:::

::: option
\(D) A-I, B-IV, C-III, D-II
:::

**2.** Given below are two statements:

Statement I: A university that admits the top 50 scorers in its test, whatever their marks, is using the test in a criterion-referenced way.

Statement II: A board that certifies every candidate who scores 60 per cent or more is using its test in a criterion-referenced way.

In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer:

::: option
\(A) Both Statement I and Statement II are true

::: option
\(B) Both Statement I and Statement II are false
:::

::: option
\(C) Statement I is true but Statement II is false
:::

\(D) Statement I is false but Statement II is true

**3.** Given below are two statements, one labelled Assertion (A) and the other Reason (R):

Reason (R): Placement evaluation checks whether learners have already achieved the intended outcomes of the unit.

In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer:

\(A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A
:::

::: option
\(B) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A

\(C) A is true but R is false
:::

::: option
\(D) A is false but R is true
:::

**4.** Consider the following statements:

2\. Assessment as learning means that students monitor and regulate their own learning.

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

\(B) 1, 2 and 3 only
:::

:::

**5.** Arrange the following steps of educational diagnosis in order:

1\. Finding the causes of the difficulty

2\. Planning remedial instruction

3\. Identifying the students who have difficulty

4\. Locating the specific areas of difficulty

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

:::

**8.** For a course outcome, the target is that 60 per cent of students score at least half marks on the linked questions. In a class of 75, 42 students do so. Which is correct?

## 6.4  Tools and techniques of evaluation; item analysis {#s6-4}

*Syllabus: Evaluation Systems: Elements and Types of evaluation*

::: {.box .footprint}
[EXAM FOOTPRINT]{.boxlabel}

Rarely asked directly; it supports single-answer items on objective versus essay items and what item analysis measures.

:::

### Core {-}

#### Techniques and tools {-}

A **technique** of evaluation is a way of collecting evidence: testing, observation, self-report, interview, peer rating. A **tool** is the instrument used in that technique: a test, a checklist, a rating scale, a rubric, an anecdotal record, a cumulative record, a portfolio, a questionnaire. Some tools give **quantitative** evidence (test scores, percentile ranks, standard scores). Others give **qualitative** evidence (anecdotal records, observation notes, portfolios). Evaluation uses both (6.1).

#### Objective-type and essay-type items {-}

An **objective-type item** (वस्तुनिष्ठ प्रश्न) has one predetermined correct answer, so every examiner gives the same mark. Multiple-choice, true-false, matching and completion items are objective. Many can be answered in a short time, so they sample a wide syllabus, and they are quick and objective to score.

An **essay-type item** (निबंधात्मक प्रश्न) asks for a long written answer that the student selects, organises and expresses in her own words. Only an essay shows whether a student can select, organise, integrate and express ideas, so essays can test higher abilities such as analysis and evaluation. Their two weaknesses are **limited sampling** (a paper has only a few questions, so it covers the syllabus narrowly) and **subjective scoring** (marks vary from examiner to examiner).

| Point | Objective-type | Essay-type |
|---|---|---|
| Sampling of content | Wide (many items) | Narrow (few questions) |
| Scoring | Objective, quick | Subjective, slow |
| What it tests best | Knowledge, understanding, application; with care, higher abilities | Organising and expressing ideas; argument |
| Guessing | Possible, especially true-false | Little room |
| Hard part for the teacher | Writing good items | Marking fairly |

So objective tests are superior in sampling, objectivity of scoring and speed of scoring. Essay tests are superior in measuring organisation and expression of ideas. Note the common trap: essay tests do not sample the syllabus more widely; they sample it more narrowly.

Objective items are of two kinds:

- **Supply-type** or recall items: the student writes a word, number or phrase. Completion (fill in the blank) and short-answer items.
- **Selection-type** or recognition items: the student chooses from given answers. True-false, matching and multiple-choice items.

#### The parts of an item {-}

A **multiple-choice item** (बहुविकल्पीय प्रश्न) has three parts. The **stem** poses the problem. The **key** is the correct option. The **distractors** (विकर्षक) are the plausible wrong options, meant to attract those who do not know the answer.

A **matching item** has two columns. The entries of the first column, for which matches are to be found, are the **premises**. The entries of the second column, from which answers are chosen, are the **responses**. Giving more responses than premises reduces guessing by elimination. Stem, key and distractor belong to MCQs; premise and response belong to matching items.

#### Guessing and the limits of each item type {-}

On a true-false item a blind guess has a 50 per cent chance of being right, because there are only two choices. A four-option MCQ reduces this to 25 per cent; a five-option MCQ to 20 per cent. Supply-type items (essay, short answer, completion), where the student must write the answer, leave little room for guessing.

| Item type | Characteristic limitation |
|---|---|
| True-false | A blind guess succeeds half the time |
| Matching | Suits only homogeneous material: sets of similar, related facts |
| Essay | Scoring varies from examiner to examiner |
| Multiple-choice | Writing several plausible distractors for each item is hard |

#### Rules for writing multiple-choice items {-}

- The stem should present a single, clearly stated problem.
- The distractors should be plausible to a student who does not know the answer.
- The key should be placed at random among the positions, with no regular pattern.
- Options should be similar in length, grammar and detail, so the key does not stand out.
- Options such as "all of the above" are best avoided: a candidate who recognises two correct options can choose it without knowing the rest.

#### The blueprint and the steps of test construction {-}

A **blueprint** (नील पत्र), also called a **table of specifications**, is a two-way table prepared before the items are written. One side lists the content units; the other lists the objectives (for example knowledge, understanding, application), and often the forms of question. Each cell shows how many marks go to that unit and objective. The blueprint makes the test sample the course in proper proportion, so it supports content validity (6.2). A marking scheme and a scoring key are prepared later, after the items are written.

The steps of constructing an achievement test run in this order:

1\. Planning: objectives, content and blueprint.

2\. Writing the items.

3\. Trying out the test on a sample of students.

4\. Item analysis of the try-out responses.

5\. Assembling the final form, keeping good items and revising or dropping weak ones.

Item analysis needs response data, so it can never come before the try-out.

#### Observation tools: checklist, rating scale, rubric {-}

- A **checklist** (जाँच सूची) records only whether a feature is present or absent: yes or no.
- A **rating scale** (निर्धारण मापनी) records the degree of each feature on a numerical or verbal scale (1 to 5; poor to excellent).
- A **rubric** goes further. For each criterion it describes what performance at each level looks like. An **analytic rubric** scores each criterion separately; a **holistic rubric** gives one overall judgement.

The test between a rating scale and a rubric is the descriptor. An instrument with criteria and a five-point scale, but no description of what each point means, is a rating scale.

#### Records: anecdotal, cumulative, portfolio {-}

- An **anecdotal record** (उपाख्यानात्मक अभिलेख) is a teacher's brief written description of a single significant incident in a student's behaviour. It is qualitative.
- A **cumulative record** (संचयी अभिलेख) is a comprehensive, year-by-year account of a student kept by the institution: attendance, health, test scores, activities, teachers' remarks. It is used for guidance.
- A **portfolio** is a collection of the student's own selected work, usually with her reflections on it.
- A **sociogram** is a chart of the choices that members of a group make about one another (whom they would like to work with), showing who is popular, isolated, or part of a clique.

#### What item analysis is {-}

**Item analysis** (पद विश्लेषण) is done after a test has been tried out. For each item it finds:

- the **difficulty index** (कठिनाई सूचकांक), also called the **facility value**: the proportion of examinees who answer the item correctly;
- the **discrimination index** (विभेदन सूचकांक): how well the item separates high scorers from low scorers on the whole test;
- how well each **distractor** is working.

Read the facility value carefully. A facility value of 0.90 means 90 per cent answered correctly: the item is **very easy**. A value of 0.10 means a very difficult item. The name "difficulty index" misleads many students: the higher the value, the easier the item. Items with a facility value near 0.50 can discriminate best; very easy and very hard items discriminate poorly, because nearly everyone (or nearly no one) gets them right.

In a norm-referenced test, an item that every examinee answers correctly contributes nothing to the differences between students, so it is dropped. In a criterion-referenced test the same item may be kept, because it may simply show that teaching succeeded (6.3).

::: {.box .example}
[CLASSROOM EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Dr Rao prepares a 50-item objective test in Indian Polity for BA Part I at a college in Rohtak. She first draws a blueprint: five units down the side, knowledge, understanding and application across the top. She writes the items, tries them out on last year's students, and analyses each item. Item 17 has a facility value of 0.95: almost everyone got it right, so she makes it harder. In Item 23 more weak students than strong students chose the key, so she reads it again and finds that the stem is ambiguous. In Item 31 no one chose option (C), so she writes a more plausible distractor. Then she assembles the final test.

:::

::: {.box .confused}
[OFTEN CONFUSED]{.boxlabel}

- Stem, key, distractor vs premise, response: the first three are parts of an MCQ; the last two are the columns of a matching item.
- Supply-type vs selection-type: the student writes the answer (completion, short answer) vs chooses it (true-false, matching, MCQ).
- Blueprint vs marking scheme: the blueprint is planned before items are written and secures content validity; the marking scheme is prepared for the items once written.
- Checklist vs rating scale vs rubric: present or absent; degree on a scale; degree with a description of each level.
- Anecdotal vs cumulative record vs portfolio: one incident; the institution's year-by-year record; the student's own selection of work.
- Facility value vs discrimination index: how many got it right vs whether the able got it right more often than the weak.
- High difficulty index vs difficult item: a high facility value means an easy item.

:::

### Going Further {-}

*This layer is for Sets 11 to 20 of the booklet. Read it after you have finished Set 10.*

#### Method 1: facility value and discrimination index {-}

Steps:

1\. Score all the scripts and arrange them in order of total score.

2\. Take the top 27 per cent as the **upper group** and the bottom 27 per cent as the **lower group**. Set the middle aside. T. L. Kelley (1939) showed that upper and lower groups of 27 per cent give the most efficient comparison. For 200 examinees, each group has 0.27 x 200 = 54 scripts; for 300, each has 81. When a question gives the sizes of the groups, simply use them.

3\. For each item count RU (number right in the upper group) and RL (number right in the lower group). Let n be the number in one group.

4\. Facility value FV = (RU + RL) / (2n), that is, the number right in both groups divided by the total in both groups.

5\. Discrimination index DI = (RU - RL) / n, the difference divided by the size of **one** group. Equivalently, DI = proportion right in the upper group minus proportion right in the lower group.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 1. Upper and lower groups have 30 students each. An item is answered correctly by 27 of the upper group and 15 of the lower group.

::: working
FV = (27 + 15) / (30 + 30) = 42 / 60 = 0.70
:::

::: working
DI = (27 - 15) / 30 = 12 / 30 = 0.40
:::

A fairly easy item that discriminates well. Keep it.

Example 2. Same groups. Upper group 12 right, lower group 18 right.

::: working
FV = (12 + 18) / 60 = 30 / 60 = 0.50
:::

::: working
DI = (12 - 18) / 30 = -6 / 30 = -0.20
:::

A negative index: more low scorers than high scorers got it right. Something is wrong with the item: an ambiguous stem, a wrong key or a misleading option. Revise or drop it.

Example 3. Same groups. Upper group 29 right, lower group 28 right.

::: working
FV = 57 / 60 = 0.95; DI = 1 / 30 = 0.03
:::

Very easy and almost no discrimination. In a norm-referenced test it adds little.

:::

How to read the discrimination index. It runs from -1 to +1. A value near zero means high and low scorers did equally well: the item fails to separate able from weak and needs revision. A negative value is a warning sign. A widely quoted rule of thumb, from the work of Ebel, is: 0.40 and above, very good; 0.30 to 0.39, reasonably good but may be improved; 0.20 to 0.29, marginal, usually needs revision; 0.19 and below, poor, to be revised or rejected.

Why items near 0.50 discriminate best. With equal groups, the largest possible DI for an item is twice the smaller of FV and (1 - FV). Python gives these ceilings:

| Facility value | Highest possible DI |
|---|---|
| 0.10 or 0.90 | 0.20 |
| 0.30 or 0.70 | 0.60 |
| 0.50 | 1.00 |
| 1.00 | 0.00 |

::: {.box .slips}
[USUAL SLIPS]{.boxlabel}

- Dividing RU - RL by both groups together (60) instead of one group (30). In Example 1 that gives 12 / 60 = 0.20 instead of 0.40.
- Giving the discrimination index when the facility value is asked, or the reverse.
- Reading a high difficulty index as a hard item. FV 0.90 is very easy.
- Counting those who got the item wrong: 30 right out of 100 gives FV 0.30, not 0.70.
- Taking 25 per cent when the question says 27 per cent: 27 per cent of 200 is 54, not 50.

:::

#### Method 2: distractor analysis {-}

A good distractor attracts more low scorers than high scorers, because it draws those who do not know the answer. Two warning signs:

- A distractor chosen by **more high scorers than low scorers** may be defensible as a correct answer, or the item may be wrongly keyed. Review the item.
- A distractor chosen by **more examinees than the key** is over-attractive; check it for ambiguity. It is certainly functioning, but perhaps too well.

A distractor that almost nobody chooses is **non-functional**: it does no work. A common rule, following Haladyna and Downing (1993), counts a distractor as non-functional if fewer than 5 per cent of examinees choose it.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 4. Forty students (upper 20, lower 20) answer a four-option item whose key is A.

| Option | Upper group | Lower group | Total | Per cent of 40 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A (key) | 15 | 6 | 21 | 52.5 |
| B | 0 | 1 | 1 | 2.5 |
| C | 1 | 10 | 11 | 27.5 |
| D | 4 | 3 | 7 | 17.5 |

::: working
FV = 21 / 40 = 0.525; DI = (15 - 6) / 20 = 0.45
:::

::: working
5 per cent of 40 = 2 students. B, chosen by 1 student (2.5 per cent), is non-functional; rewrite it.
:::

::: working
C works well: 10 low scorers, 1 high scorer.
:::

::: working
D draws more high scorers (4) than low scorers (3). Check whether D could be defended as correct.
:::

:::

#### Method 3: correction for guessing and the chance score {-}

On objective tests a student may guess. The usual **correction for guessing** (formula scoring) assumes that every wrong answer came from a blind guess:

::: working
Corrected score S = R - W / (n - 1)
:::

Here R = number right, W = number wrong, n = number of options per item. **Omitted items are not counted**: they are neither rewarded nor penalised.

The **chance score** is what a candidate would be expected to get by blind guessing on every item:

::: working
Chance score = number of items x 1 / (number of options)
:::

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 5. Five-option items. A student has 50 right, 20 wrong and leaves 10 blank.

::: working
S = 50 - 20 / (5 - 1) = 50 - 5 = 45
:::

Example 6. True-false items (n = 2). A student has 60 right and 20 wrong.

::: working
S = 60 - 20 / 1 = 40
:::

Example 7. Chance scores. 100 five-option items: 100 x 1/5 = 20. 40 true-false items: 40 x 1/2 = 20. 60 four-option items: 60 x 1/4 = 15.

A score close to the chance score shows no real knowledge.

:::

::: {.box .slips}
[USUAL SLIPS]{.boxlabel}

- Subtracting all the wrong answers (R - W) on a four-option test. The divisor is n - 1 = 3.
- Dividing by n instead of n - 1.
- Counting omitted items as wrong.
- Using the wrong number of options for the chance score: 10 would be the chance score for 50 five-option items, not four-option items (12.5).

:::

#### Two flaws that help the test-wise {-}

When the key is noticeably longer and more carefully qualified than the distractors, test-wise students learn to choose the longest option. The item then rewards test-wiseness instead of knowledge, which lowers its validity, and weak but test-wise students succeed, which lowers its discrimination. The cure is options of similar length, grammar and detail. "All of the above" is a similar giveaway.

#### Interpretive exercises and cloze tests {-}

An **interpretive exercise** gives introductory material (a passage, a table, a graph, a map) followed by several objective items that ask the examinee to interpret, infer and judge. Because the facts are supplied, the items test thinking, not recall. The reading comprehension and data interpretation sets of Paper 1 are interpretive exercises. A **cloze test** also uses a passage, but deletes words at regular intervals; the student fills the gaps. It measures reading comprehension, not a set of interpretive questions.

::: {.box .check}
[CHECK YOURSELF]{.boxlabel}

**1.** On an item, 24 of the 40 students in the upper group and 8 of the 40 in the lower group answer correctly. The facility value and discrimination index are, respectively:

::: option
\(A) 0.40 and 0.20
:::

::: option
\(B) 0.40 and 0.40
:::

::: option
\(C) 0.80 and 0.40
:::

::: option
\(D) 0.32 and 0.16
:::

**2.** Match List I (term) with List II (description):

| List I | List II |
|---|---|
| A. Premise | I. Plausible wrong option of an MCQ |
| B. Distractor | II. Entry in the first column of a matching item |
| C. Stem | III. The correct option |
| D. Key | IV. Part of an MCQ that poses the problem |

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

::: option
\(A) A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-I
:::

::: option
\(B) A-I, B-II, C-IV, D-III
:::

::: option
\(C) A-IV, B-I, C-II, D-III
:::

::: option
\(D) A-II, B-I, C-IV, D-III
:::

**3.** Given below are two statements, one labelled Assertion (A) and the other Reason (R):

Assertion (A): Essay tests are superior to objective tests in measuring the ability to organise and express ideas.

Reason (R): Essay tests sample the content of a course more widely than objective tests.

In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer:

::: option
\(A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A
:::

::: option
\(B) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A
:::

::: option
\(C) A is true but R is false
:::

::: option
\(D) A is false but R is true
:::

**4.** On a test of four-option items, a student has 36 right, 15 wrong and 9 omitted. The score corrected for guessing is:

::: option
\(A) 21
:::

::: option
\(B) 31
:::

::: option
\(C) 33
:::

::: option
\(D) 36
:::

**5.** Arrange the steps of constructing an achievement test in order:

1\. Trying out the test

2\. Preparing the blueprint

3\. Assembling the final form

4\. Writing the items

5\. Item analysis

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

::: option
\(A) 2, 4, 1, 5, 3
:::

**6.** Consider the following statements:

1\. A rating scale without a description of each level is not a rubric.

2\. A cumulative record is a student's own selection of her best work.

3\. An anecdotal record describes a single significant incident.

4\. A checklist records only whether a feature is present or absent.

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

**7.** An item analysis is to be done for 400 examinees using the upper and lower 27 per cent. How many scripts are there in each group?

**8.** Item analysis shows that option (B) of an item, which is not the key, was chosen by 12 high scorers and 3 low scorers. The most likely conclusion is that:

::: option

:::

## 6.5  Reading test scores: a method chapter {#s6-5}

*Syllabus: Evaluation Systems: Elements and Types of evaluation*

::: {.box .footprint}
[EXAM FOOTPRINT]{.boxlabel}

Rarely asked directly; it supports Data Interpretation and Mathematical Reasoning items on averages and spread.

:::

### Core {-}

#### A raw score means little by itself {-}

A **raw score** (अपरिष्कृत प्राप्तांक) is the number of marks a student actually gets. A raw score of 35 tells us almost nothing. Out of 40 or out of 100? Was the paper easy or hard? Did the others score 20 or 38? Was the pass mark 33? A raw score needs a **frame of reference** before it can be interpreted: either a **norm** (how others performed) or a **criterion** (a fixed standard). These are the two frames you met in 6.3. This section teaches the numbers used with the norm frame: averages, spread, percentile ranks, standard scores and correlation.

#### Method 1: mean, median and mode {-}

These are measures of **central tendency** (केंद्रीय प्रवृत्ति): each gives one number that represents the general level of a group.

- The **mean** (माध्य) is the arithmetic average: add all the scores and divide by how many there are.
- The **median** (माध्यिका) is the middle score when the scores are arranged in order. With an even number of scores, it is the average of the two middle scores.
- The **mode** (बहुलक) is the score that occurs most often.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 1. Ten students score: 12, 15, 15, 18, 20, 22, 24, 25, 28, 31.

::: working
Mean = sum / number = 210 / 10 = 21
:::

::: working
Median: the scores are already in order; the two middle ones (5th and 6th) are 20 and 22; median = (20 + 22) / 2 = 21
:::

::: working
Mode = 15 (it occurs twice)
:::

Example 2. Seven students score: 34, 41, 28, 50, 45, 39, 47.

::: working
First arrange in order: 28, 34, 39, 41, 45, 47, 50
:::

::: working
Median = the 4th score = 41
:::

Example 3. Why the median is sometimes better. Seven scores: 40, 42, 45, 47, 48, 50, 95.

::: working
Mean = 367 / 7 = 52.4; median = 47
:::

::: working
One very high score (95) pulls the mean up; the median is not affected. When a few extreme scores are present, the median represents the group better.
:::

:::

#### Method 2: the standard deviation {-}

Two classes can have the same mean and still be very different. One may be made of students who are all about average; another may mix very weak and very strong students. The **standard deviation** (मानक विचलन), SD, measures this spread: roughly, how far scores lie from the mean on average. A larger SD means a more varied group. (The range, the highest score minus the lowest, is a rougher measure of spread.)

Steps:

1\. Find the mean.

2\. Subtract the mean from each score to get its deviation.

3\. Square each deviation.

4\. Add the squares and divide by the number of scores. This is the **variance**.

5\. Take the square root of the variance. This is the SD.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 4. The ten scores of Example 1 (mean 21).

::: working
Deviations: -9, -6, -6, -3, -1, 1, 3, 4, 7, 10
:::

::: working
Squares: 81, 36, 36, 9, 1, 1, 9, 16, 49, 100; sum = 338
:::

::: working
Variance = 338 / 10 = 33.8
:::

::: working
SD = square root of 33.8 = 5.81
:::

Some books divide by (N - 1) instead of N when the group is a sample from a larger population; that gives 338 / 9, and SD = 6.13. Examination questions at this level usually give the SD or use the simple form.

:::

How to read it. Two classes have the same mean of 60 on an entry test. In Class A the SD is 5; in Class B it is 15. Class A is fairly homogeneous: most students are near 60. Class B contains both very weak and very strong students. A lesson pitched at the average will miss many of them, so **Class B needs more differentiated instruction**. Equal means hide unequal spread.

#### Method 3: percentile and percentile rank {-}

A **percentile rank** (शततमक कोटि), PR, is the percentage of the group whose scores fall below a given score. A **percentile** (शततमक) is the reverse: the score below which a given percentage of the group falls. If Rina's percentile rank is 80, then 80 per cent of the group scored below her; her score is the 80th percentile of that group.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 5. In a class of 40, 30 students score below Arjun.

::: working
PR = 30 / 40 x 100 = 75
:::

In a group of 60, 18 score below Meena: PR = 18 / 60 x 100 = 30.

:::

A percentile rank is a **norm-referenced** measure: it tells the position of a student among others. It is not the percentage of marks. Percentage of marks compares a score with the maximum possible; percentile rank compares it with other examinees. On a hard paper a student with 45 per cent marks may stand at the 80th percentile. "PR 80" does not mean that the student answered 80 per cent of the items correctly, nor that exactly 80 students scored lower.

::: {.box .example}
[CLASSROOM EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

In the internal assessment of MA Hindi at a university in Kurukshetra, Section A and Section B both average 18 out of 30. Dr Yadav finds that the SD is 2 in Section A and 6 in Section B. In Section B she forms small groups: some students work on basic reading of the prescribed poems, others on comparative criticism. Section A, much more uniform, she teaches mostly as a whole class. She also tells the students their percentile ranks within the class, and explains that "PR 70" means 70 per cent of the class scored below them, not that they got 70 per cent marks.

:::

::: {.box .confused}
[OFTEN CONFUSED]{.boxlabel}

- Percentile rank vs percentage of marks: position among others vs share of the maximum marks.
- Percentile vs percentile rank: a score vs a percentage of the group below a score.
- Mean vs median: the arithmetic average, pulled by extreme scores, vs the middle score, not affected by them.
- Mean vs standard deviation: general level vs spread.
- Standard deviation vs standard error of measurement: spread across the group vs error around one person's score (6.2).
- Correlation vs causation: a correlation shows that two things go together, not that one causes the other.
- Correlation coefficient vs shared variance: r = 0.60 means shared variance r² = 0.36, not 60 per cent.

:::

### Going Further {-}

*This layer is for Sets 11 to 20 of the booklet. Read it after you have finished Set 10.*

#### Scales of measurement {-}

S. S. Stevens, in a 1946 paper in *Science* ("On the theory of scales of measurement"), described four levels of measurement (मापन के स्तर). Each level has all the properties of the one before it, plus one more.

| Scale | What it allows | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Nominal | Labels categories only; no order | Roll numbers, gender, stream (Arts, Science) |
| Ordinal | Order, but unequal or unknown differences | Ranks: first, second, third in class |
| Interval | Order and equal units, but no true zero | Temperature in degrees Celsius |
| Ratio | Equal units and a true (absolute) zero | Height, weight, time taken |

Ranks are ordinal. The gap between first and second need not equal the gap between second and third, so ranks are not interval.

Test scores are not on a ratio scale. A ratio scale needs a true zero that means complete absence of the quality. A test samples only part of a subject, so a score of zero does not mean zero knowledge, and a score of 80 does not show twice the knowledge of a score of 40. Scores do show order (60 is more than 40); what they lack is an absolute zero.

#### Method 4: standard scores, z and T {-}

A **standard score** (मानक प्राप्तांक) re-expresses a raw score as its distance from the mean in units of the SD. It lets us compare scores from different tests.

::: working
z = (X - mean) / SD
:::

::: working
T = 50 + 10z
:::

A z-score has a mean of 0 and an SD of 1. Scores above the mean have positive z, scores below have negative z. A T-score has a mean of 50 and an SD of 10, which removes the minus signs and most decimals.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 6. A test has mean 60 and SD 8.

::: working
X = 72: z = (72 - 60) / 8 = 12 / 8 = 1.5; T = 50 + 10 x 1.5 = 65
:::

::: working
X = 52: z = (52 - 60) / 8 = -8 / 8 = -1; T = 50 + 10 x (-1) = 40
:::

::: working
X = 60: z = 0; T = 50
:::

Example 7. Which is the better performance? Kiran scores 72 in English (class mean 66, SD 4) and 80 in History (class mean 70, SD 10).

::: working
English: z = (72 - 66) / 4 = 1.5
:::

::: working
History: z = (80 - 70) / 10 = 1.0
:::

::: working
Her raw mark is higher in History, but relative to her classmates she did better in English.
:::

:::

Standard scores are norm-referenced: they say where a student stands in the group.

#### Combining marks from different examiners: scaling {-}

Suppose Examiner A's marks have mean 70 and SD 5, and Examiner B's marks, for a comparable group, have mean 50 and SD 15. If raw marks are simply added, the examiner whose marks vary more decides the order of merit. Converting each set to standard scores first puts both on a common scale. This is the idea behind **scaling** or **moderation**.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 8. Two students, P and Q.

::: working
P: A gives 75, B gives 50. Raw total = 125. z(A) = (75 - 70) / 5 = 1.0; z(B) = (50 - 50) / 15 = 0. Sum of z = 1.0
:::

::: working
Q: A gives 65, B gives 65. Raw total = 130. z(A) = (65 - 70) / 5 = -1.0; z(B) = (65 - 50) / 15 = 1.0. Sum of z = 0
:::

::: working
Raw totals put Q above P. Standard scores put P above Q. The raw ranking was decided by Examiner B's wider spread.
:::

:::

#### The normal curve {-}

Many sets of scores, when there are enough of them, are close to the bell-shaped **normal distribution** (प्रसामान्य वितरण): symmetrical, with mean, median and mode at the centre. The areas under the curve, computed in Python for this guide, are:

| Region | Share of cases |
|---|---|
| Between the mean and 1 SD on one side | 34.13 per cent |
| Within 1 SD on either side of the mean | 68.27 per cent (about 68) |
| Within 2 SD on either side | 95.45 per cent (about 95) |
| Within 3 SD on either side | 99.73 per cent (about 99.7) |
| Below +1 SD (z = 1) | 84.13 per cent |
| Beyond +2 SD on one side | 2.28 per cent |

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 9. Suppose IQ scores in a population are normally distributed with mean 100 and SD 15.

::: working
IQ 85 to 115 is -1 SD to +1 SD: about 68 per cent of people.
:::

::: working
IQ 70 to 130 is -2 SD to +2 SD: about 95 per cent.
:::

::: working
IQ 115 is z = +1, so about 84 per cent of people lie below it, a percentile rank of about 84.
:::

:::

#### Method 5: correlation and shared variance {-}

**Correlation** (सहसंबंध) shows how far two sets of scores go together. The correlation coefficient r runs from -1 to +1. A positive r means high scores on one go with high scores on the other; a negative r means high goes with low; r near 0 means no linear relation. Reliability and criterion validity coefficients (6.2) are correlations.

Steps for the product-moment correlation coefficient:

1\. Find the mean of X and the mean of Y.

2\. Find each deviation from its mean: dx and dy.

3\. Multiply each pair: dx x dy, and add the products.

4\. Square the dx values and add them; square the dy values and add them.

5\. r = (sum of dx dy) / square root of [(sum of dx²) x (sum of dy²)].

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Example 10. Five students: hours in the library per week (X) and marks out of 10 (Y).

| Student | X | Y | dx | dy | dx dy |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 2 | -2 | -2 | 4 |
| 2 | 2 | 4 | -1 | 0 | 0 |
| 3 | 3 | 3 | 0 | -1 | 0 |
| 4 | 4 | 6 | 1 | 2 | 2 |
| 5 | 5 | 5 | 2 | 1 | 2 |

::: working
Mean X = 3, mean Y = 4
:::

::: working
Sum of dx dy = 8; sum of dx² = 10; sum of dy² = 10
:::

::: working
r = 8 / square root of (10 x 10) = 8 / 10 = 0.80
:::

::: working
Shared variance = r² = 0.64, or 64 per cent
:::

:::

Two cautions. First, **correlation is not causation**. A correlation of +0.60 between attendance and marks shows that the two go together, moderately strongly. It does not show that attendance causes the marks: motivated students may both attend more and study more. Second, r is not a percentage. The share of variation in one variable that goes with variation in the other is the **shared variance**, r². For r = 0.60 it is 0.36 (36 per cent); for r = 0.50 it is 0.25; for r = -0.60 it is again 0.36.

::: {.box .slips}
[USUAL SLIPS]{.boxlabel}

- Dividing the raw score by the SD without first subtracting the mean: (65 / 10) is not a z-score.
- Forgetting the minus sign for scores below the mean.
- Writing T = 10 + 50z instead of T = 50 + 10z.
- Reading a percentile rank as a percentage of marks.
- Calling r = 0.60 "60 per cent shared", or "60 per cent caused by". Shared variance is r² = 0.36.
- Taking the 34 per cent (mean to 1 SD on one side) as the share within 1 SD on both sides (68 per cent).
- Not arranging scores in order before finding the median.

:::

::: {.box .check}
[CHECK YOURSELF]{.boxlabel}

**1.** Five students score 4, 7, 7, 9 and 13. The mean, median and mode are, respectively:

::: option
\(A) 7, 7, 8
:::

::: option
\(B) 8, 7, 7
:::

::: option
\(C) 8, 9, 7
:::

::: option
\(D) 7, 8, 7
:::

**2.** A test has mean 50 and SD 4. A student who scores 58 has a z-score and a T-score of, respectively:

::: option
\(A) 2.0 and 70
:::

::: option
\(B) 8.0 and 58
:::

::: option
\(C) 2.0 and 52
:::

::: option
\(D) 14.5 and 195
:::

**3.** Given below are two statements, one labelled Assertion (A) and the other Reason (R):

Assertion (A): A raw score of 42 cannot be interpreted until we know how others performed or what standard was set.

Reason (R): Raw scores must always be converted to T-scores before they are reported.

In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer:

::: option
\(A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A
:::

::: option
\(B) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A
:::

::: option
\(C) A is true but R is false
:::

::: option
\(D) A is false but R is true
:::

**4.** Match List I (example) with List II (scale of measurement):

| List I | List II |
|---|---|
| A. Hostel room numbers | I. Ratio |
| B. Merit list positions | II. Interval |
| C. Temperature in degrees Celsius | III. Nominal |
| D. Time taken to finish a race | IV. Ordinal |

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

::: option
\(A) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
:::

::: option
\(B) A-IV, B-III, C-II, D-I
:::

::: option
\(C) A-III, B-II, C-IV, D-I
:::

::: option
\(D) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
:::

**5.** In a normal distribution, approximately what percentage of cases lies between 2 SD below and 2 SD above the mean?

::: option
\(A) 34 per cent
:::

::: option
\(B) 68 per cent
:::

::: option
\(C) 99.7 per cent
:::

::: option
\(D) 95 per cent
:::

**6.** The correlation between scores on a reading test and a writing test is 0.70. Which statement is correct?

::: option
\(A) Reading ability causes 70 per cent of writing ability.
:::

::: option
\(B) About 49 per cent of the variation in one is shared with the other.
:::

::: option
\(C) 70 per cent of the students are good at both.
:::

::: option
\(D) The two abilities are unrelated.
:::

**7.** Two classes have the same mean score. Class X has an SD of 3 and Class Y an SD of 12. Given below are two statements:

Statement I: Class Y is more heterogeneous than Class X.

Statement II: Because the means are equal, the two classes need the same teaching plan.

In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer:

::: option
\(A) Both Statement I and Statement II are true
:::

::: option
\(B) Both Statement I and Statement II are false
:::

::: option
\(C) Statement I is true but Statement II is false
:::

::: option
\(D) Statement I is false but Statement II is true
:::

**8.** Examiner A's marks have mean 60 and SD 4; Examiner B's marks have mean 60 and SD 16, for comparable groups. If the raw marks of the two examiners are added to make a merit list:

::: option
\(A) both examiners will have equal influence on the order of merit
:::

::: option
\(B) Examiner A will have greater influence because his marks vary less
:::

::: option
\(C) Examiner B will have greater influence because his marks vary more
:::

::: option
\(D) neither examiner will have any influence because the means are equal
:::
