## 6.6  Continuous and Comprehensive Evaluation and grading {#s6-6}

*Syllabus: Evaluation Systems: Elements and Types of evaluation*

::: {.box .footprint}
[EXAM FOOTPRINT]{.boxlabel}

Asked occasionally; usually a single-answer or two-statement item on continuous and comprehensive evaluation or grading.

:::

### Core {-}

#### What CCE means {-}

**Continuous and Comprehensive Evaluation (सतत एवं व्यापक मूल्यांकन)**, usually shortened to CCE, is a school-level scheme of evaluation (मूल्यांकन). Its name has two parts, and the examination often asks what each part means.

- **Continuous** means that assessment (आकलन) goes on regularly through the whole year, during teaching, and not only in one examination at the end. Class work, assignments, quizzes, projects and observation all count. This is formative evaluation (रचनात्मक मूल्यांकन) built into daily teaching, together with periodic summative evaluation (योगात्मक मूल्यांकन).
- **Comprehensive** means that assessment covers the whole child: both the **scholastic (शैक्षिक)** areas and the **co-scholastic (सह-शैक्षिक)** areas.

Scholastic areas are the curricular subjects, such as languages, mathematics, science and social science, and the intellectual abilities developed through them. Co-scholastic areas are everything else that education is meant to develop: life skills (thinking, social and emotional skills), attitudes, values, health and physical education, and literary, artistic and cultural activities such as music, art, sports and games.

Keep the two words apart. 'Continuous' is about time (regular, all through the year). 'Comprehensive' is about coverage (scholastic plus co-scholastic). Neither word is about written versus oral tests, internal versus external examiners, or school versus college. Those are tempting wrong options.

#### Where CCE comes from {-}

The Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act, 2009 (the RTE Act) made this kind of evaluation a duty for elementary schools. Section 29(2)(h) of the Act says that the curriculum and evaluation procedure shall take into consideration "comprehensive and continuous evaluation of child's understanding of knowledge and his or her ability to apply the same". Notice that the Act puts 'comprehensive' first; the scheme is still commonly called CCE.

The Central Board of Secondary Education (CBSE) introduced grading in Classes IX and X from the 2009--10 session, with CCE beginning in Class IX that year and then covering Class X. Under it, marks were replaced by grades, the year was divided into formative and summative assessments, and co-scholastic areas were graded on the report card. CBSE later ended the scheme for Class X and brought back a compulsory board examination from the 2017--18 session.

#### Why grades instead of marks {-}

**Grading (श्रेणीकरण)** means reporting performance in a few broad bands, each named by a letter grade, instead of exact marks. A student does not get 67 or 68; the student gets, say, B2.

Why do this? Because marks look more precise than they are. Every examination has **measurement error (मापन त्रुटि)**: the same answer could get 66 from one examiner and 69 from another, or on another day. A difference of one or two marks between two students therefore tells us almost nothing. Fine marks invite students, parents and schools to treat these meaningless differences as real.

Grades put students into broad bands. This brings three benefits that the examination likes to ask about.

- It reduces over-fine distinctions and misclassification. Two students with 67 and 68 are in the same band, as they should be.
- It reduces unhealthy competition for every single mark.
- It reduces stress and fear of examinations.

Grades do not remove the need for examinations. Some assessment is still needed to decide the grade. Nor do grades raise the pass percentage by themselves.

#### Absolute and relative grading {-}

There are two ways of deciding which student gets which grade. This pair is asked very often.

In **absolute grading (निरपेक्ष श्रेणीकरण)** the grade boundaries are fixed in advance, in marks or per cent. For example, 80 per cent or more is A, 70 to 79 is B, and so on. Every student who crosses a boundary gets that grade, however many such students there are. If the whole class does well, the whole class can get A. The student is judged against a fixed standard. This is the logic of **criterion-referenced (निकष-संदर्भित)** interpretation.

In **relative grading (सापेक्ष श्रेणीकरण)** the grade depends on the student's position in the group. For example, the top 10 per cent get A, the next 20 per cent get B, and so on. The proportions are fixed; the mark needed for each grade floats with the performance of the group. This is the logic of **norm-referenced (मानक-संदर्भित)** interpretation. Relative grading is also called **grading on the curve**, because the proportions are often taken from the normal (bell-shaped) curve.

| Point | Absolute grading | Relative grading |
|---|---|---|
| Fixed in advance | Mark boundaries for each grade | Proportion of students in each grade |
| Grade depends on | The student's own marks against the standard | The student's rank in the group |
| Can everyone get A? | Yes, if everyone reaches the standard | No; only the fixed top proportion |
| Logic | Criterion-referenced | Norm-referenced |
| Example | A for 80 per cent and above | A for the top 10 per cent |

The chief objection to relative grading appears clearly in a small class of uniformly able students. Relative grading must spread the class over the whole range of grades. So small differences in marks are stretched, and some good students must receive low grades however well they have done. Absolute grading avoids this, because it uses a fixed standard. The opposite risk, everyone getting the top grade, belongs to lenient absolute grading, never to relative grading.

In an analogy question, keep the order: relative grading is to absolute grading as norm-referenced interpretation is to criterion-referenced interpretation. The reversed pair is a common trap. Norm-referenced and criterion-referenced tests are taught fully in Section 6.3.

::: {.box .example}
[CLASSROOM EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

At a government college in Sirsa, a B.A. class of 40 takes an internal test in English. Dr Kaur announces in advance: 80 and above is A, 65 to 79 is B, 50 to 64 is C. Twelve students score above 80 and all twelve get A. This is absolute grading. In a parallel section, Dr Yadav decides that the top four students (10 per cent) will get A, whatever their marks. One student with 83 gets B, because four classmates scored higher. This is relative grading, or grading on the curve. The same mark has led to different grades because the rule is different.

:::

::: {.box .confused}
[OFTEN CONFUSED]{.boxlabel}

- Continuous and comprehensive. Continuous = regular, through the year (time). Comprehensive = scholastic plus co-scholastic (coverage).
- Scholastic and co-scholastic. Scholastic = curricular subjects and the intellectual abilities they develop. Co-scholastic = life skills, attitudes, values, health and physical education, cultural activities.
- Absolute and relative grading. Absolute = fixed mark boundaries, anyone can reach any grade (criterion-referenced). Relative = fixed proportions, grade depends on rank (norm-referenced).
- Grading on the curve and criterion-referenced grading. On the curve = proportions fixed in advance, so it is norm-referenced, not criterion-referenced.
- Direct and indirect grading. Direct = grade given straight from qualitative judgement. Indirect = marks first, then converted to grades.

:::

::: {.box .fact}
[FACT WATCH]{.boxlabel}

- RTE Act 2009, section 29(2)(h): evaluation procedure to provide for "comprehensive and continuous evaluation" (as of October 2026).
- CBSE introduced grading in Classes IX and X, with CCE, from 2009--10 (circular of September 2009) and ended the scheme for Class X, restoring a compulsory board examination, from 2017--18. Older books still describe CCE as the current CBSE scheme.
- The National Education Policy 2020 continues the idea in schools through regular, formative, competency-based assessment and a holistic progress card (see Section 6.9).

:::

### Going Further {-}

*This layer is for Sets 11 to 20 of the booklet. Read it after you have finished Set 10.*

#### Direct and indirect grading {-}

Grades can be reached in two ways. In **direct grading (प्रत्यक्ष श्रेणीकरण)** the examiner does not award marks at all. The examiner reads the answer, judges its quality as a whole, and writes the grade straight away. Direct grading is quick and suits qualitative work, but it depends heavily on the examiner's judgement.

In **indirect grading (अप्रत्यक्ष श्रेणीकरण)** marks are awarded first and then converted into grades. The conversion can follow fixed mark ranges (that is absolute grading) or the student's position in the group (that is relative grading). So absolute and relative grading are both forms of indirect grading.

#### The CBSE nine-point scale under CCE {-}

Students sometimes meet the CBSE scale in questions. Under CCE, scholastic areas in Classes IX and X were graded on a nine-point scale: A1 (91--100, grade point 10), A2 (81--90, 9), B1 (71--80, 8), B2 (61--70, 7), C1 (51--60, 6), C2 (41--50, 5), D (33--40, 4), E1 (21--32) and E2 (20 and below). E1 and E2 meant the student needed to improve. CBSE allowed an indicative percentage to be read off by multiplying the grade point average by 9.5.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

A Class X student under CCE had a cumulative grade point average of 8.4. What was the indicative percentage?

::: working
Indicative percentage = grade point average x 9.5
:::

::: working
= 8.4 x 9.5 = 79.8 per cent
:::

:::

This multiplier belongs to CBSE's school scheme only. It is not a rule of the university Choice Based Credit System, which is the subject of Section 6.7.

#### Limits of CCE in practice {-}

Teachers often found CCE heavy in record-keeping. Continuous assessment can turn into continuous testing, and co-scholastic grading can become a formality if teachers lack time and training. These are criticisms of how the scheme was carried out, not of its two principles.

::: {.box .check}
[CHECK YOURSELF]{.boxlabel}

::: option
\(A) assessment of both scholastic and co-scholastic areas
:::

:::

::: option
\(C) assessment by both internal and external examiners
:::

::: option

:::

1\. Life skills

:::

**5.** Match List I with List II.

:::

**6.** A teacher of a small honours class in which all eight students are very able decides to give grades by a fixed distribution. The likely result is that:

::: option
\(A) every student will receive the top grade
:::

::: option

:::

:::

## 6.7  Evaluation in the Choice Based Credit System {#s6-7}

*Syllabus: Evaluation in Choice Based Credit System in Higher education*

::: {.box .footprint}
[EXAM FOOTPRINT]{.boxlabel}

Asked fairly often; usually a single-answer or two-statement item on credits, grade points and the choice-based credit system.

:::

### Core {-}

#### What the Choice Based Credit System is {-}

The **Choice Based Credit System (विकल्प आधारित क्रेडिट प्रणाली)**, or CBCS, is the pattern of courses, credits and grades that the University Grants Commission (UGC) asked Indian universities to adopt through its guidelines of 2015. The abbreviation is often tested: it is Choice Based Credit System, not 'Course Based', 'Curriculum' or 'Scheme'.

The guidelines came from the UGC, the body for university education. Do not confuse it with NCERT (school curriculum), NCTE (teacher education) or NAAC (assessment and accreditation of institutions).

The system has three ideas in its name.

- **Choice.** The student does not take a fixed package. Some courses are compulsory, but the student chooses others from a pool. The UGC describes this as a 'cafeteria' approach: students can take courses of their choice, learn at their own pace, take additional courses and follow an interdisciplinary path.
- **Credit.** Every course carries a stated number of credits, which shows how much study it involves.
- **Grades.** Performance in each course is reported as a letter grade with a grade point, and these are combined into averages for the semester and for the whole programme.

Because every university states its courses in credits on a common pattern, credits earned in one institution can be counted by another. This is **credit transfer (क्रेडिट हस्तांतरण)**, and it allows students to move between institutions. Today this is done through the Academic Bank of Credits, described below. Credit transfer means credits, not marks, fees or teachers, moving from one institution to another.

Under CBCS, performance is not reported in raw marks alone. It is reported through letter grades and grade points attached to credits, summarised as the SGPA for each semester and the CGPA over all semesters completed so far.

#### What a credit is {-}

A **credit (क्रेडिट)** is the unit by which course work is measured. The 2015 guidelines define it by the weekly teaching time: one credit is equivalent to one hour of teaching (lecture or tutorial) or two hours of practical work or field work per week, through a semester.

So a credit tells us the weight of a course. A 4-credit course carries twice the weight of a 2-credit course when averages are calculated. A credit is not a fee, a rank or an attendance figure.

To find the credits of a course, count lecture and tutorial hours one for one, and practical hours two for one.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

A course has 3 hours of lectures and 2 hours of practical work every week through the semester. How many credits does it carry?

::: working
Lecture: 3 hours x 1 credit per hour = 3 credits
:::

::: working
Practical: 2 hours / 2 hours per credit = 1 credit
:::

::: working
Total = 3 + 1 = 4 credits
:::

:::

The newer UGC Curriculum and Credit Framework for Undergraduate Programmes (CCFUP, December 2022) says the same thing per semester rather than per week. In a semester of 15 weeks, one credit of theory means 15 hours of lecture or tutorial, and one credit of practical or field work means 30 hours. The National Credit Framework (NCrF) goes further and counts all 'notional learning hours', including self-study: 30 hours of learning make one credit, and a year of 1,200 hours makes 40 credits. Weekly or per semester, the answer to the worked example is the same: 4 credits.

#### Kinds of courses under CBCS (2015) {-}

The 2015 guidelines place courses in three groups. Many questions are built on this table.

| Course type | What it is |
|---|---|
| Core course (मूल पाठ्यक्रम) | Compulsory for every student of the programme, as a core requirement of the discipline. |
| Elective course (वैकल्पिक पाठ्यक्रम) | Chosen from a pool. Three kinds: <br/> **Discipline Specific Elective (DSE):** offered by the main discipline. <br/> **Generic Elective (GE):** chosen generally from an unrelated discipline, to gain wider exposure. <br/> **Dissertation/project:** a course for special or advanced knowledge that the student studies on his or her own with advisory support from a teacher. |
| Ability Enhancement Course (क्षमता संवर्धन पाठ्यक्रम) | Two kinds: <br/> **Ability Enhancement Compulsory Course (AECC):** Environmental Science, and English or MIL (Modern Indian Language) Communication, taken by all. <br/> **Skill Enhancement Course (SEC) (कौशल संवर्धन पाठ्यक्रम):** value-based or skill-based courses chosen from a pool. |

Three traps follow from this table.

- An elective from an unrelated discipline is a Generic Elective. An elective from one's own discipline is a Discipline Specific Elective.
- The two compulsory ability enhancement courses are Environmental Science and English/MIL Communication. A short skill-based course chosen from a pool, such as 'Technical Writing', is a Skill Enhancement Course, not an AECC. Both are 'enhancement' courses, but only the AECCs are fixed for all students.
- In the 2015 classification a dissertation or project is an elective course carrying credits. Many programmes make a project compulsory in practice, but in the UGC table it sits under electives, not core.

#### Letter grades and grade points {-}

The UGC recommends a ten-point grading system. Learn this table exactly.

| Letter grade | Descriptor | Grade point |
|---|---|---|
| O | Outstanding | 10 |
| A+ | Excellent | 9 |
| A | Very Good | 8 |
| B+ | Good | 7 |
| B | Above Average | 6 |
| C | Average | 5 |
| P | Pass | 4 |
| F | Fail | 0 |
| Ab | Absent | 0 |

Points to fix in memory:

- O stands for Outstanding, not 'Ordinary' or 'Optional'.
- Plain A is Very Good with 8 points. Excellent with 9 points is A+.
- B is Above Average (6) and C is Average (5). The order of words can trick you: 'above average' is higher than 'average'.
- P (Pass) is the lowest passing grade and carries 4 points, not 5.
- F and Ab both carry 0. A student with F has failed and must reappear in the examination.
- There is no D grade on the UGC scale. Some older books and notes print one; do not use it in a UGC question.

The UGC leaves universities free to decide the marks or grade needed to pass a course and the CGPA needed for a degree, taking into account the norms of professional councils. So individual universities may differ on pass marks, but the letters and points above are the UGC scale.

#### SGPA: the method {-}

The **Semester Grade Point Average (सेमेस्टर ग्रेड पॉइंट औसत)**, or SGPA, measures performance in one semester. It is a credit-weighted average.

1\. For each course, multiply its credits by the grade point earned. The result is the course's **credit points**. (Credits are multiplied, not added, divided or subtracted.)

2\. Add the credit points of all the courses of the semester.

3\. Add the credits of all the courses of the semester.

4\. Divide step 2 by step 3. Round to two decimal places, as the UGC guidelines require.

In symbols, SGPA = sum of (Ci x Gi) / sum of Ci, where Ci is the number of credits of the ith course and Gi is the grade point scored in it. The denominator is the total credits, not the number of courses.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

This is the UGC's own illustration. Six courses: Course 1 (3 credits, A), Course 2 (4 credits, B+), Course 3 (3 credits, B), Course 4 (3 credits, O), Course 5 (3 credits, C), Course 6 (4 credits, B).

::: working
Credit points: 3 x 8 = 24; 4 x 7 = 28; 3 x 6 = 18; 3 x 10 = 30; 3 x 5 = 15; 4 x 6 = 24
:::

::: working
Total credit points = 24 + 28 + 18 + 30 + 15 + 24 = 139
:::

::: working
Total credits = 3 + 4 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 4 = 20
:::

::: working
SGPA = 139 / 20 = 6.95
:::

:::

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

A student earns A+ (9) in a 4-credit course, B (6) in a 4-credit course, O (10) in a 3-credit course, P (4) in a 2-credit course and B+ (7) in a 3-credit course.

::: working
Credit points: 4 x 9 = 36; 4 x 6 = 24; 3 x 10 = 30; 2 x 4 = 8; 3 x 7 = 21
:::

::: working
Total credit points = 119; total credits = 16
:::

::: working
SGPA = 119 / 16 = 7.4375, reported as 7.44
:::

::: working
The simple average of the grade points, (9 + 6 + 10 + 4 + 7) / 5 = 7.2, is wrong: it gives the 2-credit course the same weight as the 4-credit ones.
:::

:::

When all courses carry equal credits, the credits cancel out and the SGPA is simply the mean of the grade points. For four equal-credit courses with O, A+, B+ and P, the SGPA is (10 + 9 + 7 + 4) / 4 = 30 / 4 = 7.5.

#### CGPA: the method {-}

The **Cumulative Grade Point Average (संचयी ग्रेड पॉइंट औसत)**, or CGPA, measures performance over all the semesters completed so far. It is calculated in the same way, over all courses of all semesters: total credit points divided by total credits. If you are given each semester's SGPA and credits, first turn each SGPA back into credit points by multiplying by that semester's credits.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

The UGC's illustration of CGPA. Six semesters carry 20, 22, 25, 26, 26 and 25 credits, with SGPAs of 6.9, 7.8, 5.6, 6.0, 6.3 and 8.0.

::: working
Credit points: 20 x 6.9 = 138; 22 x 7.8 = 171.6; 25 x 5.6 = 140; 26 x 6.0 = 156; 26 x 6.3 = 163.8; 25 x 8.0 = 200
:::

::: working
Total credit points = 969.4; total credits = 144
:::

::: working
CGPA = 969.4 / 144 = 6.7319..., reported as 6.73
:::

:::

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Three semesters carry 22, 24 and 20 credits, with SGPAs of 6.8, 7.5 and 8.2.

::: working
Credit points: 22 x 6.8 = 149.6; 24 x 7.5 = 180; 20 x 8.2 = 164
:::

::: working
Total = 493.6 over 66 credits
:::

::: working
CGPA = 493.6 / 66 = 7.4787..., reported as 7.48
:::

::: working
The simple average of the three SGPAs is 7.5, which is wrong because the semesters carry unequal credits.
:::

:::

Both SGPA and CGPA appear on the transcript. SGPA belongs to one semester; CGPA to all semesters taken together, never to one course or only the final semester.

::: {.box .slips}
[USUAL SLIPS]{.boxlabel}

- Dividing by the number of courses instead of the total credits.
- Adding credits and grade points instead of multiplying them.
- Averaging SGPAs (or a CGPA and a new SGPA) without weighting them by credits.
- Using wrong grade points: P is 4 (not 5), A is 8 (not 9), B+ is 7, B is 6.
- Inventing a D grade. The UGC scale has none.
- Leaving a failed course out of the denominator when the question says to apply the UGC formula (see Going Further).

:::

#### Multiple entry and exit, and the Academic Bank of Credits {-}

The National Education Policy 2020 and the UGC's CCFUP (2022) built a four-year undergraduate programme with **multiple entry and exit (बहु-प्रवेश एवं बहु-निकास)**. A student may leave after any year with an award and come back later.

| Leaves after successfully completing | Award |
|---|---|
| One year (two semesters) | Undergraduate certificate |
| Two years (four semesters) | Undergraduate diploma |
| Three years (six semesters) | Bachelor's degree |
| Four years (eight semesters) | Bachelor's degree with honours, or honours with research |

The usual trap is to swap the first two: one year gives a certificate, two years give a diploma.

Credits make this possible. The **Academic Bank of Credits (एकेडमिक बैंक ऑफ क्रेडिट्स)**, or ABC, set up under the UGC (Establishment and Operation of Academic Bank of Credits in Higher Education) Regulations, 2021, is a digital store of credits. Credits earned by a student in recognised institutions are deposited in the student's account, can be transferred, and are later redeemed towards a certificate, diploma or degree. It works like a bank account for credits, which is why it supports multiple entry and exit.

Keep it apart from the **National Academic Depository (राष्ट्रीय शैक्षणिक निक्षेपागार)**, or NAD. The NAD is a digital store of academic awards: degrees, diplomas, certificates and mark-sheets, lodged by institutions, which students and employers can retrieve and verify online through DigiLocker. In one line: ABC stores credits; NAD stores awards.

::: {.box .example}
[CLASSROOM EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Ritu studies B.A. at a college affiliated to a state university in Haryana. In her first semester she takes four core papers of 4 credits each and the compulsory course in Environmental Science of 2 credits; she also chooses a generic elective in Economics. Her transcript shows A in Indian Writing in English (4 credits), B+ in Phonetics (4 credits), and so on, with an SGPA at the foot. At the end of the second year family needs force her to leave. Because her credits are lodged in her ABC account, she receives an undergraduate diploma and can return two years later to complete the degree. Her diploma itself is lodged in the NAD.

:::

::: {.box .confused}
[OFTEN CONFUSED]{.boxlabel}

- SGPA and CGPA. SGPA = one semester. CGPA = all semesters completed so far.
- Credits and credit points. Credits = weight of the course. Credit points = credits x grade point.
- Discipline Specific Elective and Generic Elective. DSE = from the main discipline. GE = from an unrelated discipline.
- AECC and SEC. AECC = Environmental Science and English/MIL Communication, compulsory for all. SEC = skill- or value-based, chosen from a pool.
- A and A+. A = Very Good, 8. A+ = Excellent, 9.
- B and C. B = Above Average, 6. C = Average, 5.
- P and F. P = Pass, 4 (lowest passing grade). F = Fail, 0 (as is Ab).
- Certificate and diploma. Certificate after one year; diploma after two.
- ABC and NAD. ABC stores credits; NAD stores awards (degrees, mark-sheets).
- CBCS 2015 and CCFUP 2022. CBCS: core, DSE, GE, AECC, SEC. CCFUP: major, minor, multidisciplinary, AEC, SEC, VAC, internship.

:::

::: {.box .fact}
[FACT WATCH]{.boxlabel}

- UGC CBCS guidelines (2015): ten-point scale O, A+, A, B+, B, C, P, F, Ab with points 10, 9, 8, 7, 6, 5, 4, 0, 0; SGPA and CGPA rounded to two decimal places (checked October 2026). Some older books print a D grade; it is not on the UGC scale.
- Credit: per week in the 2015 guidelines (one hour of lecture or tutorial, or two hours of practical, per week); per semester in CCFUP, December 2022 (15 hours theory or 30 hours practical per credit) (checked October 2026). NCrF counts 30 notional learning hours per credit.
- Undergraduate course categories: the 2015 CBCS names (core, DSE, GE, AECC, SEC) are still the ones asked in CBCS questions; the CCFUP of December 2022 uses major, minor, multidisciplinary courses, AEC, SEC, value-added courses and internship (as of October 2026).
- Academic Bank of Credits: UGC Regulations, 2021 (as of October 2026).

:::

### Going Further {-}

*This layer is for Sets 11 to 20 of the booklet. Read it after you have finished Set 10.*

#### Failed and absent courses in the SGPA {-}

The UGC formula divides by "the sum of the number of credits of all the courses undergone by a student". Read as written, a course with F or Ab stays in the denominator with its full credits and adds nothing (0 points) to the numerator. So a failure pulls the SGPA down. The UGC's printed illustration does not itself contain an F row, and universities differ in how they record a course that is later cleared, but when a question says to apply the CBCS formula, keep the failed course in.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

A student fails a 4-credit course (F, 0), gets A (8) in a 4-credit course, B+ (7) in a 4-credit course and O (10) in a 2-credit course.

::: working
Credit points: 4 x 0 = 0; 4 x 8 = 32; 4 x 7 = 28; 2 x 10 = 20; total = 80
:::

::: working
Credits of all courses taken = 4 + 4 + 4 + 2 = 14
:::

::: working
SGPA = 80 / 14 = 5.714..., reported as 5.71
:::

::: working
Leaving out the failed course gives 80 / 10 = 8.0, which is wrong under the UGC formula.
:::

:::

When a failed course is later cleared and the new grade replaces F, only the numerator changes. Each grade point gained adds (credits of that course x points gained) to the total.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Six courses of 4 credits each; grade points 9, 7, 7, 6, 8 and 0 (F). The student later clears the failed course with P (4).

::: working
Before: 4 x (9 + 7 + 7 + 6 + 8 + 0) = 148; 148 / 24 = 6.1666..., reported as 6.17
:::

::: working
After: 4 x (9 + 7 + 7 + 6 + 8 + 4) = 164; 164 / 24 = 6.8333..., reported as 6.83
:::

::: working
Rise = 16 extra credit points / 24 credits = 0.666..., about 0.67
:::

::: working
Divide by the total credits of all six courses, not by the number of courses or the credits of one course.
:::

:::

#### Target-grade problems {-}

Examiners like to turn the formula round: what must a student score next to reach a target? The method is the same every time.

1\. Credit points already earned = credits so far x present CGPA.

2\. Credit points needed = total credits after the next semester x target CGPA.

3\. Points still required = step 2 minus step 1.

4\. Required SGPA = step 3 / credits of the next semester.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

A student has a CGPA of 6.4 over 60 credits. The next semester carries 24 credits. What SGPA will raise the CGPA to 6.8?

::: working
Points earned = 60 x 6.4 = 384
:::

::: working
Points needed = (60 + 24) x 6.8 = 84 x 6.8 = 571.2
:::

::: working
Still required = 571.2 - 384 = 187.2
:::

::: working
Required SGPA = 187.2 / 24 = 7.8
:::

::: working
The tempting answer 7.2 (since 6.8 is midway between 6.4 and 7.2) treats the two parts as equal; the earlier 60 credits outweigh the new 24.
:::

:::

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

Five courses of equal credit. Four grades are already known: A (8), B+ (7), B (6) and A+ (9). What grade in the fifth course gives an SGPA of 7.4?

::: working
With equal credits, SGPA = mean of grade points
:::

::: working
Total needed = 5 x 7.4 = 37
:::

::: working
Known total = 8 + 7 + 6 + 9 = 30
:::

::: working
Fifth course needs 37 - 30 = 7 points, which is B+
:::

:::

The same reasoning tells you how far one good semester moves a CGPA. A strong semester added to many earlier credits moves the CGPA only a little, because the earlier credits weigh more.

#### Grade points and scales of measurement {-}

Letter grades only rank performance, so strictly they form an **ordinal scale (क्रमसूचक मापनी)**. Adding and averaging are meaningful only when the steps between values are equal, which is the property of an **interval scale (अंतराल मापनी)**. Calculating an SGPA therefore treats the grade points as if they formed at least an interval scale, with equal steps from P to C to B and so on. This is a convention that the whole system rests on. A nominal scale only names categories, and a ratio scale needs a true zero; neither describes what averaging assumes. Scales of measurement are treated in Section 6.5.

#### From CBCS 2015 to CCFUP 2022 {-}

The CCFUP of December 2022 keeps credits, grades, SGPA and CGPA but renames and reshapes the course types for the four-year programme.

| CCFUP 2022 category | What it covers |
|---|---|
| Major | Courses in the main discipline, studied in depth (the place of the old core and DSE) |
| Minor | Courses in another discipline or a vocational area |
| Multidisciplinary courses (MDC) | Introductory courses in broad areas outside the major |
| Ability Enhancement Courses (AEC) | Language and communication courses |
| Skill Enhancement Courses (SEC) | Practical and job-related skills |
| Value-Added Courses (VAC) | Courses such as environmental education, understanding India, and health and wellness |
| Internship and research project | Summer internship; a research project or dissertation in the fourth year |

So Environmental Science, which was an AECC in 2015, falls among the value-added courses in 2022, while language communication stays as the AEC. Answer a question by the framework it names. If it says 'CBCS' or 'UGC guidelines of 2015', use core, DSE, GE, AECC and SEC.

#### The National Higher Education Qualifications Framework {-}

The UGC's **National Higher Education Qualifications Framework (राष्ट्रीय उच्चतर शिक्षा अर्हता रूपरेखा)**, or NHEQF, places higher education qualifications on levels 4.5 to 8, each described by learning outcomes. Levels 1 to 4 belong to school education.

| Level | Qualification |
|---|---|
| 4.5 | Undergraduate certificate (one year) |
| 5 | Undergraduate diploma (two years) |
| 5.5 | Bachelor's degree (three years) |
| 6 | Bachelor's degree (honours or honours with research, four years); postgraduate diploma |
| 6.5 | Master's degree (two years after a three-year bachelor's, or one year after a four-year bachelor's) |
| 7 | Master's degree such as M.E. or M.Tech. (two years after a four-year bachelor's) |
| 8 | Doctoral degree |

The doctorate is at level 8, the top of the higher education framework. Not every master's degree is at 6.5: the general master's is at 6.5, and a two-year master's after a four-year bachelor's, such as M.Tech., is at level 7.

::: {.box .check}
[CHECK YOURSELF]{.boxlabel}

**1.** Match List I (letter grade) with List II (grade point) on the UGC ten-point scale.

| List I | List II |
|---|---|
| A. A+ | I. 4 |
| B. B | II. 9 |
| C. P | III. 6 |
| D. B+ | IV. 7 |

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

::: option
\(A) A-II, B-IV, C-I, D-III
:::

::: option
\(B) A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV
:::

::: option
\(C) A-IV, B-III, C-II, D-I
:::

::: option
\(D) A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV
:::

**2.** In a semester a student obtains O in a 4-credit course, B in a 3-credit course, B+ in a 3-credit course and C in a 2-credit course. The SGPA is:

::: option
\(A) 7.00
:::

::: option
\(B) 7.25
:::

::: option
\(C) 7.42
:::

::: option
\(D) 7.67
:::

**3.** Semester I carries 18 credits with an SGPA of 7.5 and Semester II carries 22 credits with an SGPA of 6.5. The CGPA after two semesters is:

::: option
\(A) 6.95
:::

::: option
\(B) 7.00
:::

::: option
\(C) 7.05
:::

::: option
\(D) 6.90
:::

**4.** A student's CGPA is 7.2 over 90 credits. The next semester carries 30 credits. The SGPA needed in that semester to bring the CGPA to 7.5 is:

::: option
\(A) 7.8
:::

::: option
\(B) 8.1
:::

::: option
\(C) 8.4
:::

::: option
\(D) 9.0
:::

**5.** A course has 3 lecture hours, 1 tutorial hour and 2 practical hours every week through the semester. Under the CBCS definition of a credit, it carries:

::: option
\(A) 4 credits
:::

::: option
\(B) 5 credits
:::

::: option
\(C) 6 credits
:::

::: option
\(D) 3 credits
:::

**6.** Given below are two statements:

Statement I: Under the UGC CBCS guidelines of 2015, Environmental Science is an Ability Enhancement Compulsory Course.

Statement II: A Generic Elective is an elective offered by the student's own main discipline.

In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer:

::: option
\(A) Both Statement I and Statement II are true
:::

::: option
\(B) Both Statement I and Statement II are false
:::

::: option
\(C) Statement I is true but Statement II is false
:::

\(D) Statement I is false but Statement II is true
:::

**7.** Assertion (A): Dividing the total credit points of a semester by the number of courses gives a wrong SGPA when courses carry unequal credits.

Reason (R): The SGPA is a credit-weighted average, so its denominator is the total credits.

::: option
\(A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A
:::

::: option
\(B) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A
:::

::: option
\(C) A is true but R is false
:::

::: option
\(D) A is false but R is true
:::

**8.** Which of the following statements are correct?

1\. The Academic Bank of Credits stores credits earned by a student and allows them to be redeemed for an award.

2\. The National Academic Depository stores degrees and mark-sheets in digital form.

3\. A student who leaves a four-year undergraduate programme after one year receives an undergraduate diploma.

4\. In the NHEQF the doctoral degree is placed at level 8.

Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

::: option
\(A) 1, 2 and 3 only
:::

::: option
\(B) 2, 3 and 4 only
:::

::: option
\(C) 1 and 3 only
:::

::: option
\(D) 1, 2 and 4 only

## 6.8  Computer-based testing {#s6-8}

*Syllabus: Computer based testing*

::: {.box .footprint}
[EXAM FOOTPRINT]{.boxlabel}

Rarely asked directly; it supports items on innovations in evaluation and online assessment.

:::

### Core {-}

#### What a computer-based test is {-}

In a **computer-based test (कंप्यूटर आधारित परीक्षण)**, or CBT, the questions appear on a computer screen and the candidate answers by clicking with a mouse or using the keyboard. The test is taken at a test centre on computers set up by the testing body. Responses are stored electronically and scored by the computer. CBT stands for Computer Based Test, not 'Centre Based' or 'Common Board'.

The UGC NET is conducted by the **National Testing Agency (NTA)**, which the Government of India set up in November 2017 as an autonomous testing body registered as a society. The NTA conducted the UGC NET for the first time in December 2018, and it did so as a computer-based test. Before that the examination was conducted by the CBSE with paper and OMR sheets.

#### How the screen works {-}

A CBT screen has features that an OMR (optical mark recognition) answer sheet cannot offer.

- A **question palette** shows the number of every question, coloured to show which are answered, not answered, not visited or marked for review.
- An answer can be changed, cleared or **marked for review** any number of times until the test is submitted. On an OMR sheet a darkened circle cannot be altered.
- An on-screen **timer** shows the time left, and the test closes itself at the end.

The candidate does not choose the difficulty of the paper, cannot see a scoring key, and still needs a centre with a steady power supply.

NTA and similar bodies put **mock tests** online before the examination. A mock test lets candidates practise the interface: moving between questions, marking and changing answers, using the palette and watching the timer. Its value is familiarity, which lowers anxiety and prevents marks being lost through handling errors. It does not show the actual questions, and it does not raise anyone's score by itself.

#### Advantages and limitations {-}

| Advantages | Limitations |
|---|---|
| Responses recorded electronically, so scoring is quick and free from human marking error | Needs test centres, computers, power and network; technical failure can disrupt a session |
| Results declared faster | Candidates differ in familiarity with computers (the digital divide) |
| Questions and options can be shuffled from a question bank, so neighbours do not see the same item in the same place; copying becomes difficult | Identity checks and invigilation are still needed |
| Every action is logged: time spent on each item, answer changes | Long reading on screen can tire some candidates |
| Tests can run in many sessions on many days | Different sessions need different papers, which must then be equated |

In a country like India the chief limitation is the **digital divide (डिजिटल विभाजन)**. A candidate from a rural or poor background who has seldom used a computer may lose marks for reasons that have nothing to do with the subject. That is a threat to fairness. Objective scoring, speed of results and shuffling of items are advantages, not limitations.

Remember also what CBT cannot do. It changes how items are delivered and scored, not what they measure. A poorly written item stays poor on a screen, so the validity (वैधता) of the items must still be established by the test makers.

#### Normalisation across shifts {-}

A large CBT is held in several shifts, often over several days, with a different question paper in each shift. The papers cannot be made exactly equal in difficulty. So raw scores from different shifts cannot be compared directly. They are converted by a statistical procedure called **normalisation (सामान्यीकरण)**, usually into **percentile scores (शततमक अंक)**: a candidate's percentile shows the percentage of candidates in the same shift who scored at or below that candidate. (Section 6.5 defines percentile rank as the percentage scoring below a score; both conventions are in use, so read the definition given in the question.)

Normalisation is the answer when a question asks how scores from different shifts are made comparable. Randomisation only shuffles questions within a paper. Negative marking only penalises guessing. Revaluation only rechecks scoring. None of them equates two different papers.

#### Adaptive testing {-}

In a **computerised adaptive test (कंप्यूटरीकृत अनुकूली परीक्षण)**, or CAT, the next question depends on the candidate's answers so far. The program estimates the candidate's ability after each response. A correct answer brings a harder item, a wrong answer an easier one. Each candidate therefore meets items suited to his or her level, and a precise score is reached with fewer items than a fixed test needs.

| Kind of CBT | How the items are chosen |
|---|---|
| Linear fixed-form | Every candidate in a session gets the same set of items, perhaps in shuffled order |
| Linear-on-the-fly (LOFT) | A different form is assembled for each candidate before the test starts, but it does not change during the test |
| Computerised adaptive | Each next item is chosen during the test according to the responses so far |

LOFT and CAT both give candidates different items, so they are easily confused. The deciding point is adaptation during the test: only CAT adapts.

::: {.box .example}
[CLASSROOM EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

A student from a village near Fatehabad prepares for the UGC NET. She has used a smartphone but never a desktop computer. Her teacher at the college makes her take the NTA mock test three times in the computer laboratory. The first time she loses five minutes finding her way between the buttons and the question palette. By the third attempt she moves through the palette with ease. On the day of the examination none of her time goes on the interface. The mock test has not given her any questions in advance; it has removed a handicap that had nothing to do with her subject.

:::

::: {.box .confused}
[OFTEN CONFUSED]{.boxlabel}

- CBT and OMR testing. CBT = answers on screen, changeable until submission. OMR = darkened circles on paper, which cannot be altered.
- Normalisation and randomisation. Normalisation = making scores from different papers comparable. Randomisation = shuffling the order of items or options.
- Adaptive testing and linear-on-the-fly testing. CAT adapts each item to the responses during the test. LOFT builds a different form for each candidate before the test, without adapting.
- Mock test and actual test. Mock = practice with the interface, different questions. Actual = the real paper.
- Item response theory and classical test theory. IRT = item parameters independent of the group tested (needed for CAT). CTT = difficulty and discrimination indices that change with the group.

:::

::: {.box .fact}
[FACT WATCH]{.boxlabel}

- National Testing Agency: set up by the Government of India in November 2017 (as of October 2026).
- First UGC NET conducted by the NTA: December 2018, in computer-based mode. Older books describe the NET as a paper-and-pencil test conducted by the CBSE.
- The mode of the examination is fixed for each cycle by the NTA's information bulletin. It has been computer-based in normal practice since December 2018, but check the mode announced in the bulletin for your own cycle.

:::

### Going Further {-}

*This layer is for Sets 11 to 20 of the booklet. Read it after you have finished Set 10.*

#### Why adaptive tests need item response theory {-}

In an adaptive test, two candidates answer different items. To compare them, the computer must know how difficult and how discriminating each item is on a single scale that does not depend on who happened to take it. **Item response theory (एकांश अनुक्रिया सिद्धांत)**, or IRT, provides such item parameters (difficulty, discrimination and, in some models, guessing). The items of the bank are calibrated in advance on large samples, and the candidate's ability is estimated on the same scale.

Classical test theory, the basis of the difficulty index and discrimination index taught in Section 6.4, gives values that change with the group tested. An item that looks easy in a strong group looks hard in a weak one. Such sample-dependent values cannot place candidates who took different items on one scale. That is why CAT depends on IRT, not on classical statistics alone. Bloom's taxonomy and Flanders' categories have nothing to do with calibration.

#### What percentiles across shifts assume {-}

When percentiles are worked out within each shift, the 90th percentile in Shift 1 is treated as equal to the 90th percentile in Shift 2. That is fair only if the candidates in each shift are of similar ability on average. The testing agency secures this by allotting candidates to shifts at random, from a large pool. The papers of different shifts are not identical; if they were, no normalisation would be needed. The assumption is about the candidates, not the papers.

#### What the log of responses offers {-}

Because a computer records every click, a testing agency can study how long candidates spend on each item and how often they change answers. Very short response times on hard items can signal guessing; patterns of identical answers can signal malpractice. Item statistics from the log also help to improve the question bank. This is a gain in information, not a guarantee of validity.

## 6.9  Innovations in evaluation {#s6-9}

*Syllabus: Innovations in evaluation systems*

::: {.box .footprint}
[EXAM FOOTPRINT]{.boxlabel}

Asked occasionally; usually a multiple-select item on newer assessment practices such as portfolios, rubrics, open-book and peer assessment.

:::

### Core {-}

#### Why innovations are needed {-}

A single written examination at the end of a course tests mainly what a student can recall in three hours. It says little about what the student can do, how the student has grown, or how the student thinks about his or her own work. The innovations in this section try to fill these gaps. Most of them are tools of formative evaluation (रचनात्मक मूल्यांकन); some also change summative examinations.

The National Education Policy 2020 states the direction plainly. In its words (paragraph 4.34), assessment should shift from one that is "summative and primarily tests rote memorization skills" to one that is "more regular and formative, is more competency-based, promotes learning and development", and "tests higher-order skills, such as analysis, critical thinking, and conceptual clarity". It also proposes a holistic, multidimensional progress card. The shift runs from summative rote testing towards regular, formative, competency-based assessment, never the other way.

#### Rubrics {-}

A **rubric (रूब्रिक, मूल्यांकन मानदंड-तालिका)** is a scoring guide. It states the criteria on which a piece of work will be judged and describes what performance looks like at each level, for example excellent, good, fair and poor. A rubric makes marking transparent and consistent, and when it is shared in advance it tells students what is expected. A rubric is not a question bank, a timetable or a marks register.

A rubric has three parts.

- **Criteria**: the aspects of performance to be judged, such as content, organisation, language and referencing.
- **Scale**: the levels of performance, such as 4 (excellent) to 1 (poor).
- **Descriptors**: the wording in each cell that says what that level of that criterion looks like. Descriptors are what make two markers give the same score.

There are two kinds of rubric.

- An **analytic rubric** scores each criterion separately and describes levels for each. It gives a profile of strengths and weaknesses as well as a total. It is slower but diagnostic.
- A **holistic rubric** describes levels of overall quality. The marker chooses the level that best fits the work as a whole and gives a single score. It is quick but gives little diagnostic feedback.

Do not confuse either with a **checklist**, which only records whether a feature is present or absent, or an **anecdotal record**, which is a narrative note of an observed incident. Both are taught in Section 6.4.

#### Portfolios {-}

A **portfolio (पोर्टफोलियो)** is a purposeful collection of a student's work gathered over a period: drafts, assignments, projects, reflections. Because it covers time, it shows growth and process as well as finished products. Two features separate a portfolio from a mere folder of papers: the work is **selected** against stated criteria, and the student adds a **reflection** explaining why each piece was chosen and what it shows. Collecting everything without selection and reflection is only storage.

An **e-portfolio** is a portfolio kept in electronic form: documents, presentations, audio and video, with the student's reflections and the teacher's comments. It can be shared online and updated over years. It is not an e-book, e-journal or e-library.

#### Open-book and on-demand examinations {-}

In an **open-book examination (खुली पुस्तक परीक्षा)** candidates may consult books and notes while answering. Since facts can be looked up, questions of recall lose their value. A good open-book paper asks candidates to apply, interpret, analyse and judge, so that the books serve as tools and not as answer keys. A paper that can be answered by copying passages tests only speed in finding pages. Open-book does not mean choosing one's own date, examiner or questions.

In an **on-demand examination (मांग पर परीक्षा)** the learner takes the examination when he or she feels ready, instead of on a date fixed for everyone. A unique paper is generated for each sitting from a large **question bank (प्रश्न बैंक)** according to a blueprint, so that papers are of equal standard. The system suits open and distance learning; the National Institute of Open Schooling (NIOS) runs an On-Demand Examination. Its defining feature is freedom of timing. Adaptive testing (Section 6.8) also draws on an item bank, but its defining feature is that difficulty changes during the test.

#### Self-assessment and peer assessment {-}

In **self-assessment (स्व-आकलन)** students judge their own work against clear criteria. The main gain is awareness of what they have mastered and what they have not, so that they can plan their next steps. This awareness of one's own learning is **metacognition (अधिसंज्ञान)**. Self-assessment supplements the teacher's feedback (प्रतिपुष्टि); it is not a way of avoiding it, ranking oneself against classmates, or getting marks without study. It is mainly reflection and need not produce a grade at all.

In **peer assessment (सहपाठी आकलन)** students judge one another's work against given criteria, usually with a rubric. It can be confined to comments that help a classmate improve a draft, without any marks. Its main benefit to the student who assesses is a clearer grasp of what good work looks like: applying the criteria to someone else's work makes the standard explicit in one's own mind and improves one's own later work. A lighter marking load is a benefit to the teacher, not to the learner.

Peer marks can be distorted by friendship, rivalry and vague standards. Four remedies improve their reliability (विश्वसनीयता): a clear rubric that gives every marker the same criteria; anonymous marking; several markers per script, averaged; and training with sample scripts. Letting friends mark each other, leaving criteria to each marker or using one peer per script all make matters worse.

#### Performance-based and authentic assessment {-}

In **performance-based assessment (निष्पादन आधारित आकलन)** students show a skill by doing something: an experiment, a presentation, a field report, a moot court argument. It reveals skills that paper-and-pencil tests cannot show. Its weakness is on the scoring side: it takes longer to mark and depends on the judgement of the assessor, which is why rubrics are used. A multiple-choice test is quicker and more objective to score; a performance task is not.

**Authentic assessment (प्रामाणिक आकलन)** goes one step further. The task reproduces real-life or work conditions: a real product, a real audience and often a real deadline, such as a news bulletin for the campus radio, a case analysis for a local firm, or a report for a panchayat. It shows what students can do with what they know.

| Term | Deciding feature |
|---|---|
| Authentic assessment | A task that reproduces real-world conditions |
| Portfolio assessment | Work collected and selected over time, with reflection |
| Ipsative assessment | The learner compared with his or her own earlier performance |
| Objective assessment | Fixed-answer items such as multiple-choice |

#### Remote proctoring {-}

In online examinations **remote proctoring (दूरस्थ निरीक्षण)** lets candidates take a test from their own location while software, a human invigilator, or both, watch them through the webcam, microphone and screen. It verifies identity and flags suspicious behaviour, doing the work of an invigilator in an examination hall. It has nothing to do with sending papers by courier or evaluating scripts at a distant centre.

::: {.box .example}
[CLASSROOM EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

In the M.A. English programme at a state university, Dr Malik replaces one sessional test with a portfolio. Each student keeps drafts of three essays, the comments of two classmates on each draft (given with a four-criterion analytic rubric), and a final one-page reflection on how the writing changed. The students mark each other's drafts anonymously and only with comments. At the end, Dr Malik grades the portfolio with the same rubric, and a colleague second-marks a sample to check consistency. The students learn the criteria by using them, and the final grade rests on selected work and reflection, not on everything a student happened to write.

:::

::: {.box .confused}
[OFTEN CONFUSED]{.boxlabel}

- Analytic and holistic rubric. Analytic = separate score for each criterion, with a profile. Holistic = one overall score.
- Rubric and checklist. Rubric = levels described for each criterion. Checklist = present or absent only.
- Criteria and descriptors. Criteria = aspects judged. Descriptors = what each level of each aspect looks like.
- Portfolio and folder. Portfolio = selected work with reflection. Folder = everything, unselected.
- Open-book and on-demand examination. Open-book = books allowed during the test. On-demand = learner chooses when to take the test.
- On-demand and adaptive testing. On-demand = choice of timing. Adaptive = difficulty changes during the test.
- Self- and peer assessment. Self = judging one's own work. Peer = judging classmates' work.
- Authentic and ipsative assessment. Authentic = real-world task. Ipsative = comparison with one's own earlier performance.
- Positive and negative washback. Positive = a test that encourages desirable learning. Negative = a test that narrows teaching to what is tested.
- Washback and practice effect. Washback = effect of a test on teaching and learning. Practice effect = score gain from having taken a test before.

:::

::: {.box .fact}
[FACT WATCH]{.boxlabel}

- National Education Policy 2020, paragraph 4.34: assessment to shift from summative, rote-memory testing to regular, formative, competency-based assessment testing higher-order skills (as of October 2026).
- UGC document: Evaluation Reforms in Higher Educational Institutions (2019) (as of October 2026).

:::

### Going Further {-}

*This layer is for Sets 11 to 20 of the booklet. Read it after you have finished Set 10.*

#### Kinds of portfolio {-}

| Kind | What it holds |
|---|---|
| Working portfolio | Work in progress, including drafts |
| Showcase (display) portfolio | The pieces the student selects as his or her best |
| Assessment portfolio | Work chosen to show achievement of stated objectives, judged against criteria |

Portfolios are strong in authenticity and in showing growth. Their weakness is in measurement: different assessors can judge the same portfolio very differently, so inter-rater reliability is low. The usual remedies are clear rubrics, training of raters, double marking and moderation meetings. Authenticity is the strength of the portfolio, not its weakness; negative marking and longer time limits have nothing to do with the problem.

#### Weighted rubric scores {-}

When the criteria of an analytic rubric carry different weights, multiply each criterion score by its weight and add.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

A rubric has three criteria weighted 50, 25 and 25 per cent, each scored on a four-point scale. A student gets 2, 4 and 4.

::: working
Weighted score = 0.50 x 2 + 0.25 x 4 + 0.25 x 4 = 1.0 + 1.0 + 1.0 = 3.0 out of 4
:::

::: working
As a percentage: 3.0 / 4 x 100 = 75 per cent
:::

::: working
The unweighted mean, (2 + 4 + 4) / 3 = 3.33, or 83.3 per cent, ignores the heavier first criterion.
:::

:::

#### Fair marks for group work {-}

Giving every member of a group the same mark lets those who did little benefit from the work of others (free riding). A fairer procedure combines a mark for the group product with peer ratings of each member's contribution, made against agreed criteria. One common way is to multiply the group mark by a contribution factor: the member's average peer rating divided by the group's average rating.

::: {.box .worked}
[WORKED EXAMPLE]{.boxlabel}

A group project earns 70. Members' average peer ratings (out of 5) are Aman 4.5, Bela 4.0, Chetan 3.5 and Dev 2.0. The group average is 3.5.

::: working
Aman: 70 x 4.5 / 3.5 = 90
:::

::: working
Bela: 70 x 4.0 / 3.5 = 80
:::

::: working
Chetan: 70 x 3.5 / 3.5 = 70
:::

::: working
Dev: 70 x 2.0 / 3.5 = 40
:::

:::

#### Washback {-}

**Washback (परीक्षा का पश्च प्रभाव)**, also called backwash, is the effect of a test on teaching and learning. It is **negative** when a test narrows the curriculum, for example when a high-stakes multiple-choice test makes teachers drill answering technique and drop extended writing. It is **positive** when the test encourages desirable learning, for example when an examination with open-ended problems makes teachers teach problem-solving. Alderson and Wall examined the idea in their paper 'Does washback exist?' (1993). Messick counted such consequences of testing as part of a full account of validity (consequential validity). Washback is not the practice effect (score gain from retaking a test) or the halo effect (one impression colouring other judgements).

#### Comment-only feedback {-}

In a study published in 1988, the Israeli psychologist Ruth Butler gave school pupils one of three kinds of feedback on their work: comments only, grades only, or grades with comments. Those who received comments only improved most and stayed most interested. Those who received grades with comments did no better than those given grades alone: the grade drew attention away from the advice. Paul Black and Dylan Wiliam, whose 'Inside the Black Box' (1998) made the case for formative assessment, drew on Butler's work, and her findings are widely cited in support of comment-only marking of formative work. Two kinds of information seem better than one, but here they are not.

#### 360-degree feedback {-}

**360-degree feedback** collects judgements about a person from all around: from superiors, colleagues, those served, and the person himself or herself. In higher education it is used mainly to appraise teachers, combining feedback from students, peers and the head of department with the teacher's self-appraisal. Its strength is a rounder picture than any single source gives; its risk is that unguided raters repeat each other's biases, so criteria are needed here too.

#### Question banks {-}

A **question bank** is a large, classified store of items, each tagged by unit, objective, cognitive level and, after try-out, difficulty and discrimination. Papers can then be drawn from it according to a blueprint, so that different papers are comparable. Question banks make on-demand examinations, shuffled computer-based tests and adaptive tests possible. Their risk is exposure: an item seen too often becomes known.

#### Outcome-based assessment and the UGC's reform documents {-}

In **outcome-based education (परिणाम आधारित शिक्षा)** the programme first states its learning outcomes (अधिगम परिणाम): what a graduate should know and be able to do. Teaching and assessment are then aligned to those outcomes, and each question or task is checked against the outcome it is meant to measure. The UGC's Learning Outcomes-based Curriculum Framework (LOCF) and the NHEQF (Section 6.7) both describe qualifications in this way.

The UGC's document Evaluation Reforms in Higher Educational Institutions (2019) applied these ideas to examinations in colleges and universities. It argued for continuous internal assessment alongside the end-semester examination, assessment linked to stated learning outcomes instead of rote memory, and wider use of technology in examinations. Read it as the higher education counterpart of the school-level shift in NEP 2020.

## Answers to "Check yourself": Chapter 6 {-}

#### Section 6.1 {-}

**1.** (C), because only (C) passes a judgement of worth against objectives. (A), (B) and (D) report quantities (items, time, marks), which is measurement.

**2.** (B), because measurement only assigns numbers, assessment gathers and interprets evidence, and evaluation judges worth and includes both.

**3.** (A), because measurement stops at the number, and evaluation combines quantitative and qualitative description with a value judgement.

**4.** (D), because re-teaching is instructional, promotion is administrative, choice of elective is guidance and data for a study is research. (C) wrongly swaps promotion and guidance.

**5.** (A), because both are true and the indirect, sampled nature of measurement is exactly why error is always present.

**6.** (C), because objectives come first, then learning experiences, then tools are applied, then results are interpreted. (D) interprets before collecting evidence.

**7.** (D), because CIPP stands for Context, Input, Process and Product. Implementation is what the Process part studies, but it is not the name of a letter.

#### Section 6.2 {-}

**1.** (C), because 2 x 0.80 / (1 + 0.80) = 1.60 / 1.80 = 0.89. Choosing 0.80 reports the half-test correlation, which is always lower than the full-test reliability.

**2.** (B), because test-retest gives stability, parallel forms give equivalence, Kuder-Richardson gives internal consistency from one sitting, and inter-rater gives agreement between examiners.

**3.** (A), because both are true, and the fact that reliability does not guarantee validity is exactly why a consistent test can still measure the wrong thing.

**4.** (D), because the criterion (first-year grades) comes later. If both measures had been taken at the same time, it would be concurrent validity.

**5.** (B), because SEM = 8 x square root of 0.25 = 8 x 0.5 = 4, and 50 plus or minus 4 gives 46 to 54. (D) uses 8 x 0.75 = 6, which forgets the square root.

**6.** (A), because statement 2 is wrong: on a pure speed test the odd and even halves are nearly identical, so split-half gives a spuriously high estimate.

**7.** (B), because the ceiling is the square root of the reliability, which is 0.70. (C) repeats the reliability itself.

**8.** (C), because the narrow examination leaves out an essential part of the construct, while face validity is only a surface impression; correlation with a later criterion is predictive validity.

#### Section 6.3 {-}

**1.** (C), because placement decides the starting point, formative gives feedback during learning, diagnostic finds causes, and summative certifies at the end. (A) wrongly gives the causes to summative.

**2.** (D), because admitting a fixed number by rank is norm-referenced; certifying all who reach a fixed standard is criterion-referenced.

**3.** (A), because both are true, and it is precisely because placement evaluation checks for outcomes already achieved that it can send some learners ahead.

**4.** (A), because statement 3 is wrong (daily feedback is formative) and statement 4 is wrong (CRT scores are often bunched at the high end after good teaching).

**5.** (D), because diagnosis goes: who has difficulty, where, why, and then remedy. (A) looks for causes before locating the area.

**6.** (B), because Scriven wrote about curriculum and programme evaluation in 1967; Bloom and colleagues later applied the terms to student learning and mastery learning.

**7.** (C), because the student is compared with her own earlier performance, not with others or with a fixed standard.

**8.** (A), because 42 / 75 = 0.56, or 56 per cent of students, which is below the target of 60 per cent. (B) treats the number of students as a percentage.

#### Section 6.4 {-}

**1.** (B), because FV = (24 + 8) / 80 = 0.40 and DI = (24 - 8) / 40 = 16 / 40 = 0.40. (A) divides the difference by 80, both groups together.

**2.** (D), because a premise is in the first column of a matching item, a distractor is a plausible wrong option, the stem poses the problem and the key is the correct option.

**3.** (C), because the Assertion is true but the Reason is false: essay tests have few questions and sample the course narrowly.

**4.** (B), because S = 36 - 15 / 3 = 36 - 5 = 31; the 9 omitted items are not counted. (A) subtracts all 15 wrong answers.

**5.** (A), because planning (blueprint) comes first, then writing, try-out, item analysis and the final form. (C) puts item analysis before the try-out, which gives it no data.

**6.** (C), because statement 2 describes a portfolio; a cumulative record is the institution's year-by-year record of the student.

**7.** (A), because 0.27 x 400 = 108. (D) is both groups together.

**8.** (C), because a working distractor attracts more low scorers than high scorers; when able students prefer it, the item needs review.

#### Section 6.5 {-}

**1.** (B), because the mean is 40 / 5 = 8, the middle score is 7 and 7 occurs twice. (A) puts the mean and mode in the wrong places.

**2.** (A), because z = (58 - 50) / 4 = 2.0 and T = 50 + 10 x 2 = 70. (D) divides the raw score by the SD without subtracting the mean.

**3.** (C), because a raw score needs a norm or a criterion to be meaningful, but there is no rule that it must be turned into a T-score; it may equally be read against a standard.

**4.** (A), because room numbers only label, merit positions show order, Celsius has equal units but no true zero, and time has a true zero.

**5.** (D), because about 95 per cent of cases lie within 2 SD either way. 68 per cent is within 1 SD.

**6.** (B), because shared variance is r² = 0.49. Correlation does not show cause, and r is not a percentage of students.

**7.** (C), because the larger SD shows a more varied class, which needs more differentiated teaching; equal means hide unequal spread.

**8.** (C), because when raw marks are added, the set with the larger spread decides the ranking. Converting both to standard scores first removes this unfairness.

#### Section 6.6 {-}

**1.** (B), because 'continuous' is about time: assessment goes on regularly through the year. Option (A) describes 'comprehensive', the other half of the name.

**2.** (C), because life skills, values and attitudes, and health and physical education are co-scholastic. Mathematics and social science are curricular subjects, so they are scholastic.

**3.** (D), because in absolute grading it is the mark boundaries that are fixed, not the number of students; any number can get the top grade. Statement II correctly describes relative grading.

**4.** (A), because grades are less prone to misclassification precisely because small mark differences are mostly measurement error; R explains A.

**5.** (A), because a fixed mark boundary is absolute grading, a fixed top proportion is relative grading, a grade without marks is direct grading, and marks converted into grades is indirect grading.

**6.** (C), because a fixed distribution must spread even a uniformly able class over the grades, so some good students are pushed down. Everyone getting the top grade is the risk of lenient absolute grading, not of relative grading.

#### Section 6.7 {-}

**1.** (B), because A+ is 9, B (Above Average) is 6, P is 4 and B+ is 7.

**2.** (C), because credit points are 4 x 10 + 3 x 6 + 3 x 7 + 2 x 5 = 40 + 18 + 21 + 10 = 89 over 12 credits, and 89 / 12 = 7.4166..., reported as 7.42. The simple mean of the grade points, 7.00, ignores the credits.

**3.** (A), because 18 x 7.5 + 22 x 6.5 = 135 + 143 = 278 over 40 credits gives 6.95. The simple average 7.00 forgets that Semester II carries more credits.

**4.** (C), because points earned are 90 x 7.2 = 648, points needed are 120 x 7.5 = 900, and the 252 still required over 30 credits give 8.4. The answer 7.8 wrongly treats the two parts as equal.

**5.** (B), because lectures and tutorials count one credit per weekly hour (3 + 1 = 4) and two practical hours make one credit, so 4 + 1 = 5. Counting each practical hour as a credit gives the wrong answer 6.

**6.** (C), because Environmental Science is one of the two AECCs, but a Generic Elective is taken from an unrelated discipline; one offered by the student's own discipline is a Discipline Specific Elective.

**7.** (A), because the SGPA weights each course by its credits, so the correct denominator is total credits; R explains why A is true.

**8.** (D), because statements 1, 2 and 4 are correct. Exit after one year earns a certificate; the diploma comes after two years.

#### Section 6.8 {-}

**1.** (B), because a CBT answer can be changed, cleared or marked for review until submission, while a darkened OMR circle cannot be altered. A CBT candidate cannot choose the difficulty of the paper.

**2.** (D), because unequal familiarity with computers is a limitation of CBT, not something it removes; but the computer does log time spent on each item.

**3.** (C), because normalisation equates shift papers, randomisation shuffles items, adaptive testing chooses each item by earlier responses, and a mock test is practice with the interface.

**4.** (C), because A is true but R is false: in an adaptive test candidates meet different items chosen by their own responses.

**5.** (A), because a linear-on-the-fly test builds a separate form for each candidate in advance but does not adapt during the test; adaptation is the mark of CAT.

**6.** (D), because quick scoring, shuffling and time logs are advantages; CBT still needs test centres.

#### Section 6.9 {-}

**1.** (B), because a holistic rubric gives one overall score, an analytic rubric scores each criterion, a checklist records presence or absence, and an anecdotal record narrates an incident.

**2.** (A), because recall loses value exactly because the facts can be looked up; R explains A.

**3.** (D), because a showcase portfolio holds only the pieces the student selects as best, while low inter-rater agreement is indeed the chief weakness of portfolios.

**4.** (A), because 0.3 x 4 + 0.3 x 3 + 0.4 x 2 = 1.2 + 0.9 + 0.8 = 2.9 out of 4, which is 72.5 per cent. The unweighted mean, 3 out of 4 or 75 per cent, ignores the weights.

**5.** (C), because a common rubric, anonymity and averaging several markers all reduce bias and error; letting each marker choose criteria makes marks less consistent.

**6.** (D), because the test has narrowed teaching to what it tests, which is negative washback. The practice effect is a score gain from taking a test again.

**7.** (B), because freedom to choose the date, with a paper drawn from a bank by blueprint, defines on-demand examination; adaptive testing changes difficulty during the test.

**8.** (A), because comment-only feedback produced the most improvement, and adding a grade cancelled the benefit of the comments.

These tables gather, in one place, the names, orders, full forms, dated facts and formulae taught in the chapters; the last column gives the section to reread.

#### Levels, stages and sequences to learn in order {-}

Each order runs from first to last, or from lowest to highest, as the guide teaches it.

| Sequence | Order | See |
|---|---|---|
| Teaching continuum (least to most use of understanding) | Conditioning, Training, Instruction, Teaching | 1.1 |
| Phases of teaching (Jackson) | Pre-active, Interactive, Post-active (no "reactive" phase) | 1.2 |
| Functions of teaching | Diagnostic, Prescriptive, Evaluative | 1.2 |
| Aims to objectives (broad to narrow) | Aim, Goal, Instructional objective, Learning outcome | 1.3 |
| Bloom's cognitive domain, 1956 (lowest to highest) | Knowledge, Comprehension, Application, Analysis, Synthesis, Evaluation | 1.4 |
| Revised cognitive domain, 2001 (lowest to highest) | Remember, Understand, Apply, Analyse, Evaluate, Create | 1.4 |
| Affective domain, Krathwohl, Bloom and Masia, 1964 (lowest to highest) | Receiving, Responding, Valuing, Organisation, Characterisation by a value or value complex | 1.4 |
| Psychomotor domain, Dave (5 levels) | Imitation, Manipulation, Precision, Articulation, Naturalisation | 1.4 |
| Psychomotor domain, Simpson (7 levels) | Perception, Set, Guided response, Mechanism, Complex overt response, Adaptation, Origination | 1.4 |
| Psychomotor domain, Harrow (6 levels) | Reflex movements, Basic fundamental movements, Perceptual abilities, Physical abilities, Skilled movements, Non-discursive communication | 1.4 |
| Levels of teaching (lowest to highest) | Memory (Herbart), Understanding (Morrison), Reflective (Hunt) | 1.5 |
| Herbart's own four stages | Clearness, Association, System, Method | 1.5 |
| Herbartian five formal steps | Preparation (ending with statement of aim), Presentation, Comparison and association, Generalisation, Application | 1.5 |
| Morrison's mastery formula | Pre-test, teach, test the result, adapt procedure, teach and test again to the point of actual learning | 1.5 |
| Morrison's five steps (E-P-A-O-R) | Exploration, Presentation, Assimilation, Organisation, Recitation | 1.5 |
| Reflective-level teaching | Recognising and defining the problem, Formulating hypotheses, Testing the hypotheses, Drawing conclusions | 1.5 |
| Dewey's five steps of reflective thinking (*How We Think*) | Felt difficulty, Location and definition, Suggestion of a possible solution, Development by reasoning of its bearings, Further observation and experiment | 1.5, 4.4 |
| Micro-teaching cycle | Plan, Teach, Feedback, Re-plan, Re-teach, Re-feedback | 1.8 |
| Indian model of micro-teaching, minutes | Teach 6, Feedback 6, Re-plan 12, Re-teach 6, Re-feedback 6; total 36 (initial planning not counted) | 1.8 |
| Phases of a micro-teaching programme | Knowledge acquisition, Skill acquisition, Transfer | 1.8 |
| Glaser's Basic Teaching Model | Instructional objectives, Entering behaviour, Instructional procedures, Performance assessment | 1.9 |
| Concept attainment model | Presentation of data and identification of the concept, Testing attainment of the concept, Analysis of thinking strategies | 1.9 |
| Gagné's nine events of instruction | Gaining attention, Informing learners of the objective, Stimulating recall of prior learning, Presenting the stimulus, Providing learning guidance, Eliciting performance, Providing feedback, Assessing performance, Enhancing retention and transfer | 1.9, 2.3 |
| Erikson's stages around the college years | 4 Industry versus inferiority (school age), 5 Identity versus role confusion (adolescence), 6 Intimacy versus isolation (young adulthood), 7 Generativity versus stagnation (middle adulthood) | 2.1 |
| Piaget's stages | Sensorimotor (birth to 2), Pre-operational (2 to 7), Concrete operational (7 to 11), Formal operational (11 or 12 onwards) | 2.2 |
| Vygotsky's development of speech | Social speech, Private speech, Inner speech | 2.2 |
| Bruner's modes of representation | Enactive, Iconic, Symbolic | 2.2 |
| Multi-store model of memory (Atkinson and Shiffrin) | Sensory register, Short-term (working) memory, Long-term memory | 2.2 |
| Gagné's eight types of learning (simplest to most complex) | Signal learning, Stimulus-response learning, Chaining, Verbal association, Discrimination learning, Concept learning, Rule (principle) learning, Problem solving | 2.3 |
| Bandura's processes of observational learning | Attention, Retention, Reproduction, Motivation | 2.3 |
| Kolb's experiential learning cycle | Concrete experience, Reflective observation, Abstract conceptualisation, Active experimentation | 2.4 |
| SQ3R (Robinson) | Survey, Question, Read, Recite, Review | 2.4 |
| Zimmerman's self-regulation cycle | Forethought, Performance, Self-reflection | 2.4 |
| Kohlberg's moral reasoning | Pre-conventional (1 punishment and obedience; 2 self-interest), Conventional (3 approval of others; 4 law and order), Post-conventional (5 social contract; 6 universal ethical principles) | 2.5 |

#### Dates and official facts {-}

These are the positions printed in the guide, checked in October 2026. Facts that can change are listed again, with where to check them, in "Facts to Recheck before Your Examination".

| Fact | Position as given in the guide | See |
|---|---|---|
| Bloom's taxonomy in current use | Revised taxonomy of Anderson and Krathwohl (2001), Create at the top; cognitive handbook 1956, affective handbook 1964 | 1.4 |
| NEP 2020: teachers' professional development | At least 50 hours of continuous professional development a year for every teacher; school principals and school complex leaders 50 hours or more | 1.10 |
| NEP 2020: school teacher qualification | By 2030 the minimum degree qualification for school teaching will be a four-year integrated B.Ed. (a target date) | 1.10 |
| UGC Code of Professional Ethics | The one in the UGC Regulations of 2018; older books may quote an earlier version | 1.10 |
| RPwD Act, 2016 | 21 specified disabilities; benchmark disability not less than 40 per cent; not less than 5 per cent of seats in government and government-aided higher education, with 5 years' upper age relaxation (Section 32); not less than 4 per cent of vacancies in government employment (Section 34); assent December 2016, in force April 2017. The 1995 Act: 7 disabilities and 3 per cent | 2.9 |
| RTE Act, 2009 | Section 29(2)(h): "comprehensive and continuous evaluation"; free and compulsory education for children aged 6 to 14 | 2.9, 6.6 |
| Hawthorne effect | Named after studies of 1924 to 1932 at the Western Electric Company's Hawthorne Works; John Henry effect named by Gary Saretsky in 1972; halo effect named by Edward Thorndike in 1920 | 3.6 |
| IQAC and NAAC | NAAC asks every accredited institution to set up an IQAC, which submits an AQAR each year; NAAC's framework has been under revision, so do not rely on older grading scales | 3.7 |
| SWAYAM launch | 9 July 2017, by the President of India, at the National Convention on Digital Initiatives, with 32 SWAYAM PRABHA channels and the National Academic Depository | 4.6 |
| SWAYAM National Coordinators | Ten (NPTEL, UGC, AICTE, CEC, IGNOU, IIMB, NITTTR, INI, NCERT, NIOS), per the PIB backgrounder of March 2026; older sources list nine, without INI; Banaras Hindu University designated for INI in 2025 | 4.6 |
| SWAYAM PRABHA channels | Began with 32 (2017); later 34, then 40. In 2026 official sources disagree: 48 in the PIB backgrounder of 10 March 2026 and a PIB document of June 2026; 40 on the SWAYAM PRABHA website and in a PIB factsheet of August 2026 | 4.6 |
| SWAYAM PRABHA delivery | 24 hours a day, 7 days a week; GSAT-15; uplinked from BISAG-N, Gandhinagar; free on DD Free Dish; at least four hours of new content daily, repeated five more times | 4.6 |
| SWAYAM credit transfer | Up to 40 per cent of the total courses in a programme in a semester (UGC Regulations, 2021, Gazette 25 March 2021); the 2016 Regulations (19 July 2016) allowed 20 per cent; a Lok Sabha reply of July 2026 still gave 40 per cent | 4.6 |
| SWAYAM four quadrants | e-Tutorial, e-Content, Discussion forum, Assessment; revised SWAYAM Guidelines approved in 2024 (circulated May 2024) | 4.6 |
| SWAYAM certificate fee | Charged for the proctored examination; usually Rs 1,000 per course on NPTEL pages, set course by course | 4.6 |
| SWAYAM Plus | Separate platform for industry-relevant and skill courses, launched February 2024, operated by IIT Madras | 4.6 |
| NPTEL | Started 2003 by seven IITs and IISc Bangalore; National Coordinator for engineering | 4.6 |
