| DIKSHA | Launched 5 September 2017; led by NCERT | 4.6 |
| ShodhShuddhi | Began 2019 with Urkund (later Ouriginal) until 30 September 2023; DrillBit since 1 November 2023 | 4.6 |
| One Nation One Subscription | Approved by the Union Cabinet 25 November 2024; operational from 1 January 2025; INFLIBNET the implementing agency; about 13,000 e-journals of 30 publishers; builds on e-ShodhSindhu (December 2015) | 4.6 |
| NDLI | Pilot from 2015; nationally launched 19 June 2018; developed at IIT Kharagpur | 4.6 |
| Dale's cone editions | 1946: ten bands; 1954: "dramatised experiences" and television; 1969: eleven bands, related to Bruner's modes; the retention percentages are not Dale's (traced by Subramony and others, 2014) | 5.1 |
| Moodle and Creative Commons | Moodle: free, open-source, GNU General Public License version 3; Creative Commons current version 4.0 | 5.4 |
| CBSE and CCE | Grading with CCE in Classes IX and X from 2009--10; board examination for Class X restored from 2017--18 | 6.6 |
| UGC CBCS grading (2015) | O 10, A+ 9, A 8, B+ 7, B 6, C 5, P 4, F 0, Ab 0; no D grade; SGPA and CGPA to two decimal places | 6.7 |
| Definition of a credit | 2015: one hour of lecture or tutorial, or two hours of practical, per week; CCFUP (December 2022): 15 hours theory or 30 hours practical per semester; NCrF: 30 notional learning hours, 1,200 hours a year = 40 credits | 6.7 |
| Course categories | CBCS 2015: core, DSE, GE, AECC, SEC; CCFUP 2022: major, minor, MDC, AEC, SEC, VAC, internship | 6.7 |
| Academic Bank of Credits | UGC Regulations, 2021 | 6.7 |
| NHEQF | Higher education qualifications on levels 4.5 to 8; doctorate at level 8 | 6.7 |
| National Testing Agency | Set up November 2017; first conducted UGC NET in 2018, as a computer-based test; the mode is fixed by the NTA information bulletin | 6.8 |
| NEP 2020, paragraph 4.34 | Assessment to shift from summative, rote-memory testing to regular, formative, competency-based assessment of higher-order skills | 6.9 |
| UGC Evaluation Reforms | Evaluation Reforms in Higher Educational Institutions (2019) | 6.9 |

#### Formulae {-}

Work every formula as the guide's worked examples do, with the steps written out.

| Formula | Meaning of symbols | See |
|---|---|---|
| Higher-order share = (items at higher-order levels / total items) x 100 | Higher-order usually Analyse, Evaluate, Create, unless the question says otherwise | 1.4 |
| One micro-teaching cycle = 6 + 6 + 12 + 6 + 6 = 36 minutes; complete cycles = session minutes / 36 (whole number only) | Teach, feedback, re-plan, re-teach, re-feedback (Indian model) | 1.8 |
| Teacher talk % = (categories 1 to 7) / total x 100; Student talk % = (8 + 9) / total x 100; Silence % = 10 / total x 100 | Flanders tallies; total includes all ten categories | 1.8 |
| I/D ratio = (tallies in 1 to 4) / (tallies in 5 to 7) | Indirect over direct; below 1 means more direct | 1.8 |
| Time observed (minutes) = total tallies x 3 / 60 = total / 20 | One tally every 3 seconds | 1.8 |
| Rule of two-thirds: teacher talk share of class time = 2/3 x 2/3 = 4/9 (about 44 per cent); direct teacher talk = 8/27 (about 30 per cent) | Flanders | 1.8 |
| B = f(P, E) | Behaviour is a function of the person and the psychological environment (Lewin) | 2.3 |
| Motivation = expectancy of success x value of the goal | Expectancy-value theory | 2.6 |
| Motivational force = expectancy x instrumentality x valence | Vroom; multiplied, so one zero makes the force zero | 2.6 |
| IQ = (MA / CA) x 100; MA = IQ x CA / 100; CA = MA x 100 / IQ | MA mental age, CA chronological age, in the same units (months if needed) | 2.8 |
| Deviation IQ = 100 + 15 x z | z = (person's score - mean of age group) / SD of age group | 2.8 |
| Completion rate = completers / base | Base is enrolments or starters; say which | 4.6 |
| Evaluation = measurement + value judgement; fuller form that Indian textbooks attribute to Gronlund: quantitative description + qualitative description + value judgements | Measurement is the quantitative description | 6.1 |
| Obtained score = true score + error | Classical test theory | 6.1 |
| Full-test reliability = 2r / (1 + r) | Spearman-Brown; r = correlation between the two halves | 6.2 |
| Reliability of a test n times as long = n r / (1 + (n - 1) r) | r = present reliability | 6.2 |
| KR-20 = [k / (k - 1)] x [1 - (sum of pq) / s²] | k items; p proportion right on an item; q = 1 - p; s² variance of total scores | 6.2 |
| KR-21 = [k / (k - 1)] x [1 - M (k - M) / (k s²)] | M mean of total scores; assumes equal item difficulty | 6.2 |
| SEM = SD x square root of (1 - r) | r reliability; obtained score plus or minus 1 SEM about 68 per cent, plus or minus 1.96 SEM about 95 per cent | 6.2 |
| Highest possible validity = square root of reliability | The index of reliability | 6.2 |
| Reliability = 1 - (error variance / total variance) | Explains restriction of range | 6.2 |
| Attainment of an outcome = students reaching the level / students in class x 100 | Compare with the target | 6.3 |
| Facility value FV = (RU + RL) / (2n) | RU, RL right in upper and lower groups; n size of one group; groups usually 27 per cent each | 6.4 |
| Discrimination index DI = (RU - RL) / n | Divide by one group only; runs from -1 to +1 | 6.4 |
| Highest possible DI = 2 x the smaller of FV and (1 - FV) | Equal groups | 6.4 |
| Corrected score S = R - W / (n - 1) | R right, W wrong, n options per item; omitted items not counted | 6.4 |
| Chance score = number of items x 1 / number of options | Expected score by blind guessing | 6.4 |
| Mean = sum of scores / number of scores | Median is the middle score after ordering; mode the most frequent | 6.5 |
| Variance = sum of squared deviations from the mean / N; SD = square root of variance | Some books divide by N - 1 for a sample | 6.5 |
| PR = number scoring below / number in group x 100 | Percentile rank as defined in 6.5 (6.8 notes the "at or below" convention used in normalisation) | 6.5, 6.8 |
| z = (X - mean) / SD; T = 50 + 10z | X raw score | 6.5 |
| r = (sum of dx dy) / square root of [(sum of dx²) x (sum of dy²)] | dx, dy deviations from the means of X and Y | 6.5 |
| Shared variance = r² | r = 0.60 gives 0.36 | 6.5 |
| Normal curve | Mean to 1 SD: 34.13 per cent; within 1 SD: 68.27; within 2 SD: 95.45; within 3 SD: 99.73 | 6.5 |
| Indicative percentage = grade point average x 9.5 | CBSE school CCE scheme only; not a CBCS rule | 6.6 |
| Credits per week = lecture and tutorial hours + practical hours / 2 | 2015 UGC definition | 6.7 |
| Credit points = credits x grade point | For one course | 6.7 |
| SGPA = sum of (Ci x Gi) / sum of Ci | Ci credits of the ith course, Gi grade point; F and Ab stay in the denominator | 6.7 |
| CGPA = sum of (semester credits x SGPA) / total credits | Weight each semester by its credits | 6.7 |
| Required SGPA = [(credits so far + next credits) x target CGPA - credits so far x present CGPA] / next credits | Target-grade problems | 6.7 |
| Weighted rubric score = sum of (weight x criterion score) | Weights as fractions | 6.9 |
| Member's mark = group mark x member's average peer rating / group's average rating | Group-work contribution factor | 6.9 |

# Confusable Pairs at a Glance

Every "Often confused" box in the guide is collected here, in the order of the sections. Cover the middle column and test yourself: can you state the deciding difference for each pair?

| Pair | The deciding difference | Section |
|---|---|---|
| Training and teaching | Training builds one skill to a standard by practice; teaching also seeks understanding and judgement | 1.1 |
| Instruction and teaching | Instruction passes on organised knowledge and can be done by a book or machine; teaching involves a person and aims at understanding; all teaching includes instruction, not the reverse | 1.1 |
| Indoctrination and teaching | Decided by manner and aim, not subject: indoctrination wants belief without question; teaching gives reasons and welcomes objections | 1.1 |
| Conditioning and training | Conditioning links stimulus and response with no understanding (lowest); training builds a skill by practice | 1.1 |
| Gage and B. O. Smith definitions | Gage: interpersonal influence on behaviour potential; Smith: system of actions intended to induce learning | 1.1 |
| Morrison and Amidon definitions | Morrison: more mature and less mature personality; Amidon: interactive process of classroom talk | 1.1 |
| Teacher-learner-content and objectives-learning experiences-evaluation | First is the triad of teaching (three focal points); second is the tripolar view of the educative process | 1.1 |
| Task sense and achievement sense of teaching | Task: trying, need not succeed; achievement: implies learning took place | 1.1 |
| Pre-active and post-active phase | Pre-active plans before the lesson (fixing objectives); post-active evaluates after it (selecting tests, changing strategies) | 1.2 |
| Sizing up the class and evaluating outcomes | Sizing up is interactive (facing the class); evaluating outcomes is post-active | 1.2 |
| Teacher as independent variable and method as independent variable | In analysis of teaching the teacher is independent; in an experiment on methods the method is independent | 1.2 |
| Intervening and moderator variable | Intervening carries the effect on the causal path; moderator changes the strength or direction of the relation | 1.2 |
| Diagnostic and prescriptive function | Diagnostic finds entry behaviour and difficulties; prescriptive chooses content and methods to remedy them | 1.2 |
| Aim and objective | Aim is broad, long-term, not directly testable; objective is narrow and testable at the end of a lesson or unit | 1.3 |
| Objective and teacher activity | Objective states what the learner will do; "the teacher will explain" is not an objective | 1.3 |
| Condition and criterion (Mager) | Condition is the circumstance ("given a map"); criterion is the standard ("90 per cent accuracy") | 1.3 |
| Mager and Bloom | Mager: how to write one objective; Bloom: how to classify objectives into levels | 1.3 |
| Mager and Gronlund | Mager: single objective with performance, condition, criterion; Gronlund: general objective clarified by specific learning outcomes | 1.3 |
| Programme outcomes and programme educational objectives | POs: at graduation; PEOs: a few years into the career | 1.3 |
| Original top and revised top of Bloom's taxonomy | 1956: Evaluation highest, Synthesis fifth; 2001: Create highest, Evaluate fifth | 1.4 |
| Analyse and Evaluate | Analyse breaks into parts and relates them; Evaluate judges against criteria | 1.4 |
| Comprehension (translation) and Application | Changing words into a graph is translation (Comprehension); Application uses a rule in a new situation | 1.4 |
| Evaluate (cognitive) and Valuing (affective) | Evaluate: justifying a choice against criteria; Valuing: attaching worth and commitment | 1.4 |
| Valuing and Organisation | Valuing concerns one value; Organisation builds several values into a system | 1.4 |
| Organisation and Characterisation | Organisation builds the value system; Characterisation is when it governs conduct consistently | 1.4 |
| Receiving and Responding | Mere attention is Receiving; active reaction (answering, complying, volunteering) is Responding | 1.4 |
| Imitation and Receiving | Imitation is Dave's first psychomotor level; Receiving is the first affective level | 1.4 |
| Simpson and Dave | Simpson: seven levels, Perception to Origination; Dave: five levels, Imitation to Naturalisation | 1.4 |
| Complexity, internalisation, coordination | Organising principles of cognitive, affective and psychomotor domains respectively | 1.4 |
| Herbart's steps and Morrison's steps | Herbart: preparation, presentation, comparison/association, generalisation, application; Morrison: exploration, presentation, assimilation, organisation, recitation; only presentation is common | 1.5 |
| Preparation and exploration | Preparation (Herbart) recalls related knowledge and states the aim; exploration (Morrison) finds what learners already know of the unit | 1.5 |
| Application and recitation | Application is Herbart's last step; recitation is Morrison's last step | 1.5 |
| Assimilation and organisation | Assimilation is supervised study ending in a mastery test; organisation is an unaided written outline | 1.5 |
| Understanding level and reflective level | Understanding: teacher explains, learner grasps and applies in familiar cases; reflective: learner meets an unfamiliar problem and reaches and tests conclusions | 1.5 |
| Hunt and Herbart | Hunt: reflective level; Herbart: memory level | 1.5 |
| Extrinsic and intrinsic motivation (levels) | Extrinsic (marks, praise) marks the memory level; intrinsic (felt problem) marks the reflective level | 1.5 |
| Objective tests and essay tests (levels) | Memory: recall and recognition items; understanding: both; reflective: mainly essay type | 1.5 |
| Concrete to abstract vs particular to general | First is about kind of experience (things to ideas); second is about scope (cases to a rule); separate maxims | 1.6 |
| Particular to general vs empirical to rational | Several cases lead to one rule vs one observed fact given its reasoned explanation | 1.6 |
| Whole to part vs analysis to synthesis | Present the whole first vs break into elements and then rebuild | 1.6 |
| Psychological order vs logical order | Follows the learner's interests and readiness vs follows the subject's expert structure; teach psychological first | 1.6 |
| Inductive vs deductive method | Examples to rule, slow, suits discovery vs rule to examples, quick, suits practice; combine induction then deduction | 1.6 |
| Analytic vs synthetic method | Unknown (conclusion) back to known (data), shows why vs known to unknown, short finished proof | 1.6 |
| Maxim "known to unknown" vs analytic method "unknown to known" | Linking new knowledge to old vs direction of a proof from conclusion back to data | 1.6 |
| Deduction vs analysis | Applying a general rule to cases vs working backwards from a conclusion | 1.6 |
| Reflective practice vs subject mastery | Examining one's own teaching to improve it vs knowing the subject | 1.7 |
| Democratic vs laissez-faire teaching | Guides, reasons, invites participation and still corrects errors vs gives up direction | 1.7 |
| Authoritarian vs democratic groups (Lewin, Lippitt and White) | Worked mainly while the leader watched vs kept working in the leader's absence with good morale | 1.7 |
| Classroom management vs punishment | Creates conditions for engaged learning time and prevents problems vs reacts after problems | 1.7 |
| Input vs outcome of teaching | Lectures, notes, coverage vs learning achieved; outcome judges teaching | 1.7 |
| Withitness vs overlapping (Kounin) | Showing awareness of the whole room vs attending to two matters at once | 1.7 |
| Presage vs context vs process vs product variables | What the teacher brings vs the setting vs classroom behaviour vs pupil outcomes | 1.7 |
| Teacher immediacy vs clarity vs credibility vs efficacy | Reducing psychological distance vs intelligible explanation vs perceived competence and trust vs teacher's belief in own capability | 1.7 |
| Set induction vs closure | Opens the lesson, links to previous knowledge vs ends it and consolidates | 1.8 |
| Set induction vs stimulus variation | Attention at the opening only vs attention throughout by changes in teacher behaviour | 1.8 |
| Reinforcement vs probing | Praises or accepts a response vs asks follow-up questions to deepen it | 1.8 |
| Probing vs illustrating with examples | Works on a student's answer vs clarifies the idea for the whole class | 1.8 |
| Prompting vs redirection (probing) | Hints after no or wrong answer vs same question put to other students | 1.8 |
| Convergent vs divergent question | One correct answer (even if computed) vs many acceptable answers, usually higher-order | 1.8 |
| Micro-teaching vs simulated teaching | Scaled-down cycle for one skill with feedback vs role-play among trainees with rotated roles | 1.8 |
| Micro-teaching vs link practice | One skill at a time vs several skills combined in longer lessons with larger groups | 1.8 |
| Stanford model vs Indian model of micro-teaching | Real pupils and videotape vs peers as pupils, supervisor and peer feedback, no costly equipment; same cycle | 1.8 |
| Knowledge acquisition vs skill acquisition phase | Reading, discussion, model lesson vs plan-teach-feedback-re-plan-re-teach | 1.8 |
| Indirect vs direct influence (Flanders) | Categories 1-4 (feelings, praise, students' ideas, questions) vs 5-7 (lecture, directions, criticism) | 1.8 |
| Student response (8) vs student initiation (9) | Answers the teacher vs talk the student starts | 1.8 |
| I/D ratio vs its inverse | Indirect over direct (120/240 = 0.5) vs direct over indirect (2.0) | 1.8 |
| PCK vs TPACK | Content plus pedagogy (Shulman 1986) vs adds technology (Mishra and Koehler 2006) | 1.8 |
| TPACK vs SAMR | Teacher knowledge framework vs levels of technology use | 1.8 |
| Social system vs support system (models) | Roles and relationships of people vs materials and facilities | 1.9 |
| Syntax vs focus vs principles of reaction | Sequence of phases vs central aim vs how the teacher responds | 1.9 |
| Concept attainment vs advance organiser | Bruner; examples and non-examples vs Ausubel; general framework before detail | 1.9 |
| Inquiry training vs group investigation | Suchman, information-processing, individual questioning vs Thelen, social, democratic group process | 1.9 |
| Inquiry training vs synectics | Questioning a puzzling event vs creativity through analogy (Gordon, personal family) | 1.9 |
| Glaser's basic teaching model vs Gagné's nine events | Four components of a teaching system with feedback loops vs nine events of a lesson | 1.9 |
| Academic freedom vs freedom from rules | Inquiry, expression, choice of method vs not ignoring curriculum or evaluation norms | 1.10 |
| Academic freedom vs plagiarism | Right to publish one's own ideas vs no right to others' work | 1.10 |
| Confidentiality vs accountability | Keeping personal information private vs answering for one's work | 1.10 |
| Marks of a profession vs conditions of employment | Specialised knowledge, training, service motive, code vs salary, post, facilities | 1.10 |
| Gift vs ghost authorship | Non-contributor put in vs real contributor left out | 1.10 |
| NEP 2020 CPD vs four-year integrated B.Ed. | In-service growth (50 hours a year) vs pre-service qualification (by 2030); separate provisions | 1.10 |
| Identity vs role confusion / intimacy vs isolation | Identity is Erikson's adolescent crisis; intimacy belongs to young adulthood | 2.1 |
| Industry vs inferiority / identity vs role confusion | Industry is school age (about 6-12); identity is adolescence | 2.1 |
| Hall / Havighurst / Erikson | Hall: storm and stress; Havighurst: developmental tasks; Erikson: psychosocial stages | 2.1 |
| Moratorium / diffusion | Both lack commitment; moratorium explores, diffusion does not | 2.1 |
| Foreclosure / achievement | Both committed; foreclosure without exploration, achievement after exploration | 2.1 |
| Imaginary audience / personal fable | Being watched and judged vs being unique and invulnerable (both Elkind) | 2.1 |
| Arnett / Levinson | Emerging adulthood (18-25) vs seasons of adult life | 2.1 |
| Assimilation / accommodation | Fit experience into old schema vs change the schema | 2.2 |
| Equilibration / conservation | Balancing process behind development vs a concrete-operational achievement | 2.2 |
| Centration / conservation | Focusing on one feature (pre-operational) is why conservation fails | 2.2 |
| Concrete operational / formal operational | Logic on real objects vs abstract, hypothetical reasoning | 2.2 |
| Zone of proximal development / level of potential development | The zone is the gap; potential level is its upper edge | 2.2 |
| Cognitive constructivism / social constructivism | Piaget (individual) vs Vygotsky (interaction and culture) | 2.2 |
| More knowledgeable other / generalised other | Helper in the ZPD vs G. H. Mead's term for society's attitudes | 2.2 |
| Scaffolding / advance organiser | Help during the task, withdrawn vs framework given before the lesson | 2.2 |
| Enactive / iconic | Action vs images | 2.2 |
| Bruner / Ausubel | Discovery learning vs meaningful reception learning | 2.2 |
| Egocentric speech / private speech | Piaget's reading (immaturity) vs Vygotsky's (tool guiding thought) | 2.2 |
| Comparative / expository organiser | Relates to familiar material vs framework for wholly new material | 2.2 |
| Progressive differentiation / integrative reconciliation | General to detailed vs relating and reconciling ideas | 2.2 |
| Maintenance / elaborative rehearsal | Plain repetition vs linking to existing knowledge | 2.2 |
| Serial position / von Restorff effect | Position decides recall vs distinctiveness decides recall | 2.2 |
| Proactive / retroactive interference | Old disturbs new vs new disturbs old | 2.2 |
| Classical / operant conditioning | Involuntary response by pairing vs voluntary behaviour by consequences | 2.3 |
| Negative reinforcement / punishment | Removes unpleasant, behaviour rises vs behaviour falls | 2.3 |
| Negative punishment / negative reinforcement | Removes pleasant to reduce vs removes unpleasant to increase | 2.3 |
| Extinction / punishment | Reinforcer withheld vs unpleasant applied or pleasant removed | 2.3 |
| Stimulus generalisation / discrimination | Similar stimuli evoke response vs only the original does | 2.3 |
| Stimulus generalisation / response generalisation | Many stimuli, one response vs one stimulus, many responses | 2.3 |
| Law of readiness / law of effect | Preparedness before acting vs consequences after acting | 2.3 |
| Trial and error / insight | Gradual (Thorndike) vs sudden (Köhler) | 2.3 |
| Insight / latent learning | Köhler's chimpanzees vs Tolman's rats | 2.3 |
| Ratio / interval schedules | Number of responses vs time | 2.3 |
| Direct / vicarious reinforcement | Received oneself vs seen given to a model | 2.3 |
| Bruner / Gagné | Discovery learning vs conditions and hierarchy of learning | 2.3 |
| Shaping / chaining | Refining one response by approximations vs linking separate responses | 2.3 |
| Connectivism / constructivism | Learning in networks and devices vs learner's own constructions | 2.3 |
| VARK / Bruner's modes | Learning preferences vs ways knowledge is represented | 2.4 |
| Diverger / assimilator | Both reflective observation; concrete experience vs abstract conceptualisation | 2.4 |
| Converger / accommodator | Both active experimentation; abstract conceptualisation vs concrete experience | 2.4 |
| Field-independent / field-dependent | Analytical, self-directed vs global, socially oriented | 2.4 |
| Impulsive / reflective tempo | Fast and inaccurate vs slow and accurate (Kagan) | 2.4 |
| Reflector / theorist | Watches and gathers data vs builds logical theory | 2.4 |
| Pragmatist / activist | Tests ideas in practice vs plunges into new experience | 2.4 |
| Pre-conventional / conventional | Punishment and self-interest vs approval and law and order | 2.5 |
| Stage 4 / stage 5 (Kohlberg) | Upholds rules as such vs rules as changeable social contract | 2.5 |
| Kohlberg's levels / Piaget's moralities | Pre-/conventional/post-conventional vs heteronomous/autonomous | 2.5 |
| Moral reasoning / identity | Kohlberg vs Erikson and Marcia | 2.5 |
| intrinsic / extrinsic motivation | reward inside the activity (interest, satisfaction) or outside it (marks, scholarship, fear) | 2.6 |
| deficiency / growth needs (Maslow) | physiological, safety, belonging, esteem stop motivating once met; self-actualisation keeps motivating; esteem is still a deficiency need | 2.6 |
| introduced / popularised emotional intelligence | Salovey and Mayer introduced the term; Goleman popularised it (1995) | 2.6 |
| emotional intelligence / Gardner's personal intelligences | Goleman's EI is a separate theory from Gardner's interpersonal and intrapersonal intelligences | 2.6 |
| self-efficacy / self-esteem | belief in capability for a task, or general feeling of worth | 2.6 |
| Yerkes-Dodson: moderate / maximum arousal | performance peaks at moderate arousal (inverted U); optimum lower for hard tasks | 2.6 |
| Weiner: effort / ability | both internal; effort unstable, ability stable | 2.6 |
| learned helplessness / test anxiety | stops trying altogether after uncontrollable failure, or fear interfering during a test | 2.6 |
| fixed (entity) / growth (incremental) mindset | ability unchangeable, or grows with effort; "incremental" = growth | 2.6 |
| mastery / performance goal | aim to understand and improve, or to look able compared with others | 2.6 |
| Alderfer / McClelland three-need sets | existence, relatedness, growth; or achievement, affiliation, power | 2.6 |
| Deci and Ryan / Alderfer "relatedness" | only Deci and Ryan have autonomy and competence | 2.6 |
| ARCS confidence / satisfaction | expectation of success before and during; good feeling after achievement | 2.6 |
| overjustification / Hawthorne / Pygmalion / Zeigarnik | reward kills interest; being observed; teacher expectation; unfinished tasks remembered | 2.6 |
| Vroom: multiply / add | terms are multiplied; any zero gives zero | 2.6 |
| pedagogy / andragogy | teaching children (dependent, subject-centred, external) or helping adults learn (self-directing, problem-centred, internal) | 2.7 |
| readiness / orientation (Knowles) | when adults become ready (role demands), or how content is organised (around problems) | 2.7 |
| self-directed / incidental / programmed learning | planned by learner; unplanned; path fixed by programme | 2.7 |
| learning contract / portfolio | plan drawn up in advance, or evidence of work already done | 2.7 |
| andragogy / heutagogy | self-directed within a teacher-designed course, or learner decides what and how (self-determined) | 2.7 |
| Mezirow / Kolb | transforming a frame of reference after a disorienting dilemma, or general cycle of learning from experience | 2.7 |
| situational / institutional / dispositional barriers | learner's life circumstances; provider's practices; learner's attitudes | 2.7 |
| inter- / intra-individual differences | between persons, or within one person's abilities | 2.8 |
| aptitude / achievement | potential to learn with training (future), or what has been learnt | 2.8 |
| aptitude / attitude (and Hindi अभिक्षमता / अभिवृत्ति) | potential to learn, or settled favourable/unfavourable tendency | 2.8 |
| Spearman / Thurstone | g plus s, or seven primary mental abilities | 2.8 |
| Thurstone / Guilford | both many abilities; three-dimensional operations-contents-products is Guilford's | 2.8 |
| fluid / crystallised intelligence | reasoning on new problems (declines), or stored knowledge (maintained or grows) | 2.8 |
| interpersonal / intrapersonal intelligence | understanding others, or understanding oneself | 2.8 |
| Sternberg creative / practical | dealing with novelty, or adapting to everyday settings ("street-smart") | 2.8 |
| convergent / divergent production | one right answer, or many answers (creativity) | 2.8 |
| ratio / deviation IQ | MA ÷ CA × 100, or standing among same-age peers (mean 100, SD 15) | 2.8 |
| Flynn / Matthew effect | rise in average IQ across generations, or widening gaps between individuals | 2.8 |
| enrichment / acceleration | deeper, wider work at same level, or faster movement through curriculum | 2.9 |
| slow learner / underachiever | low ability with matching attainment, or adequate ability with low attainment | 2.9 |
| slow learner / learning disability | generally below-average ability, or specific difficulty despite average or higher intelligence | 2.9 |
| dyslexia / dysgraphia / dyscalculia / dyspraxia | reading; handwriting; number; movement | 2.9 |
| alt text / captions | for learners who cannot see, or who cannot hear | 2.9 |
| integration / inclusion | learner fits unchanged system, or system changes for learner | 2.9 |
| RPwD 5 per cent / 4 per cent | seats in government and aided higher education, or vacancies in government employment | 2.9 |
| RPwD Act 2016 / RTE Act 2009 | rights of persons with disabilities, or free and compulsory education for children | 2.9 |
| differentiated instruction / UDL | teacher varies content, process, product for learners present, or variety designed in from the start for all | 2.9 |
| Support material vs instructional facilities | Material is made or chosen for a particular content (chart, worksheet, slides, e-journal); facilities are lasting infrastructure the institution provides for all teaching (laboratory, library, projector, network) | 3.1, 3.4, 3.5 |
| Learning environment vs instructional facilities | Environment is the condition of the surroundings (light, noise, seating, climate); facilities are the equipment and spaces themselves | 3.1, 3.5 |
| Necessary vs sufficient condition | Necessary: cannot succeed without it; sufficient: enough on its own. Subject mastery and access to facilities are necessary, not sufficient | 3.1, 3.5 |
| Pygmalion effect vs Golem effect | Same self-fulfilling mechanism; high expectations raise performance (Pygmalion), low expectations lower it (Golem) | 3.2 |
| Pygmalion effect vs Hawthorne effect | Pygmalion works through the teacher's expectations; Hawthorne through the subjects' awareness of being observed | 3.2, 3.6 |
| Pygmalion effect vs halo effect | Pygmalion changes actual performance; halo changes only the rater's judgement | 3.2, 3.6 |
| Teacher efficacy vs teacher autonomy | Efficacy is belief in one's capacity to bring about learning; autonomy is freedom to take professional decisions | 3.2 |
| Maturation vs learning | Maturation comes from growth with age, independent of training; learning comes from experience and practice | 3.3 |
| Aptitude vs prior knowledge | Aptitude is potential to learn; prior knowledge is what has already been learned | 3.3 |
| Cognitive vs affective learner factors | Intelligence, aptitude, prior knowledge are cognitive; interest, attitude, motivation, anxiety are affective | 3.3 |
| Cultural capital vs aptitude | Cultural capital is familiarity with educated culture gained at home; aptitude is the potential to learn | 3.3 |
| Suitability vs accuracy of support material | Suitability concerns the learners' level; accuracy concerns the truth of the content; separate criteria, neither explains the other | 3.4 |
| Physical environment vs psychological climate | Light, air, noise, seating are physical; relationships, warmth, fear are psychological | 3.6 |
| Psychological safety vs laissez-faire climate | Safety keeps guidance and high expectations; laissez-faire withdraws guidance | 3.6 |
| Hawthorne effect vs John Henry effect | Hawthorne: the studied group improves because it is observed; John Henry: the control group works harder to compete | 3.6 |
| Halo effect vs leniency error vs central tendency error | Halo: one trait colours other ratings of the same person; leniency: everyone rated high; central tendency: everyone rated near the middle | 3.6 |
| Board of Studies vs Academic Council | Board of Studies frames the syllabus of one subject; Academic Council approves courses, syllabi and regulations for the university | 3.7 |
| Academic Council vs IQAC | Academic Council approves academic matters; IQAC plans, guides and monitors quality and prepares the AQAR | 3.7 |
| Institutional climate vs classroom climate | Institutional climate (leadership, recognition, staff relations) shapes teacher morale; classroom climate shapes student participation | 3.7 |
| Approach / method | Approach is the underlying view of learning; method is the organised way of teaching a lesson | 4.1 |
| Method / technique | Method organises a whole lesson; technique is a small device inside it (questioning, buzz group) | 4.1 |
| Facilitator / transmitter | Facilitator guides active learners (learner-centred); transmitter delivers content to listeners (teacher-centred) | 4.1 |
| Inductive / deductive | Inductive: examples to rule, slower, discovery; deductive: rule to examples, quicker, practice | 4.1 |
| Lecture / demonstration | Lecture: learners hear; demonstration: learners also see a process performed; neither gives hands-on practice | 4.2 |
| Demonstration / laboratory practice | Demonstration: teacher performs; practice: each learner performs; skills come from practice | 4.2 |
| Economy / participation (lecture) | Lecture is economical (many learners per teacher) but not participatory | 4.2 |
| Immediate confirmation / self-pacing | Knowing at once whether a response is right / going at one's own speed | 4.2 |
| Linear / branching programming | Linear (Skinner): small frames, constructed responses, same path; branching (Crowder): larger frames, multiple choice, remedial frames, path depends on response | 4.2 |
| Mathetics / linear programming | Mathetics (Gilbert): backward chaining, last step first; linear: forward small steps | 4.2 |
| Seminar / symposium | Seminar: one paper discussed in depth; symposium: several speakers on different aspects in turn | 4.3 |
| Symposium / panel discussion | Symposium: prepared talks, no conversation among speakers; panel: informal conversation under a moderator | 4.3 |
| Seminar / workshop | Seminar: paper and discussion; workshop: practical work and a product | 4.3 |
| Seminar / conference | Conference is larger and broader, with plenary and parallel sessions; both include papers | 4.3 |
| Chairperson / moderator / rapporteur / convener | Chairperson conducts seminar or symposium; moderator conducts panel; rapporteur records and reports; convener organises | 4.3 |
| Tutorial / lecture | Tutorial: small group, remedial, supplements; lecture: large group, presentation | 4.3 |
| Team teaching / peer tutoring | Several teachers share one course / students teach students | 4.3 |
| Buzz group / fishbowl | Buzz: many small groups talk at once briefly; fishbowl: inner circle talks, outer circle observes | 4.3 |
| Forum / panel | Forum: the audience talks from the floor; panel: experts on the platform talk | 4.3 |
| Positive / negative interdependence | Cooperation: each succeeds only if others succeed; competition: one succeeds only if others fail | 4.3 |
| Jigsaw / STAD | Jigsaw: members teach one another their parts; STAD: team scores from individual improvement points | 4.3 |
| Project / case method | Project: real task in the field producing something; case: analysis of a situation already written up | 4.4 |
| Project / problem-solving | Problem-solving is chiefly mental and ends in a solution; project is real-life activity ending in a product | 4.4 |
| Project method / PBL | PBL: ill-structured problem first, facilitator, no product required; product is the mark of a project | 4.4 |
| Role play / simulation | Role play: acting a person's part for empathy; simulation: practising a task in a reality-like setting for skills and decisions | 4.4 |
| Role play / dramatisation | Role play is unscripted; dramatisation uses a learnt script | 4.4 |
| Heuristic / inductive | Heuristic: learner discovers by own experiment, minimal telling; inductive: reasoning from examples to rule | 4.4 |
| Kilpatrick / Parkhurst / Armstrong / Montessori | Project method / Dalton Plan / heuristic method / early childhood method | 4.4 |
| Mastery learning / PSI | Bloom: class taught together with formative tests and correctives; Keller: self-paced written units with proctors | 4.4 |
| Psychodrama / sociodrama | Psychodrama: individual's private problem, therapy; sociodrama: problem shared by a group | 4.4 |
| Case study / incident method | Case: full facts given; incident (Pigors): brief incident, facts obtained by questioning | 4.4 |
| Induction / deduction (logic) | Induction: instances to rule, conclusion probable; deduction: rule to case, certain if premises true | 4.4 |
| Action research / ex post facto | Action research: plan-act-observe-reflect to improve own practice; ex post facto: studies effects after the event without intervention | 4.4 |
| Synchronous / asynchronous | Same time (live class, webinar, chat) / different times (recorded lecture, forum, e-mail) | 4.5 |
| Blended / flipped | Blended: general mix of face-to-face and online; flipped: one blended model with content online first, class for application | 4.5 |
| Blended / distance learning | Blended keeps regular face-to-face classes; distance learning separates teacher and learner for most of the course | 4.5 |
| Flipped / conventional classroom | Flipped: content at home, application in class; conventional: content in class, homework at home | 4.5 |
| Rotation / flex model | Rotation: move between modes on a schedule; flex: online learning is the backbone with flexible support | 4.5 |
| SWAYAM / SWAYAM PRABHA | SWAYAM: web MOOCs with quizzes, exams, credits, needs internet; SWAYAM PRABHA: one-way DTH TV, no internet, no assessment | 4.6 |
| e-PG Pathshala / ePathshala | e-PG Pathshala: postgraduate e-content (UGC, INFLIBNET, NME-ICT); ePathshala: NCERT school textbooks | 4.6 |
| Shodhganga / Shodhgangotri | Submitted theses / synopses of research in progress | 4.6 |
| Shodhganga / ShodhShuddhi | Thesis repository / plagiarism-detection software access | 4.6 |
| DIKSHA / SWAYAM | DIKSHA: school education and teacher training; SWAYAM: courses school to PG and beyond | 4.6 |
| NPTEL / NDLI | Online courses by IITs and IISc / digital library search platform (IIT Kharagpur) | 4.6 |
| Massive / open (MOOC) | Massive: unlimited enrolment; open: no entry requirements, usually free | 4.6 |
| cMOOC / xMOOC / SPOC | cMOOC: connectivist, learner networks (Siemens, Downes); xMOOC: structured instructor-led; SPOC: small private course for one group | 4.6 |
| NAD / ABC | NAD stores degrees and mark-sheets for verification (DigiLocker); ABC records credits earned | 4.6 |
| Parent / host institution | Parent: where the student is enrolled and credits are recorded; host: whose faculty offers the SWAYAM course | 4.6 |
| NETF / NRF / NTA | NETF: forum on educational technology; NRF: funds research; NTA: conducts tests | 4.6 |
| Study trip vs demonstration (Dale's cone) | Demonstrations lie below study trips; a study trip is more abstract in Dale's order | 5.1 |
| Visual symbols vs verbal symbols | Visual symbols (charts, maps, graphs) lie just below verbal symbols (words), which form the apex | 5.1 |
| Visual symbols vs still pictures | A photograph resembles its object (still pictures band, iconic); a graph is an abstract sign (visual symbols, symbolic) | 5.1 |
| Projected vs non-projected aids | Projected aids need a projector to throw an image on a screen (epidiascope, OHP, slides, filmstrip); non-projected are seen directly (chalkboard, charts, flash cards, flannel board, models) | 5.1 |
| Audio vs audio-visual aids | Radio and tape recorder address hearing only; television and sound film address both; a silent filmstrip is visual | 5.1 |
| Model vs specimen | A model represents the real thing (enlarged, reduced or simplified); a specimen is the real thing in sample form | 5.2 |
| Diorama vs mock-up vs cut-away model | Diorama: miniature scene before a painted background; mock-up: simplified working replica for training; cut-away: shows the inside | 5.2 |
| Chalkboard vs prepared slide | Chalkboard suits content that unfolds during the lesson; slide suits content prepared in advance | 5.2 |
| Flannel board vs chart | Both non-projected visual aids; only the flannel board lets a display be built up and rearranged | 5.2 |
| Episcope vs diascope | Episcope projects opaque material by reflected light; diascope (slide projector) projects transparent slides | 5.3 |
| Epidiascope vs OHP | Epidiascope: opaque objects and slides, darkened room; OHP: large transparencies, lit room, teacher faces class | 5.3 |
| Webinar vs podcast | Webinar is live (synchronous); podcast is on demand (asynchronous) | 5.4 |
| Wiki vs blog | Wiki is jointly edited with change history; blog is a journal of dated posts | 5.4 |
| Video conferencing vs webcast | Video conferencing is two-way among several places; webcast is one-way from one source to many | 5.4 |
| LMS vs file-sharing service | Both store files; only an LMS tracks and reports each learner's progress | 5.4 |
| Open source vs free of charge | Open source code may be modified and shared (Moodle); a free service may still be proprietary (Google Classroom) | 5.4 |
| CC SA vs ND vs NC | SA: adaptations under the same licence; ND: no adaptations distributed; NC: no commercial use | 5.4 |
| SAMR Substitution vs Augmentation | Substitution: no functional change; Augmentation: functional improvement (e.g. spell-check) | 5.4 |
| Self-contained vs self-evaluating (SLM) | Self-contained: needs no outside sources; self-evaluating: in-text questions with answers let the learner check progress | 5.4 |
| Measurement vs evaluation | Measurement gives a number and stops; evaluation adds a judgement of worth | 6.1 |
| Assessment vs evaluation | Assessment gathers and interprets evidence; evaluation judges worth and includes assessment | 6.1 |
| Test vs measurement | Test is the instrument; measurement is the process of assigning numbers | 6.1 |
| Evaluation triangle vs teaching triangle | Objectives, learning experiences, evaluation vs teacher, learner, curriculum | 6.1 |
| Instructional vs guidance function | Improving the present course vs helping choose courses or careers | 6.1 |
| Reliability vs validity | Consistency vs measuring the right thing; reliable-not-valid possible, valid-not-reliable impossible | 6.2 |
| Objectivity vs reliability | Freedom from scorer judgement vs consistency of scores generally | 6.2 |
| Content vs face validity | Systematic check against blueprint vs surface impression | 6.2 |
| Concurrent vs predictive validity | Criterion available now vs criterion obtained later | 6.2 |
| Construct vs content validity | Theoretical trait reflected vs course adequately sampled | 6.2 |
| Test-retest vs parallel forms | Same test twice (stability) vs two equivalent versions (equivalence) | 6.2 |
| Split-half vs Kuder-Richardson | Halves correlated and Spearman-Brown corrected vs item-level 1/0 data | 6.2 |
| SEM vs standard deviation | Error band around one person's score vs spread across the group | 6.2 |
| Construct under-representation vs construct-irrelevant variance | Something essential missing vs something extra measured | 6.2 |
| Formative vs summative | Use during learning to improve vs use at end to certify; use decides, not instrument | 6.3 |
| Formative vs diagnostic | Shows a weakness and gives quick feedback vs finds cause of persistent difficulty | 6.3 |
| Diagnostic vs prognostic | Causes of present errors vs forecast of future performance | 6.3 |
| Placement vs diagnostic | Entry behaviour before instruction vs causes of persisting difficulty | 6.3 |
| Norm-referenced vs criterion-referenced | Comparison with others (cut-off floats) vs fixed standard (number passing floats) | 6.3 |
| Percentile rank vs percentage of objectives mastered | Norm-referenced vs criterion-referenced | 6.3 |
| Criterion-referenced vs ipsative | Fixed external standard vs learner's own earlier work | 6.3 |
| Assessment for learning vs assessment as learning | Teacher uses the evidence vs students use it to regulate themselves | 6.3 |
| Scriven vs Bloom (formative/summative) | Scriven 1967, curriculum; Bloom, Hastings, Madaus 1971, student learning | 6.3 |
| Direct vs indirect assessment | Examines performance vs gathers opinions (surveys) | 6.3 |
| Stem/key/distractor vs premise/response | Parts of an MCQ vs columns of a matching item | 6.4 |
| Supply-type vs selection-type items | Student writes the answer vs chooses it | 6.4 |
| Blueprint vs marking scheme | Planned before item writing for content validity vs prepared for written items | 6.4 |
| Checklist vs rating scale vs rubric | Present/absent vs degree vs degree with descriptors per level | 6.4 |
| Anecdotal vs cumulative record vs portfolio | Single incident vs institution's year-by-year record vs student's selected work | 6.4 |
| Facility value vs discrimination index | How many got it right vs whether able did better than weak | 6.4 |
| High difficulty index vs difficult item | High facility value means an easy item | 6.4 |
| Percentile rank vs percentage of marks | Position among examinees vs share of maximum marks | 6.5 |
| Percentile vs percentile rank | A score vs the percentage of the group below a score | 6.5 |
| Mean vs median | Average pulled by extremes vs middle score unaffected | 6.5 |
| Correlation vs causation | Going together vs one producing the other | 6.5 |
| r vs shared variance | r = 0.60 gives shared variance r squared = 0.36 | 6.5 |
| Ordinal vs interval scale | Order only (ranks) vs equal units | 6.5 |
| Interval vs ratio scale | No true zero vs true zero; test scores lack a true zero | 6.5 |
| Continuous / comprehensive (CCE) | Continuous = regular, through the year (time); comprehensive = scholastic plus co-scholastic (coverage) | 6.6 |
| Scholastic / co-scholastic | Scholastic = curricular subjects and intellectual abilities; co-scholastic = life skills, attitudes, values, health and physical education, cultural activities | 6.6 |
| Absolute / relative grading | Absolute = fixed mark boundaries, anyone can reach any grade (criterion-referenced); relative = fixed proportions, grade by rank (norm-referenced) | 6.6 |
| Grading on the curve / criterion-referenced grading | On the curve fixes proportions in advance, so it is norm-referenced | 6.6 |
| Direct / indirect grading | Direct = grade straight from qualitative judgement; indirect = marks first, then converted | 6.6 |
| SGPA / CGPA | SGPA = one semester; CGPA = all semesters completed so far | 6.7 |
| Credits / credit points | Credits = weight of course; credit points = credits x grade point | 6.7 |
| Discipline Specific Elective / Generic Elective | DSE = from the main discipline; GE = from an unrelated discipline | 6.7 |
| AECC / SEC | AECC = Environmental Science and English/MIL Communication, compulsory; SEC = skill- or value-based, chosen from a pool | 6.7 |
| A / A+ (UGC scale) | A = Very Good, 8; A+ = Excellent, 9 | 6.7 |
| B / C (UGC scale) | B = Above Average, 6; C = Average, 5 | 6.7 |
| P / F (UGC scale) | P = Pass, 4 (lowest pass); F = Fail, 0 (Ab also 0) | 6.7 |
| UG certificate / UG diploma | Certificate after one year; diploma after two years | 6.7 |
| Academic Bank of Credits / National Academic Depository | ABC stores credits; NAD stores awards (degrees, mark-sheets) | 6.7 |
| CBCS 2015 / CCFUP 2022 course types | CBCS: core, DSE, GE, AECC, SEC; CCFUP: major, minor, MDC, AEC, SEC, VAC, internship | 6.7 |
| CBT / OMR test | CBT answers changeable until submission; OMR circles cannot be altered | 6.8 |
| Normalisation / randomisation | Normalisation equates scores of different shift papers; randomisation shuffles item order | 6.8 |
| Adaptive testing / linear-on-the-fly testing | CAT adapts each item during the test; LOFT builds a unique form before the test without adapting | 6.8 |
| Item response theory / classical test theory | IRT item parameters are sample-independent (needed for CAT); CTT indices change with the group | 6.8 |
| Mock test / actual test | Mock = practice with the interface, different questions | 6.8 |
| Analytic / holistic rubric | Analytic = score per criterion, profile; holistic = one overall score | 6.9 |
| Rubric / checklist | Rubric describes levels per criterion; checklist records present/absent only | 6.9 |
| Criteria / descriptors | Criteria = aspects judged; descriptors = what each level of each aspect looks like | 6.9 |
| Portfolio / folder | Portfolio = selected work with reflection; folder = everything unselected | 6.9 |
| Open-book / on-demand examination | Open-book = books allowed during the test; on-demand = learner chooses when to sit | 6.9 |
| On-demand / adaptive testing | On-demand = choice of timing; adaptive = difficulty changes during the test | 6.9 |
| Self- / peer assessment | Self = own work; peer = classmates' work | 6.9 |
| Authentic / ipsative assessment | Authentic = real-world task; ipsative = comparison with own earlier performance | 6.9 |
| Positive / negative washback | Positive = test encourages desirable learning; negative = test narrows teaching | 6.9 |
| Washback / practice effect | Washback = effect of a test on teaching and learning; practice effect = score gain from retaking | 6.9 |

# Revision Sheets

One sheet per chapter, for the last week. The most-asked points come first; the number in brackets is the section that teaches each point in full. If a point is not clear, go back to that section. The last line of each sheet lists the commonest confusions.

#### Revision Sheet 1: Teaching: Concept, Objectives, Levels, Characteristics and Basic Requirements {-}

- **Levels**, lowest to highest: memory (Johann Friedrich **Herbart**), understanding (Henry Clinton **Morrison**), reflective (Maurice P. **Hunt**; also Morris L. Bigge). If Hunt is an option, choose Hunt. Each level includes the one below; they differ in the amount of thinking demanded. (1.5)
- Herbart's steps: preparation, presentation, comparison/association, generalisation, application. Morrison's: exploration, presentation, assimilation, organisation, recitation. (1.5)
- Memory: teacher dominant, extrinsic motivation, objective tests. Understanding: essay and objective tests. Reflective: felt problem, teacher a democratic guide, intrinsic motivation, mainly essay tests. (1.5)
- **Bloom 1956**: Knowledge, Comprehension, Application, Analysis, Synthesis, Evaluation. **2001** (Anderson and Krathwohl): Remember, Understand, Apply, Analyse, Evaluate, Create; Create is now highest. Knowledge dimension: factual, conceptual, procedural, metacognitive. (1.4)
- Affective (1964): Receiving, Responding, Valuing, Organisation, Characterisation. Psychomotor: Simpson, Perception to Origination (7); Dave, Imitation to Naturalisation (5); Harrow, Reflex movements to Non-discursive communication (6). (1.4)
- Teaching is purposeful and triadic (teacher, learner, subject matter). Continuum: conditioning, training, instruction, teaching. Indoctrination differs by manner and aim, not subject. (1.1)
- Definitions: Morrison, mature and less mature personality; Gage, interpersonal influence on behaviour potential; B. O. Smith, system of actions intended to induce learning; Amidon, interactive process, classroom talk. (1.1)
- Phases: pre-active (fixing objectives), interactive (sizing up the class, diagnosing), post-active (selecting testing devices, evaluating). Teacher independent, learner dependent, content and methods intervening variables. (1.2)
- An objective states what the learner will do. Mager: performance, condition, criterion; ABCD adds Audience. (1.3)
- **Micro-teaching**: Stanford, Dwight W. Allen and colleagues, usually dated 1963; 5 to 10 pupils, 5 to 10 minutes, one skill. Cycle: plan, teach, feedback, re-plan, re-teach, re-feedback. Indian model: 6, 6, 12, 6, 6 = 36 minutes. (1.8)
- **Flanders**: ten categories; 1 to 4 indirect (4 is asking questions), 5 to 7 direct, 8 and 9 student talk, 10 silence; a tally every 3 seconds; I/D = indirect / direct. (1.8)
- Set induction opens the lesson; stimulus variation works throughout; probing deepens a partial answer. (1.8)
- Effective teaching is judged by learning: interactive, feedback-based, flexible, democratic. Lewin, Lippitt and White: democratic groups worked even when the leader was away. (1.7)
- Maxims run from the easier end: known to unknown, simple to complex, concrete to abstract, particular to general, whole to part. Inductive: particular to general; deductive: general to particular. (1.6)
- Joyce and Weil: focus, syntax, social system, principles of reaction, support system. Concept attainment (Bruner), advance organiser (Ausubel), inquiry training (Suchman). (1.9)
- NEP 2020: at least 50 hours of continuous professional development a year. (1.10)

**Traps:** Herbart (memory) against Hunt (reflective); never "Herbert". Evaluation tops 1956, Create tops 2001. Sizing up the class is interactive, not post-active. Flanders category 4 is indirect. Bruner with concept attainment, Ausubel with advance organisers.

#### Revision Sheet 2: Learner's Characteristics {-}

- **Learning theories**: Pavlov, classical conditioning; Thorndike, trial and error, laws of readiness, exercise and effect; Skinner, operant conditioning; Köhler, insight; Tolman, latent learning; Bandura, observational learning (attention, retention, reproduction, motivation). (2.3)
- Reinforcement increases behaviour; punishment decreases it. Negative reinforcement removes something unpleasant. Ratio schedules count responses; interval schedules count time. (2.3)
- Gagné's hierarchy ends: discrimination, concept, rule, problem solving. Dewey: learning by doing. (2.3)
- **Piaget**: sensorimotor (0 to 2), pre-operational (2 to 7), concrete operational (7 to 11), formal operational (11 or 12 onwards). Conservation marks the concrete stage. Order fixed, ages approximate. (2.2)
- **Vygotsky**: the ZPD is the gap between what the learner does alone and with help. Scaffolding is temporary help (term from Wood, Bruner and Ross, 1976). (2.2)
- **Bruner**: discovery; enactive, iconic, symbolic; spiral curriculum. **Ausubel**: meaningful reception learning; advance organiser given before the lesson. (2.2)
- Miller: seven plus or minus two; chunking; distributed practice beats massed. (2.2)
- **Adolescence**: Hall, storm and stress; Erikson, identity versus role confusion; Havighurst, developmental tasks; Marcia, diffusion, foreclosure, moratorium, achievement; Arnett, emerging adulthood (18 to 25). (2.1)
- Maslow, from the base: physiological, safety, love and belonging, esteem, self-actualisation; the lower four are deficiency needs. Intrinsic reward lies inside the activity. (2.6)
- Yerkes-Dodson: inverted U, moderate arousal best. Emotional intelligence: introduced by Salovey and Mayer, popularised by Goleman. (2.6)
- **Andragogy** (Knowles): adults are self-directing, use experience, are ready when life roles demand, problem-centred, internally motivated. (2.7)
- IQ = mental age / chronological age x 100. Spearman, g and s; Thurstone, primary mental abilities; Guilford, convergent and divergent; Cattell, fluid and crystallised; Gardner, multiple intelligences (eight, with naturalistic); Sternberg, triarchic. (2.8)
- VARK (Fleming): Visual, Aural, Read/Write, Kinaesthetic. Kolb's cycle: concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract conceptualisation, active experimentation. (2.4)
- Kohlberg: pre-conventional, conventional (stage 4, law and order), post-conventional. (2.5)
- RPwD Act 2016: 21 disabilities; 5 per cent of higher education seats, 4 per cent of government vacancies. Inclusion changes the institution; integration does not. (2.9)

**Traps:** Negative reinforcement is not punishment. The ZPD is the gap, not the potential level. Identity (adolescence) against intimacy (young adulthood). Readiness (life roles) against orientation (problem-centred). Interpersonal (others) against intrapersonal (oneself).

#### Revision Sheet 3: Factors Affecting Teaching {-}

- Six groups: teacher, learner, support material, instructional facilities, learning environment, institution. Place a factor by asking where it lives and who controls it. (3.1)
- Subject mastery is necessary but not sufficient; no single factor "alone ensures" good teaching. (3.1)
- Teacher factors: subject mastery, communication, questioning, warmth and enthusiasm, expectations. (3.2)
- Pygmalion effect (Rosenthal and Jacobson, 1968): high expectations raise performance, a self-fulfilling prophecy. Golem effect: low expectations lower it. (3.2)
- Learner factors: prior knowledge (entry behaviour), intelligence, interest (affective), anxiety (emotional), health, maturation and readiness. (3.3)
- Support material is made for a lesson (chart, slides); relevance to the objective comes first, then suitability, accuracy, visibility. (3.4)
- Facilities are lasting infrastructure (laboratory, library, projector, network); without a laboratory, psychomotor objectives suffer. Facilities enable but do not cause good teaching. (3.5)
- Environment: physical (light, ventilation, seating, noise) and psychological (respect, freedom from fear). Noise distracts attention; it does not change intelligence. U-shaped seating suits discussion. (3.6)
- Hawthorne effect: people improve because they know they are observed. John Henry effect: the control group competes. Halo effect: one trait colours other ratings. (3.6)
- Institution factors: class size, timetable, workload, evaluation policy, leadership, climate (teacher morale). The Board of Studies frames the syllabus; the Academic Council approves it. (3.7)

**Traps:** Support material against facility. Physical against psychological environment. Hawthorne (students' awareness) against Pygmalion (teacher's expectations). Interest is affective, not cognitive.

#### Revision Sheet 4: Methods of Teaching in Institutions of Higher Learning {-}

- Teacher-centred (lecture, demonstration): teacher transmits, learners passive. Learner-centred (project, problem-solving, discussion, case, role play): teacher facilitates; based on constructivism. No single best method. (4.1)
- Approach is widest, technique smallest. (4.1)
- **SWAYAM**: Study Webs of Active-Learning for Young Aspiring Minds; launched 9 July 2017; free, fee only for the proctored examination. Quadrants: e-Tutorial, e-Content, discussion forum, assessment. Credit transfer up to 40 per cent (2021 Regulations; 20 per cent in 2016). (4.6)
- Coordinators: NPTEL engineering; CEC undergraduate non-technical; UGC postgraduate non-technical; IIM Bangalore management; NCERT and NIOS school. (4.6)
- **SWAYAM PRABHA**: DTH channels, free on DD Free Dish, no internet, one-way, no credits. Began with 32 channels; 2026 sources give 40 or 48, so check before the examination. (4.6)
- e-PG Pathshala (postgraduate) against ePathshala (NCERT, school); Shodhganga (theses) against Shodhgangotri (synopses). (4.6)
- MOOC: massive, open, online, course; low completion. cMOOCs connectivist, xMOOCs instructor-led. (4.6)
- Seminar: one paper in depth, chairperson. Symposium: several talks on different aspects, no conversation. Panel: experts converse, moderator. Workshop: hands-on product. (4.3)
- Brainstorming (Osborn): defer judgement, aim at quantity. Team teaching: several teachers; peer tutoring: students teach students. (4.3)
- Lecture: strength economy; weak in feedback and skills. Linear programming (Skinner): small steps, same path. Branching (Crowder): multiple-choice, remedial frames. (4.2)
- Project method: Kilpatrick, after Dewey; purposing, planning, executing, evaluating. Dalton Plan: Parkhurst. Heuristic: Armstrong. PBL: McMaster, problem first. (4.4)
- Mastery learning: Bloom. Keller Plan: self-paced units with proctors. (4.4)
- Synchronous (live) against asynchronous (forum, recording). Flipped classroom: content at home, application in class; it is one model of blended learning. (4.5)

**Traps:** Seminar, symposium and panel. Linear against branching. SWAYAM against SWAYAM PRABHA. Shodhganga against Shodhgangotri. Blended against flipped.

#### Revision Sheet 5: Teaching Support System {-}

- **Dale's Cone** (*Audiovisual Methods in Teaching*, 1946; eleven bands in 1969): base, direct purposeful experiences; then contrived, dramatised, demonstrations, study trips, exhibits, educational television, motion pictures, recordings and still pictures, visual symbols; apex, verbal symbols. (5.1)
- Study trips lie above demonstrations. The retention percentages often printed on the cone are not Dale's. (5.1)
- Bruner's modes on the cone: enactive (lowest three bands), iconic (middle), symbolic (visual and verbal symbols). (5.1)
- Audio (radio, tape recorder); visual (chart, model, silent filmstrip); audio-visual (television, sound film). (5.1)
- Projected (OHP, slides, epidiascope) against non-projected (chalkboard, chart, model, flash card). Too many aids overload working memory. (5.1)
- Chalkboard: suits material that unfolds in the lesson; loses eye contact. A model represents; a specimen is real. (5.2)
- OHP: transparencies, lit room, teacher faces class. Episcope: opaque material. Epidiascope: opaque and transparent. (5.3)
- Language laboratory: listening and speaking practice. (5.3)
- An LMS tracks each learner's progress. Moodle is open source; Google Classroom is proprietary. (5.4)
- Webinar and video conferencing are synchronous; podcast and wiki are asynchronous. OER may be used, adapted and redistributed under an open licence. (5.4)

**Traps:** The epidiascope is projected. Visual symbols are symbolic, not iconic. Free is not open source. Webinar (live) against podcast (on demand).

#### Revision Sheet 6: Evaluation Systems {-}

- Placement before, formative during, diagnostic for causes of persistent difficulty, summative at the end. The use of results decides the label. (6.3)
- Norm-referenced: compared with others (percentile rank). Criterion-referenced: fixed standard (driving test). Ipsative: own earlier work. (6.3)
- Scriven (1967): formative and summative. Glaser (1963): norm- and criterion-referenced. (6.3)
- Test (instrument), measurement (numbers), assessment (evidence), evaluation (adds a value judgement; widest). (6.1)
- Evaluation triangle: objectives, learning experiences, evaluation. Obtained score = true score + error. (6.1)
- Validity matters most; reliability, objectivity, usability follow. Concurrent validity: criterion now; predictive: criterion later. Content validity: blueprint. (6.2)
- Test-retest gives stability; parallel forms, equivalence; split-half and Kuder-Richardson, internal consistency. A test can be reliable without being valid, not valid without being reliable. (6.2)
- Objective items sample widely; essay items sample narrowly and are scored subjectively. MCQ: stem, key, distractors. (6.4)
- Facility value = (RU + RL) / 2n; high means easy. Discrimination = (RU - RL) / n. Correction for guessing: R - W / (n - 1). (6.4)
- Percentile rank: percentage below, not percentage of marks. z = (X - mean) / SD; T = 50 + 10z. About 68 and 95 per cent lie within 1 and 2 SD. (6.5)
- CCE: continuous (time), comprehensive (scholastic plus co-scholastic). Absolute grading is criterion-referenced; relative grading is norm-referenced. (6.6)
- CBCS (UGC, 2015): one credit = one hour of lecture or two of practical a week. Core, DSE, GE, AECC, SEC. (6.7)
- O 10, A+ 9, A 8, B+ 7, B 6, C 5, P 4, F 0, Ab 0; no D. SGPA = sum of (credits x grade points) / total credits. (6.7)
- One year certificate, two years diploma. ABC stores credits; NAD stores awards. (6.7)
- NTA, set up November 2017, conducts UGC NET as a computer-based test. Shifts are compared by normalisation. Adaptive tests change items during the test. (6.8)
- Rubric: criteria, scale, descriptors. Portfolio: selection and reflection. On-demand: choice of timing. (6.9)

**Traps:** A high difficulty index means an easy item. Percentile rank is not percentage. Relative grading is norm-referenced. Formative against diagnostic. P is 4, not 5.

# Bilingual Glossary

English term, the Hindi term used in this guide, and a one-line meaning. Hindi terms follow NTA's Hindi syllabus where it uses a settled term, and otherwise the term standard in Hindi-medium textbooks.

| English | हिंदी | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| ability enhancement course | क्षमता संवर्धन पाठ्यक्रम | AECC and SEC under CBCS 2015 |
| absolute grading | निरपेक्ष श्रेणीकरण | Grades by fixed mark boundaries |
| abstract | अमूर्त | Removed from direct perception |
| Academic Bank of Credits | एकेडमिक बैंक ऑफ क्रेडिट्स | Digital store of a student's credits |
| Academic Council | विद्या परिषद | University's chief academic body |
| academic freedom | शैक्षणिक स्वतंत्रता | freedom to inquire, teach, research and publish within obligations |
| acceleration | त्वरण | faster progress through the curriculum |
| accommodation | समायोजन | changing a schema to fit new experience |
| achievement | उपलब्धि | what has already been learnt |
| achievement motivation | उपलब्धि अभिप्रेरणा | desire to succeed against a standard of excellence (McClelland) |
| action zone | क्रिया क्षेत्र | Front and centre seats where most interaction occurs |
| administrative function | प्रशासनिक कार्य | use of evaluation for promotion, selection, certification |
| adolescence | किशोरावस्था | period between childhood and adulthood |
| adolescent | किशोर | a person between childhood and adulthood |
| adolescent egocentrism | किशोर अहंकेंद्रिता | Elkind's adolescent self-centredness |
| advance organiser | अग्रिम संगठक | general introduction given before new material (Ausubel) |
| advance organiser model | अग्रिम संगठक प्रतिमान | general framework given before detailed material (Ausubel) |
| affective | भावात्मक | Concerning feelings, interests, attitudes |
| affective domain | भावात्मक पक्ष | area of feelings, attitudes and values |
| aim | लक्ष्य | broad, long-term purpose of education |
| analytic method | विश्लेषण विधि | unknown (conclusion) back to known (data) |
| andragogy | प्रौढ़ शिक्षाशास्त्र | art and science of helping adults learn (Knowles) |
| anecdotal record | उपाख्यानात्मक अभिलेख | brief description of a single significant incident |
| anxiety | दुश्चिंता | worry and tension about what may happen |
| apperception | अवबोधन | absorbing a new idea into existing ideas (Herbart) |
| apperceptive mass | पूर्व-ज्ञान पुंज | body of existing ideas into which new ideas are absorbed (Herbart) |
| approach | उपागम | the broad view of teaching and learning behind a teacher's work |
| aptitude | अभिक्षमता | potential to learn a skill with training |
| aptitude-treatment interaction | अभिक्षमता-उपचार अंतःक्रिया | Effect of a method depends on learner characteristics |
| assessment | आकलन | gathering and interpreting evidence about learning |
| assessment as learning | अधिगम के रूप में आकलन | students monitor and regulate their own learning |
| assessment for learning | अधिगम के लिए आकलन | teacher uses evidence during learning to adjust teaching |
| assessment of learning | अधिगम का आकलन | records achievement at the end |
| assimilation | आत्मसात्करण | fitting new experience into an existing schema |
| asynchronous | असमकालिक | At each learner's own time |
| asynchronous learning | असमकालिक अधिगम | learning in which each takes part at a convenient time |
| attitude | अभिवृत्ति | settled favourable or unfavourable tendency to respond |
| attribution | गुणारोपण | the cause a person gives for success or failure (Weiner) |
| audio aids | श्रव्य सामग्री | Aids addressing hearing |
| audio-visual aids | दृश्य-श्रव्य सामग्री | Aids addressing both sight and hearing |
| authentic assessment | प्रामाणिक आकलन | Real-world task assessment |
| behavioural objective | व्यवहारगत उद्देश्य | objective stated as observable learner behaviour |
| blended learning | मिश्रित अधिगम | planned mix of face-to-face and online learning |
| blueprint | नील पत्र | two-way table of content x objectives before item writing |
| Board of Studies | अध्ययन मंडल | Body that frames a subject's syllabus |
| brainstorming | मस्तिष्क उद्वेलन | group production of many ideas with judgement deferred |
| branching programming | शाखीय अभिक्रमण | Crowder's style: response decides next frame |
| bulletin board | सूचना पट्ट | Board for notices and displays |
| case method | व्यष्टि अध्ययन विधि | analysis of a written real or realistic situation |
| central tendency | केंद्रीय प्रवृत्ति | one number representing the group's general level |
| chalkboard | श्यामपट्ट | Board for writing with chalk |
| checklist | जाँच सूची | records presence or absence of features |
| Choice Based Credit System | विकल्प आधारित क्रेडिट प्रणाली | UGC 2015 system of credits, choice and grades |
| chronological age | कालानुक्रमिक आयु | actual age since birth |
| chunking | खंडीकरण | grouping items into meaningful units |
| class size | कक्षा का आकार | Number of students in a class |
| classical conditioning | शास्त्रीय अनुबंधन | learning by pairing stimuli (Pavlov) |
| classroom climate | कक्षा का वातावरण | Socio-emotional atmosphere of a class |
| classroom management | कक्षा प्रबंधन | organising space, time, routines and relationships for engaged learning |
| closure | समापन | consolidating the lesson at its end |
| co-scholastic area | सह-शैक्षिक क्षेत्र | Life skills, attitudes, values, health, cultural activities |
| code of conduct | आचार संहिता | accepted rules of right conduct for members |
| cognitive | संज्ञानात्मक | to do with knowing and thinking |
| cognitive domain | संज्ञानात्मक पक्ष | area of knowing and thinking |
| cognitive load theory | संज्ञानात्मक भार सिद्धांत | Working memory's limited capacity governs learning design |
| cognitive style | संज्ञानात्मक शैली | typical way of perceiving and thinking |
| communication | संप्रेषण | sending a message from sender to receiver through a channel |
| communication skill | संप्रेषण कौशल | Ability to make ideas reach learners |
| computer based testing | कंप्यूटर आधारित परीक्षण | Test taken and scored on computer |
| computer-assisted instruction | कंप्यूटर सहायक अनुदेशन | Teaching through computer programs |
| computerised adaptive test | कंप्यूटरीकृत अनुकूली परीक्षण | Test whose next item depends on earlier responses |
| concept attainment model | संप्रत्यय उपलब्धि प्रतिमान | concepts formed from examples and non-examples (Bruner) |
| concrete | मूर्त | Directly perceived |
| concurrent validity | समवर्ती वैधता | agreement with a criterion measured at the same time |
| conditioning | अनुबंधन | building a stimulus-response link by pairing or reinforcement, without understanding |
| Cone of Experience | अनुभव शंकु | Dale's arrangement of experiences from concrete to abstract |
| conference | सम्मेलन | large meeting on a broad theme with paper sessions |
| confidentiality | गोपनीयता | keeping personal or examination information private |
| connectivism | संयोजनवाद | theory that learning is forming connections across networks |
| conservation | संरक्षण | quantity stays same despite change in appearance |
| construct validity | संरचना वैधता | scores reflect a theoretical trait |
| constructivism | रचनावाद | learners build their own knowledge |
| content validity | विषयवस्तु वैधता | items represent course content and objectives in proportion |
| Continuous and Comprehensive Evaluation | सतत एवं व्यापक मूल्यांकन | Regular, whole-year assessment of scholastic and co-scholastic areas |
| continuous professional development | सतत व्यावसायिक विकास | lifelong in-service learning of teachers (CPD) |
| conventional | परंपरागत | Kohlberg level guided by approval and law |
| convergent production | अभिसारी चिंतन | thinking towards one correct answer (Guilford) |
| cooperative learning | सहकारी अधिगम | small groups working to a shared goal |
| core course | मूल पाठ्यक्रम | Compulsory course of the programme |
| correlation | सहसंबंध | degree to which two sets of scores go together |
| credit | क्रेडिट | Unit measuring course work by teaching hours |
| credit transfer | क्रेडिट हस्तांतरण | counting credits earned elsewhere towards one's degree |
| criterion-referenced | निकष-संदर्भित | Judged against a fixed standard |
| criterion-referenced test | निकष-संदर्भित परीक्षण | interprets a score against a fixed standard |
| critical reflection | आलोचनात्मक चिंतन | examining one's own assumptions |
| crystallised intelligence | क्रिस्टलीकृत बुद्धि | stored knowledge and skills (Cattell) |
| cultural capital | सांस्कृतिक पूँजी | Familiarity with educated culture passed on by family (Bourdieu) |
| Cumulative Grade Point Average | संचयी ग्रेड पॉइंट औसत | Credit-weighted average over all semesters |
| cumulative record | संचयी अभिलेख | institution's year-by-year comprehensive record |
| Dalton Plan | डाल्टन योजना | Parkhurst's plan of individual monthly assignments |
| debate | वाद-विवाद | two sides argue for and against a motion |
| deductive method | निगमन विधि | rule first, applied to examples |
| democratic teaching | लोकतांत्रिक शिक्षण | respectful teaching that invites questions and participation |
| demonstration method | प्रदर्शन विधि | teacher shows step by step how something is done |
| dependent variable | आश्रित चर | the learner (in analysis of teaching); the measured outcome (in an experiment) |
| developmental tasks | विकासात्मक कार्य | tasks society expects at each life stage (Havighurst) |
| diagnostic and remedial | निदानात्मक एवं उपचारात्मक | finding difficulties and helping overcome them |
| diagnostic evaluation | निदानात्मक मूल्यांकन | finding causes of persistent difficulties |
| diagnostic function | निदानात्मक कार्य | finding entry behaviour and difficulties |
| diascope | डायस्कोप | Slide projector |
| differentiated instruction | विभेदित अनुदेशन | varying content, process and product to suit learners |
| difficulty index / facility value | कठिनाई सूचकांक | proportion answering an item correctly |
| digital divide | डिजिटल विभाजन | Unequal access to and familiarity with computers |
| direct assessment | प्रत्यक्ष आकलन | examines actual performance |
| direct grading | प्रत्यक्ष श्रेणीकरण | Grade given without marks |
| discovery learning | खोज अधिगम | learning by finding out oneself (Bruner) |
| discrimination index | विभेदन सूचकांक | upper-group minus lower-group proportion correct |
| distance education | दूरस्थ शिक्षा | education with teacher and learner mostly separated |
| distractor | विकर्षक | plausible wrong option |
| divergent production | अपसारी चिंतन | thinking that produces many answers; creativity (Guilford) |
| effective teaching | प्रभावी शिक्षण | teaching judged by the learning it produces |
| egocentrism | अहंकेंद्रिता | difficulty seeing another's point of view |
| elective course | वैकल्पिक पाठ्यक्रम | Course chosen from a pool |
| emerging adulthood | उभरती वयस्कता | Arnett's stage, about 18 to 25 |
| emotion | संवेग | a feeling such as fear, joy or anger that affects behaviour |
| emotional intelligence | संवेगात्मक बुद्धि | ability to recognise and manage one's own and others' emotions |
| enactive | क्रियात्मक | Bruner's mode: learning by doing |
| enrichment | संवर्धन | deeper and wider work at the same level |
| entry behaviour | प्रवेश व्यवहार | what the learner already knows and can do before instruction |
| environment | वातावरण | what a person experiences: family, culture, schooling, health |
| epidiascope | एपिडायस्कोप | Projects opaque material and slides |
| episcope | एपिस्कोप | Projects opaque material by reflected light |
| equilibration | संतुलनीकरण | balancing assimilation and accommodation |
| essay-type item | निबंधात्मक प्रश्न | item needing a long organised written answer |
| evaluation | मूल्यांकन | judging worth against objectives; includes measurement and assessment |
| evaluation triangle | मूल्यांकन त्रिभुज | objectives, learning experiences and evaluation as three linked corners |
| evaluative function | मूल्यांकनात्मक कार्य | checking whether objectives have been achieved |
| experiential learning | अनुभवात्मक अधिगम | learning from experience and reflection (Kolb) |
| external evaluation | बाह्य मूल्यांकन | by outside examiners or agency |
| extinction | विलोपन | fading of a response when not reinforced |
| extrinsic motivation | बाह्य अभिप्रेरणा | motivation from marks, praise, rewards |
| face validity | आभासी वैधता | surface appearance of relevance |
| facilitator | सुगमकर्ता | teacher who arranges conditions and guides learning |
| factors affecting teaching | शिक्षण को प्रभावित करने वाले कारक | Influences that make teaching more or less successful |
| feedback | प्रतिपुष्टि | information returned to the sender about the effect of a message or action |
| field-dependent | क्षेत्र-आश्रित | perceives globally, relies on outside cues |
| field-independent | क्षेत्र-स्वतंत्र | able to separate item from background |
| flannel board | फलालैन बोर्ड | Cloth board on which backed cut-outs stick |
| flash card | फ़्लैश कार्ड | Card shown briefly for drill |
| flipped classroom | फ़्लिप्ड कक्षा | content studied before class; class used for application |
| fluid intelligence | तरल बुद्धि | reasoning in new situations (Cattell) |
| focus (of a model) | केंद्र-बिंदु | central aim of a model |
| formative evaluation | रचनात्मक मूल्यांकन | frequent evaluation that guides learning |
| from concrete to abstract | मूर्त से अमूर्त की ओर | things learners can perceive before ideas and definitions |
| from empirical to rational | अनुभवजन्य से तर्कसंगत की ओर | observed fact before reasoned explanation |
| from indefinite to definite | अनिश्चित से निश्चित की ओर | vague ideas made clear and exact |
| from known to unknown | ज्ञात से अज्ञात की ओर | start from what learners know and move to the new |
| from particular to general | विशिष्ट से सामान्य की ओर | several cases before the rule; basis of induction |
| from psychological to logical | मनोवैज्ञानिक से तार्किक की ओर | learner's interests before subject structure |
| from simple to complex | सरल से जटिल की ओर | easy elements before difficult ones |
| from whole to part | पूर्ण से अंश की ओर | complete picture before details (Gestalt) |
| Gestalt | गेस्टाल्ट | whole, form; school of Wertheimer, Köhler, Koffka |
| gifted learner | प्रतिभाशाली शिक्षार्थी | learner far above age group in ability or performance |
| Golem effect | गोलेम प्रभाव | Low teacher expectations lower performance |
